  - **Why P2 rather than P1**: it is a duplicate toast for a fault that is genuinely being reported, not a missed fault or a pile-up — dedup bounds it at one row per signature. It becomes P1 the day a failing agent produces both a `critical` and a `warning` with different lifecycles, since `failures.py` owns a ladder and the log family owns a quiet-timeout
  - **Fixed 2026-08-17 (Session 65) by candidate (c), narrowed to one derived entry.** `COVERED_SIGNATURES` in `sysadmin/monitor/log_aggregator.py` maps `(source, signature)` to the family that owns the fault, and the one entry is keyed on constants the two producers already hold — `OWN_UNIT` from `core/unit_failure.py` and the new `AGENT_RUN_FAILED_EVENT` from `core/agent.py`, which replaces the string literal `BaseAgent.run` used to pass to `logger.exception`. Nothing is copied, so nothing can drift, which is `max_priority_for` against `PRIORITY_MAP` and `chk_alert_agent` against `AGENT_NAMES`
  - **Quietened to `info`, never dropped** — `known_noise` rule 2 for its reason. The row is still written, still counts occurrences, still reaches `GET /api/logs/trends` and still resolves on silence; `details['covered_by']` **names** the owning family rather than flagging that one exists, so a reader who finds a quiet row for a real fault is told where the loud row comes from. `noise_reason` and `covered_by` are separate keys deliberately: an operator's judgement that a fault is harmless and a structural fact that another family owns it are different claims, and one field holding both is `UnitFinding.enabled`'s trap
  - **Candidate (b) was refused on measurement, and the entry as filed understated the collision.** 713 `ERROR`/`CRITICAL` lines from this daemon resolve to **249 incidents** and 5 signatures. Of those, **34 carry no `agent_run_failed` at all** — `file_organiser_scan` ×27 and `retention_purge` ×7 — and `retention_purge` is not an agent, so no family covers it anywhere. Excluding `OWN_UNIT` from the alert half would delete the only witness those have. The entry also said "two rows": historically it was **four**, since 215 of 215 incidents fired `agent_run_failed`, `scheduler_job_error` and apscheduler's own `Job "…" raised an exception` in the same second. Post-Session-41 that co-occurrence cannot recur for an ordinary `_execute` failure, so the live collision genuinely is two — the entry was right by accident
  - **Quietening is safe because the case where `failures.py` is blind is the case where a different signature is still loud.** `failures.py` reads `agent_runs`, so it cannot see a failure `_record_outcome` failed to record — but `_record_outcome` is awaited *outside* `run()`'s `try`, so its failure propagates into APScheduler and raises `scheduler_job_error`, which stays at `warning`. Measured: all 215 historic `agent_run_failed` incidents carry `scheduler_job_error` in the same second, and `agent_runs` holds **zero** `failed` rows across 7,816 sysadmin runs — the two facts are the same fact. `SNAG-LOG-006` is the one path this does not cover
  - **The population is historic and the fix ships untriggered.** All 215 `agent_run_failed` fall on 2026-08-08 → 08-10, the `SNAG-DB-001` window; there have been none since Session 41 fixed `_record_outcome` and Session 43 added `schema_guard`. Also corrected: the entry quotes the title as `Log error: sysadmin-service — …`, but `log_entries.source` is the **unit**, so it is `sysadmin.service`
  - **Verified live, not only against fixtures.** The real historic `agent_run_failed` and `scheduler_job_error` lines were pulled from the journal, unwrapped by the real `unwrap_json_message`, and driven through the real `_execute` against the live database in a rolled-back transaction: `info` with `covered_by` for the first, `warning` for the second, occurrences counted on both, **0 rows of residue**. Six tests, each falsified deliberately — emptying `COVERED_SIGNATURES` breaks five, and re-keying the lookup on the signature alone breaks the sixth, which is the one asserting a negative and which an empty set can never break

- [P3] SNAG-LOG-006: **a manual agent run has no loud fallback if its own run record fails** (2026-08-17, opened by Session 65, **costed and FIXED 2026-08-28** by Session 112)
  - **What `SNAG-LOG-005`'s rule 5 rests on**: quietening `agent_run_failed` is safe because `_record_outcome` is awaited outside `run()`'s `try`, so a failure to *record* the failure propagates into APScheduler and raises `scheduler_job_error`, which stays loud. That holds for the nine scheduled jobs and is measured — 215 of 215 historic incidents carry both lines in the same second
  - **It does not hold for a manual run.** `POST /api/sysadmin/scan-all` and `POST /api/files/organise` start agents with `asyncio.create_task(agent.run(run_type="manual"))` and keep no reference to the task, so there is no scheduler listener and nothing awaits the coroutine. If `_record_outcome` fails there, `agent_run_failed` is quietened by design, `agent_runs` keeps a permanent `running` row, and nothing speaks
  - **Not fully dark, and the residual signal is unmeasured** — which is why this is filed rather than assumed away. asyncio logs `Task exception was never retrieved` at `ERROR` when the discarded task is garbage-collected, and that record propagates to the root JSON handler and so carries a level prefix and a distinct, uncovered signature. What is not established is *when*: GC timing is not deterministic, and no such line exists in this journal to measure against
  - **Population zero on this box.** `agent_runs` holds two `running` rows, both 2026-08-14, and no manual run has ever failed. The fault requires a manual trigger *and* a failure in the run-record write, which is the narrow window Session 41 already shrank
  - **Two candidate fixes, neither costed.** Retain the task references and attach a done-callback that logs a distinct event — which is the honest fix and touches two composition roots; or narrow `COVERED_SIGNATURES` to `run_type == "scheduled"`, which the log family cannot do, since `run_type` is in the envelope this daemon writes and not in the signature the exclusion keys on. The second would need the unwrap to promote a field into the identity, which is the coupling `SNAG-LOG-003`'s fix was chosen to avoid
  - **Costed 2026-08-28, and the ranking the entry implied is inverted.** `run()` swallows `_execute`'s exception, so the exceptions that escape it come from the bookkeeping around the work — **four shapes**, driven through the real `BaseAgent.run`: `_execute` *and* `_record_outcome` raising (journal holds `agent_run_failed`), `_record_outcome` alone (`agent_run_completed`), `_record_start` (**no line at all**) and `_flush_events` (`agent_run_completed`). The second candidate can only speak where an `agent_run_failed` line **exists**, which is **1 of the 4**; the other three write no line for a narrowed cover to un-quieten. It is also the *more* expensive of the two, not the cheaper — it needs `unwrap_json_message` to promote `run_type` out of the envelope, which that function's own docstring refuses, and gives `COVERED_SIGNATURES` a third key component `known_noise` does not share while `NOISE_SEVERITY`'s comment turns on the two answering one question. A quarter of the fault for strictly more work
  - **The entry names the wrong second trigger, and the check that watched it could not have said so.** `POST /api/files/organise` is a *synchronous* action route returning `FileActionResponse`; the discarded task was in `POST /api/files/scan` (`sysadmin/files/router.py:417`). `check_manual_run_unawaited` counted five discards across two *files* and never named a route, so it stayed `match` either side of the misdescription — a check measuring the right number about the wrong thing
  - **The residual signal is measured now, and *when* was never the problem.** The asyncio fallback fires at `ERROR` on the loop turn **after** the task completes — prompt, not GC-deferred; the loop drops its reference, CPython collects the task, and `Task.__del__` calls the exception handler synchronously. No `gc.collect()` is needed and none helps. **What** it emits is the defect: a **252-character signature** and a **220-character title** reading ``Task exception was never retrieved future: <Task finished name='Task-N' coro=<BaseAgent.run() done, defined at …/sysadmin/core/agent.py:N> …`` — `SNAG-LOG-003`'s shape arriving by the one route `unwrap_json_message` cannot help, since the *unwrapped* message is itself the repr. Three costs visible in that one string: it names `BaseAgent.run`, so all five triggers share one signature and the row cannot say which agent died; it carries the module path, so moving `run()` forks the row on a commit that changed nothing; and the exception's own text sits inside the repr, so on a checkout path shorter than this one it falls inside the cap and forks a row per distinct failure — `SNAG-AGENT-005`'s pile-up rebuilt inside the family built to end it
  - **Fixed by the honest candidate.** `sysadmin/core/agent.py`'s `spawn_manual_run` holds the reference and reports through `_report_manual_run`; all five triggers go through it. `MANUAL_RUN_FAILED_EVENT` is `manual_run_failed` — signature 17 characters, title 46, `exc_info` measured landing under its own envelope key so the traceback stays out of the identity and one query away in `raw_line`. Driven across all four shapes: every one now writes one `<3>` line and the healthy run writes none. **It reports to the journal and never to the database**, because the exceptions that reach it *are* database failures — `unit_failure.py`'s argument, which runs while the application is dead, one layer in
  - **Cancellation is recorded and not announced**, and the rung is the whole mechanism: `Task.exception()` raises on a cancelled task so it is tested first, and `MANUAL_RUN_CANCELLED_EVENT` is written at `warning`, which `FAULT_SEVERITIES` excludes — stored, counted, carried into `GET /api/logs/trends`, raising nothing. That tuple was extracted from an inline literal in `LogAggregatorAgent._execute` so the choice is derived rather than restated
  - **The check retires with the entry and the detector does not.** Every member of `CHECKS` names an *open* entry, so `manual_run_unawaited` is gone; the AST walk that found this lives on as `TestNoTriggerDiscardsItsTask` in `tests/test_manual_run_supervision.py` — `FROZEN_TABLES`' rule, since deleting a guard with its last finding takes the guard against the defect coming back. Ten mutations were driven against the twenty new tests and each lands red on the right one, including two the first draft got wrong

- [P3] SNAG-LOG-008: **ten of this daemon's own stored log rows are frozen as raw JSON, and no read will ever unwrap them** (2026-08-17, opened by Session 67, **fixed 2026-08-28** by Session 116)
  - **Symptom**: `GET /api/logs/trends` serves ten `sysadmin.service` signatures reading `{"timestamp": "N-N-N N:N:N,N", "level": "WARNING", "logger": …}` — the shape `SNAG-LOG-003` exists to remove, appearing in the endpoint that entry's fix was supposed to clean up
  - **Not a regression, and the fix is working.** `unwrap_json_message` applies at **read** time, in `read_journal`, so it can only ever shape rows ingested after the Session 64 declaration was deployed. Rows stored before it keep the raw envelope in `message` for ever, and `signature()` is computed from `message`
  - **Historic, measured rather than assumed**: all ten were ingested **14:12–14:22**, and the seventeen readable rows for that source begin at **19:50:19**, which is the restart that deployed the declaration. Nothing is creating new ones
  - **Found by reading a before/after, not by looking for it** — the purge's endpoint diff put the ten beside the readable ones in the same list, which is the only view in which the split is visible. `SNAG-LOG-004`'s ordering a third time
  - **Deliberately not fixed in the sitting that found it.** The remedy is a backfill — re-derive `message` from `raw_line` for rows where the source declares `format: json` — which is a *second* data migration, and Session 67 had already made one. Bundling them would put an unrehearsed `UPDATE` behind a rehearsed `DELETE` in the same transaction-free sitting
  - **The backfill is not free, either**: `raw_line` is truncated at 2000 characters, so a long record's `MESSAGE` may be unrecoverable from the stored row — the same truncation that hid the `__CURSOR` on the mosquitto group
  - **Measured 2026-08-26 by Session 90, and it is 10 of 10.** Every one of the ten parses out of its `raw_line` and unwraps: `api.auth_token is not set …` for the first and `alert_raised` for the other nine, `logger` recovered for all ten. Their stored lines run **1396–1651 characters**, so the cap was never near them
  - **The truncation the entry feared is real, present in this very source, and lands entirely on the other side.** `sysadmin.service` holds exactly **10 rows truncated at 2000** — and the intersection with the ten needing a backfill is **zero**. Those are the `SNAG-DB-005` lifespan tracebacks of 2026-08-22/23, whose `message` the declaration already unwrapped correctly, so they are the rows that need nothing and could recover nothing. The anti-correlation has a mechanism — truncation tracks record length, and the long records here are the 12.8 kB tracebacks Session 61's level prefix made readable *after* the declaration — but it is **a property of this ten-minute window rather than a law**: the pre-declaration window simply contained no traceback, and one arriving inside it would have been both unrecoverable and in need of the backfill
  - **`raw_line` is not a stable identity for a record, which a backfill keying on it would discover late.** `journalctl -o json` does not emit a record's fields in a fixed order, so the same record read twice yields two byte-different lines that parse to the identical dict — measured at **0 of 50** paired across two reads seconds apart, and it is what broke this check's first draft. It also decides whether `__CURSOR` falls inside the 2000-character cap, which is the mechanism behind the mosquitto case this entry already names
  - **Checked, and reproduced rather than counted** (`check:unwrap_is_read_time`). The endpoint's population empties on a schedule nobody chose: the ten left the current 7-day window on **2026-08-24**, leave `GET /api/logs/trends` altogether on **2026-08-31** when `previous_start` passes them — today they are served with `change: gone`, `current: 0`, `previous: 1` — and leave `log_entries` at 30 days' retention on **2026-09-16**. So the check drives the real `read_journal` over this daemon's own journal twice in one process, at the two declarations that bracket the Session 64 deploy, and pins that the unwrap has exactly one production call site and it is inside `read_journal` — the second half being the only one a backfill can move
  - **Cost is legibility only**, which is why this is P3: the identity is stable, dedup works, and `alert_title` truncates the same way for all ten, so they collapse rather than fork. It is `SNAG-LOG-003` as a residue rather than as a live defect
  - **Fixed 2026-08-28 by Session 116 with `sysadmin-backfill-messages`** (`sysadmin/monitor/message_backfill.py`), and the sitting was told to measure whether retention had already emptied the population before choosing. **It had not, and it was three days away.** All ten rows were intact, serving as 10 of the 24 `sysadmin.service` signatures on `GET /api/logs/trends` at `change: gone, current: 0, previous: 1` — exactly where the entry predicted they would be. They would have left that endpoint on 2026-08-31 as `previous_start` passed them, and `log_entries` on 2026-09-16. Live either side of the write: **60 → 50** signatures total, `sysadmin.service` **24 → 14**, raw-JSON **10 → 0**, and the ten collapse to **two** readable ones (`alert_raised` ×9, one `api.auth_token is not set …`) with `logger` recovered for all ten
  - **The entry's proposed derivation was the wrong one, and the cost it priced in is not on the path at all.** It asks for `message` to be re-derived *from* `raw_line` and files that column's 2000-character truncation as the reason a backfill "is not free". Read what `read_journal` composes: `message = message_text(MESSAGE)` **first**, then `unwrap_json_message` on that same string only where the source declares it. A `text`-declared row's stored `message` **is** the unwrap's input, so applying the unwrap to it reproduces the `json` read by construction, while going through `raw_line` re-implements `message_text(json.loads(line)["MESSAGE"])` — a second statement of the reader's own parse, free to drift from it. Driven over the live ten, the two derivations agree **10 of 10**, so the cheaper route is also the exact one
  - **`raw_line` earns a different job instead, and it is the one the entry never assigned it: the *witness*.** "Does this message look like JSON" cannot tell a frozen envelope from a correctly-unwrapped message that is itself a JSON document, and acting on the guess destroys the second. The exact test is byte equality against the record's own `MESSAGE`: a row nothing ever unwrapped still holds it verbatim, and an unwrapped row holds the fragment. So the truncation the entry feared is real and lands on the **witness** rather than on the derivation — the weaker half, because a row that cannot be witnessed is *refused* rather than corrupted, and reported rather than skipped. Re-measured today the anti-correlation Session 90 found still holds and has grown: **16** `sysadmin.service` rows now carry a `raw_line` cut at 2000, and the intersection with the ten needing repair is **zero**
  - **Idempotence is that same witness read again, not a flag.** A repaired row's `message` no longer equals the record's `MESSAGE`, so the second pass leaves it alone with nothing recording that it was ever repaired — a `backfilled` column would be a second statement of a fact the data already carries and one that can disagree with it
  - **A console script, not a data migration**, which inverts the obvious ranking. An Alembic revision would move the packaged head for no structural reason, so the box would then owe `alembic upgrade head` **plus a restart** or `schema_guard` refuses to boot — `SNAG-DB-005`'s twenty-three hours bought for ten rows — and it would repair this population once where the defect is a *class*: it recurs for every source whose declaration arrives after its rows do. It is a dry run unless `--confirm` (`files/actions.py`'s contract), it is never scheduled (a test pins that no job plan or agent reaches it, `check-migrations.sh`'s rule), and its three exit statuses keep "nothing frozen" apart from "could not measure" — `ports_checked`'s rule
  - **Applying it exposed something three sittings had not noticed, and it is not this fix's doing**: two of the ten have a **readable twin** in the table, same `logged_at` to the microsecond, ingested at **19:50:19** — the restart that deployed the declaration. The declaration was committed at **17:53:33** and `SNAG-LOG-007`'s boundary-close landed at **20:09:44**, *nineteen minutes after that restart*, so `_resume_floor` re-admitted its own inclusive second and both records at `14:21:03` were stored twice, the second time unwrapped. Before the backfill the twins looked like different signatures and hid each other; afterwards they are identical, which is why it took this sitting to see them. Filed as `SNAG-LOG-014` rather than deleted here
  - **The check retired with the entry** (`unwrap_is_read_time`, and the marker with it) — every member of `snag_claims.CHECKS` names an open one — **and the detector did not**: its half 1, the two-declaration drive against this daemon's own journal, is re-homed as `tests/test_message_backfill_live.py::TestTheShapeIsDecidedAtReadTime`, `FROZEN_TABLES`' rule. Re-homing it caught the entry's own warning being walked into a second time: the drive paired the two reads on `raw_line`, which `journalctl -o json` does not emit in a stable field order, so it silently compared nothing and **skipped**. It pairs on `__REALTIME_TIMESTAMP` now, and carries a premise test asserting the two reads shared a record at all

- [P2] SNAG-LOG-009: **every `journalctl` command this endpoint emits points one hour early, and in a western timezone it would point late enough to miss the incident entirely** (2026-08-18, opened by Session 68, **fixed 2026-08-24** by Session 71)
  - **Measured, not reasoned**: `sysadmin.log_entries.logged_at` is `timestamp with time zone` and holds the crash correctly as `2026-08-12 12:32:51.283926+01`. The trend serialises it as UTC (`11:32:51`), and `journal_command` formats *that* into `--since '2026-08-12 11:32'`. journalctl reads a bare datetime as **local**, so the emitted command starts an hour before the event
  - **It works here and that is the trap.** BST is UTC+1, so the error widens the read — running the emitted command returns 339,012 lines starting at local `11:32:02` and the incident is comfortably inside. The sign flips west of Greenwich: at UTC−5 the same arithmetic emits a `--since` **five hours after** the event and returns nothing, which is `SNAG-UNITS-003`'s trade failing on the wrong side — a command that misleads silently rather than one that fails loudly
  - **Population**: **9 of the 11** rows the endpoint now serves carry a `--since` command. It affects `_new_recommendation`, `_surge_recommendation` and the new `_incident_recommendation` identically, because all three format the same field
  - **Not fixed in Session 68 deliberately.** It is a one-line `astimezone()` on a field three functions share, and every existing assertion in `TestJournalCommand` pins the current rendering — so it is a change to what the tests say is correct, not a change underneath them. Putting that behind a correlation rule in one sitting is `SNAG-LOG-008`'s "an unrehearsed change behind a rehearsed one" argument, and the defect is latent on this box
  - **Found**: by running the command the new incident row emits, which is Session 27's rule (*"three of the emitted commands did not work, and only a live run said so"*) catching a fourth on its second outing
  - **Fixed by taking a `datetime`, not by converting one**, and the entry's own proposed remedy was the weaker of the two. It called the fix "one `astimezone()`" — which renders a **local wall clock**, correct on this box and still ambiguous: it is right only while the process writing the command and the human running it share a timezone, and an autumn-fold local time names two instants. `journal_command` now takes the `datetime` and the rendering belongs to `journal.since_timestamp`, which has emitted `@<epoch>` and said why since the module was written — so the defect was **the same fact implemented twice with one of them wrong**, which is what that function's own docstring and `journal_command`'s `-k` bullet are already about, met from a third direction. `@<epoch>` carries no zone at all, so it is unambiguous rather than merely correct here
  - **The three callers cannot re-derive it.** All three formatted `f"{first_seen:%Y-%m-%d %H:%M}"` independently, so fixing the string in one place is precisely the shape being removed; the parameter type is what makes a fourth caller impossible rather than merely unlikely
  - **Verified by running both commands, at two timezones.** The mosquitto specimen is stored `2026-08-22 18:10:16.115268+01`; the emitted `--since '@1787418616'` resolves to exactly that and the read opens on that line. On this box the old form lost **one** line (`Starting Mosquitto MQTT Broker daemon.` at 18:10:15) — the trap stated precisely, since BST makes the error benign and the box that would notice is the one that never runs the command. Re-run under `TZ=America/New_York` the epoch form is unmoved at **57,695 lines** and the wall-clock form returns **48,946**, opening at `17:10:00 -04:00` — **four hours past** the incident, which is absent from the output entirely
  - **The entry's "five hours" is right about the rule and not about August.** A UTC-rendered wall clock read at UTC−*N* opens *N* hours late, so it is five in EST and **four measured in EDT**; the incident is outside the window either way
  - **The population had moved**: the entry says 9 of 11 rows, and the endpoint served **9 of 9** on 2026-08-24, every one carrying a `--since`
  - **The entry was right that this changes what the tests call correct**, and that is what the new ones are for. `TestJournalCommand`'s assertions pinned the *rendering*, which is how a wrong command stayed green across three sittings; `TestTheWindowJournalctlOpens` models the **consumer** instead — `_journalctl_reads` resolves the emitted argument the way journalctl does, in London, New York and UTC, and asserts it lands on the event. All four were falsified against the behaviour they replace, the truncation-direction one needing its own falsification (`int` → `math.ceil`, which opens the window 1 s *after* the event)
  - **`since_timestamp` now refuses a naive datetime.** `datetime.timestamp()` reads one as local, which is the reading being removed, so accepting it would rebuild this defect inside its own fix with the right-looking type. Empty population by construction — every caller reads `logged_at`, a `timestamp with time zone` — and the guard exists to keep it that way
  - **The prose was labelled in the same sitting** so the fix does not leave a row disagreeing with itself: `detail`'s "First seen …" and the incident line's "within Ns of …" render the same UTC instant the command now points at, and now say `UTC`. Not converted to local: `@<epoch>` removes a timezone from the command, and putting one back into the prose beside it is the opposite direction — and it agrees with `GET /api/logs/trends`, which serialises `first_seen` with a `+00:00` offset

- [P3] SNAG-UNITS-006: **drop-in directories are invisible to the unit sweep, and one of the two on this box changes exactly the arithmetic `restart_bounded` performs** (2026-08-18, opened by Session 68)
  - **Symptom**: `discover_units` walks `*.service`/`*.timer` files and skips `*.service.d/` directories entirely, so any `[Unit]`/`[Service]` key a drop-in sets is unread. `/etc/systemd/system/mosquitto.service.d/override.conf` sets `StartLimitIntervalSec=180s`, `StartLimitBurst=30` and `RestartSec=5s` — the three values `restart_is_bounded` multiplies — precisely to make a restart loop survivable, and the sweep would compute the packaged unit's answer instead
  - **Empty population today, measured rather than assumed**: both drop-in directories on this box (`mosquitto.service.d`, `ollama.service.d`) belong to units the sweep does **not** see — they are distro-owned, so `discover_units` excludes them as symlinks/packaged before a drop-in could matter. **Zero** of the 38 units the sweep does see has a drop-in, and neither existing drop-in declares a relation at all
  - **It is two gaps sharing one cause**, and the newer one is the reason it is filed now: `declared_relations` (Session 68) reads the same files and inherits the same blind spot, so a `After=` added by drop-in would silently fail to correlate an incident. Silent is the operative word — the failure mode is a group that does not form, which looks exactly like two unrelated faults
  - **Deliberately not fixed here.** Reading drop-ins is a change to `discover_units`, which feeds the orphan/armed classification, the alert families and `GET /api/units/*` — a sweep-wide change made on the strength of a log-correlation session, with no live case to verify against
  - **Check**: <!--check:dropin_blind_spot--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when the sweep reads a drop-in — **reproduced, never counted**: the check builds a synthetic unit with an overriding `RestartSec=` and sweeps it, because this entry's population is measured empty and a population check would refute it on the day it was filed

- [P2] SNAG-LOG-007: **every restart re-read its own resume boundary, and a five-day-old coredump was announced as news three times** (2026-08-17, **found and fixed same day** by Session 66)
  - **Symptom**: `_resume_floor()` returns the newest `logged_at` already stored for a source and `_read_journal_source` opens the catch-up window there. `journalctl --since` is **inclusive** and `since_timestamp` renders `@<int>`, which truncates sub-second precision — so the window re-admitted the boundary entry *and every other entry sharing its whole second*, on every restart, for every source
  - **Measured live before the fix**: **339 duplicate groups covering 497 surplus rows** in `log_entries`, the worst a single journal entry stored **19 times**. Every duplicated entry is the newest stored one for its source — `sportsanalyser-frontend` 12:34:26, `estate-broker-provision` 12:32:51, `alfred-backend` 12:33:08, `kernel` 19:11:24 — which is the boundary the off-by-one predicts and a pattern no other cause explains
  - **The row count was never the cost.** `_resolve_quiet` closes a row unobserved for `alert_quiet_minutes`, so by the next restart the previous copy is resolved and the re-read entry raises a **fresh** alert. The mosquitto coredump of 2026-08-12 12:32:51 was raised three separate times — 12:34:15, 14:12:00 and 19:50:20 — each at `critical`, the one severity `sysadmin_tray/notifications.py` leaves on screen. **Every restart of this daemon announced a five-day-old fault that had been fixed five seconds after it happened**
  - **Found by measuring a claim, not by looking for it.** The sitting's task was to verify Session 63/64/65's four claims; the duplicate surfaced because the alert timestamps for one coredump were one-per-restart and nothing in the four claims predicted that. Same ordering as `SNAG-LOG-004` — the defect was under the verification, not in it
  - **The obvious fix creates a gap and was refused.** Opening at `floor + 1s` trades the duplicate for missed entries, and this module chose a cursor over a narrower window precisely because a gap is the worse failure for a monitor. So the window stays wide and the boundary is closed against the stored rows instead — `_is_unstored` keeps anything strictly newer than the floor, and at the floor's own microsecond keeps only messages not already stored
  - **The message is compared, not only the timestamp**, because two entries can share a microsecond: the Bluetooth pair on this box sits 29 µs apart, close enough to make "one timestamp is one entry" unsafe rather than untidy
  - **`STORED_MESSAGE_CHARS` exists so the truncation and the comparison cannot drift.** The row holds `message[:5000]`; comparing the untruncated line against it would make every long message unequal to itself and re-ingest on every restart — this defect rebuilt by its own fix. A test pins it
  - **Verified as a before/after on the same box.** The 19:49 restart re-ingested 26 entries reaching back to 2026-08-12; the 20:03 restart, with the fix, re-ingested **0** and created **0** new duplicate groups, taking only 6 genuinely-new rows all stamped after the restart
  - **What was left**: the 497 pre-existing surplus rows are historic data and were *not* purged by the fix — **purged 2026-08-17 by Session 67**, and the entry's own statement of the cost turned out to understate it
  - **The identity was proved before anything was deleted, and the obvious evidence pointed the wrong way.** `raw_line` differs in **all 339** groups, which reads as proof they are distinct entries; it is journalctl's JSON key ordering varying between reads. Settled against journald's own identity instead: **338 of 339 groups carry exactly one distinct `__CURSOR` and none carries more than one**. The 339th is this entry's own mosquitto coredump, whose `raw_line` is truncated at 2000 characters so the cursor fell off the end — its three `ingested_at` stamps are the three restarts, the same evidence by another route
  - **The purge key must be the fix's key.** `(source, logged_at, message)` is exactly what `_is_unstored` uses to decide an entry is already stored, so the surviving table holds no shape the running code refuses to re-create. Deduplicating on anything wider — `raw_line`, say — leaves behind precisely the rows the fix now declines to create
  - **The tie-break in the filed plan was wrong.** It said "keeping the earliest `id`"; `UUIDPrimaryKeyMixin` is `uuid.uuid4`, so ordering by `id` is arbitrary. The earliest `ingested_at` is kept instead — the first observation — because keeping a random copy falsifies when the service first saw the entry while leaving `logged_at` correct, a row that disagrees with itself
  - **A purge can re-open this defect by hand, which is the check nobody would think to write.** `_resume_floor()` reads `max(logged_at)` per source *and the message set at it*; deleting the last surviving row there moves the floor backwards and the next poll re-reads the window. Asserted 0 inside the transaction, alongside the row count, and both guards were falsified deliberately — each aborts, and the `DELETE` never executes in either falsified run
  - **Deleted 497 rows**: 626,976 → 626,479, duplicate groups 339 → **0**. Backed up first to `~/projects/.backups/sysadmin_assistant-data/` with the restore path **verified rather than claimed** — re-inserting the CSV inside a transaction gives back 626,976 rows and all 339 groups, then rolls back
  - **The cost was understated: four recommendations were fabricated, not inflated.** `GET /api/logs/actions` went **28 → 24** at unchanged `confidence: medium` with no new rows. Both `alfred-backend` surges read 21 vs 5 (ratio 4.2) against a genuine **4 vs 5**, and both `sportsanalyser-frontend` surges read 19 vs 6 (ratio 3.17) against a genuine **1 vs 3** — a **decline being reported as a surge**. Duplication inverted the direction, which "overstated by up to 19×" does not predict
  - **Worst surviving inflation**: `estate-broker-provision` 18 → 1, `kernel` "failed to reset" 17 → 1, `estate-manager-api` 23 → 11, `venture-assistant-backend` surge 48 → 27, the mosquitto coredump 3 → 1. The two `noise` rows moved 39,922 → 39,885 — tens of thousands of genuine occurrences against 37 duplicates, so the family this month's work unblocked was the least distorted of them
  - **The aggregator was confirmed alive before "no duplicates re-created" was written down**: 91 completed `log_aggregator` runs since the restart with 0 rows stored since 20:06:38, because a dead agent produces the same zero

- [P2] SNAG-LOG-003: **this daemon's own alerts will carry a JSON document where the message should be** (2026-08-17, opened by Session 61, **FIXED 2026-08-17 by Session 64**)
  - **Symptom, measured before it can happen**: with the priority half fixed, `sysadmin.service`'s errors finally reach `log_entries` — and `entry["message"]` is the whole formatted JSON line, because that is what journald's `MESSAGE` field holds for a service that logs structured JSON to stdout. `alert_title` therefore produces `Log error: sysadmin-service — {"timestamp": "N-N-N N:N:N,N", "level": "ERROR", "logger": "sysadmin.core.retention", "message": "retention_purge_failed", "service": …` at 252 of the 255 available characters, and `alert.message` reaches a notification body verbatim
  - **Detection is unaffected and that is why this is P2 rather than P1.** Driven through the real `read_journal` and the real `alert_title`: **two distinct faults produced two distinct titles**, so the signature discriminates and dedup works. `signature()` normalises the timestamp's digit runs to `N`, and the traceback's line numbers with them, so repeats of one fault collapse correctly. What is wrong is legibility, and only for this one source
  - **The obvious fix is the coupling this session's remedy was chosen to avoid.** Sniffing a leading `{` in `read_journal` and lifting out `message`/`level` puts a special case for one source into a reader serving fourteen, keyed on this application's log format — precisely the argument that sent the priority half to the producer instead
  - **The candidate that is not a coupling** is to make it a **declared property of the source** rather than a guess about it: `log: { type: journalctl, severity_filter: warning, format: json }` in `services.yaml`, with the unwrap applied only where declared. That is the shape `kind:` already has in the same file — the reader would be honouring a declaration, not recognising an application. Note it also gives `metadata['logger']` for free, which nothing currently has
  - **Deliberately not bundled into Session 61.** It is a second decision about a different module, and the fix it needs is a schema change to `services.yaml` rather than a line in a formatter
  - **Not observable until the restart**, since `log_entries` holds 0 rows for this source until `sudo systemctl restart sysadmin` runs — so the first real instance is also the first chance to check the title against a live notification
  - **Fixed 2026-08-17 (Session 64) by the candidate the entry named**, and the entry's reasoning held up at every step. `LogFormat = Literal["text", "json"]` on `sysadmin/core/config.py`'s `LogSource` and on `services.yaml`'s `LogRef`; `read_journal` takes `log_format` and applies `unwrap_json_message` only where declared. The reader honours a statement the source made about itself and knows nothing about this application
  - **Measured over the 723 real `ERROR` lines this daemon has written**, not against fixtures: the title goes from **6 distinct values of 242–253 characters of JSON to 5 of 46–151 readable characters**. The one merge is `sysadmin.core.scheduler` and `sysadmin.services.scheduler` both emitting `scheduler_job_error` (236 + 18) — one fault under a renamed module, which the old key forked only because the module path happened to fall inside the 252 characters that survived truncation. Putting `logger` back in the title would restore that fork by design, so it goes to `metadata['logger']` instead, which is the free field the entry predicted
  - **It fails open at every step, and that is not defensive habit.** systemd writes its **own** plain-text lines into a unit's journal at error level — `Failed to start SportsAnalyser - Frontend (Next.js).` appears **668 times** live and nine distinct such messages exist — so a declaration that discarded non-JSON would silence exactly the line saying the service died. A declaration describes what the *application* writes; it can never describe everything in the journal it writes to. Verified by reading `sportsanalyser-frontend` with `format: json` forced on: six real entries, all plain text, all preserved
  - **Severity is deliberately not taken from the envelope.** `"level": "ERROR"` sits beside the message and is ignored, because `JournalLevelPrefixFormatter` already puts it in `PRIORITY` — two statements of one fact that can disagree, which is what `max_priority_for` exists to avoid — and only the prefix is visible to `journalctl -p err` and `OnFailure=`. A test drives a record carrying `PRIORITY=4` and `level: ERROR` and requires `warning`
  - **The two declarations are pinned together rather than restated.** `service.log_format` in config.yaml decides what this process emits and `log.format` in services.yaml decides how the reader parses it; they sit in different files and nothing connected them, so a test asserts they agree, keyed on `OWN_UNIT` rather than on the historical `name`. A second test asserts no *other* source declares a format, which is the measured claim the fix rests on
  - **Verified live**, twice: the same read driven with `log_format` `text` then `json` against a transient unit gave a 249-character JSON title against `Log error: sysadmin-service — scheduler_job_error` at 49; and the real `LogAggregatorAgent._read_journal_source` against the live journal and database, rolled back, returned `alert_raised` with `metadata['logger'] = 'sysadmin.core.agent'`
  - **`raw_line` still holds the journalctl record verbatim**, so `exc_info` and every other envelope field stays one query away. The unwrap changes what the *identity* is built from, never what is retained
  - **The limit is stated rather than implied**: `agent_run_failed` is written by all five agents, so five different failures share one signature. Not a regression — the truncated JSON title cut at `"servic` and never reached the `agent` field either — and closing it means choosing which further envelope fields join the identity, which is recognising this application again
  - **The fixture was the thing that had to change, not the read.** Twelve tests built a `SimpleNamespace` stand-in for `LogSource` and broke on `source.format`; `getattr(source, "format", "text")` would have made them pass and swallowed a genuine wiring failure, so the fixture now constructs the real model — `UnitFinding.enabled`'s trap answered on the correct side

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-009: **the dev-server quietening is only as good as the sweep's age, and the window is six hours wide** (2026-08-17, opened by Session 57)
  - **Symptom**: `judge_audit_findings` quietens a ports breach to `info` when the stored sweep attributes the port to a session scope. The sweep runs six-hourly (`agents.service_discovery.scan_interval_hours: 6`) and the estate judge hourly, so a dev server started inside a sweep window has no attribution, reads as an ordinary unclaimed listener, and is raised at `warning` with a toast
  - **Measured, not assumed**: the two live rows were raised at 12:07 off a 06:07 sweep. They happened to be attributable because the editor had been open across the sweep; had VS Code been opened at 09:00 the same rows would have spoken loudly and stayed loud until the 12:07 sweep, then held (dedup suppresses the re-raise) — so the toast happens whatever the next sweep learns
  - **Two fixes considered and both rejected in Session 57.** Running `ss` inside the judge closes the window exactly and is refused in writing by `EstateJudgeAgent._attribution`: two calls at two moments give two answers to one question with neither surface saying which it used, which is the defect the cross-domain read exists to avoid. Raising the sweep to hourly is one config line and pays six times the sweep's cost across every consumer of `unit_ports` for the benefit of one annotation
  - **Its cost fell on 2026-08-28 and its mechanism did not** (Session 117, `SNAG-ESTATE-010`). A quieter judgement now reaches a row that is already open, so the loud rung this entry is about lasts **one sweep window** rather than the life of the listener. Measured, not reasoned: driven against the live database in a rolled-back transaction, a breach the stored sweep does not name is raised `warning` on run 1, and a second `_execute` after a sweep that *does* name it leaves **1 row, unresolved, at `info`**, with `alerts_raised=0` and `refreshed=1`. Nothing about the window moved — a dev server started inside it is still unattributed and still speaks loudly on the first run that sees it — so this is a re-ranking rather than a closure, and both refused closures (a second `ss` caller, an hourly sweep) still stand
  - **Why P3, re-ranked 2026-08-28 (Session 118) against the new cost — and the number holds for a different reason than the one it was given.** The bullet this replaces priced the failure at *one extra toast* and was wrong on the day it was written: `reminder_hours` had shipped two sittings earlier (Session 53, and again for the understudy in Session 55), and the only episode of this listener class this box has ever recorded stood **31.88 h** at `warning` throughout — 2026-08-16 12:07:11 → 2026-08-17 20:00:05, the pair this entry was filed from. So a mid-window start cost one toast **and every restatement due after it**, unbounded in the listener's life. What today's fix buys is a **ceiling, not a narrower window**: the loud rung now ends at the first judge run after the next sweep, which is `agents.service_discovery.scan_interval_hours` (6) + `agents.estate_judge.poll_interval_hours` (1) = **7 h worst case**, against a `reminder_hours` of 24. The repeat is therefore unreachable by **arithmetic** where it was previously survived by luck, and the old bullet's claim — one transient toast, and nothing after it — is true for the first time. The honest fix it named is unchanged and still ships: `details['holder']['observed_at']` says how old the evidence is
  - **Its ranking lever is guarded since 2026-08-28 (Session 119), and the reason the guard was offered as weak does not hold.** Session 118 left it unbuilt on the grounds that `TrayNotificationsConfig` parses `mute_services` alone, so a test *"can only read `notifications.desktop.reminder_hours`, the understudy's copy"*. That model is the **backend's slice**, not the file: `sysadmin_tray/config.py` reads `notifications.tray.reminder_hours` out of the same `config.yaml`, ships in this wheel, and is already imported across the seam by `tests/test_desktop_notifier.py`. Driven at a copy with the tray leaf set to 6, `load_tray_config` returns `6.0` while the understudy still reads `24.0` — so the guard asserts the leaf a repeat is actually due on. `tests/test_config_defaults.py::TestTheLoudRungEndsBeforeItIsRestated`, falsified at the lever (6 → 24 puts the sum at 25) and at the legitimate configurations it must not refuse
  - **P4 was considered and refused, on this file's only precedent for it.** `SNAG-LOG-014` is P4 because *"it is residue from a fixed entry, not a live defect"*; this mechanism is untouched and live. Driven today **after** the fix landed, `unswept_port_is_loud` still reads `match`: port 65008 is named by the stored sweep and judged `info` holding `transient: True`, port 65009 is not named and is judged `warning` with `holder=None` and an **identical detail key set** beside it, `alerts_raised=2`. A cost that fell is not a mechanism that closed, and moving the number would say it had. Both refused closures — a second `ss` caller, an hourly sweep — still stand, and the second is now refused twice over, since the ceiling below already delivers most of what raising the sweep would buy
  - **What would change the ranking** — two levers now, and the second is new, arithmetical, and unasserted. Still: **a second family learning to read `PortAttribution`**, and now *asymmetrically*, because the ceiling belongs to the judge's own quietening — a second consumer would inherit the stale window with no ceiling at all, so one consumer tolerating it is a stated limit while two is the copied-filter shape `snapshots.py` exists to prevent. And new: **the ceiling is a sum measured against `reminder_hours`, so anything lifting it past 24 h returns this entry to its pre-fix cost.** Raising `scan_interval_hours` to daily — the opposite of the refused hourly sweep, and the cheap move the day the six-hourly sweep is ever costed — puts the sum at 25 and the loud rung back inside a reminder. The margin is **3.4×** and **nothing asserts it**: the inequality's right-hand side is `notifications.tray.reminder_hours`, which `TrayNotificationsConfig` deliberately does not parse (it holds `mute_services` alone — `may_quieten_in_place` rule 2, one leaf over). A guard would have to read `notifications.desktop.reminder_hours`, the understudy's copy, which is the same 24 and is held to the tray's by a **comment** rather than by a test — so the assertion is writable, and only against the second statement of the fact
  - **The annotation this entry has claimed since Session 57 exists now, and it was refused as a fix for the rung — on correctness, where both standing closures were refused on cost** (2026-08-29, Session 128). `PortAttribution.reading()` and `details['attribution']`: `holder` is `None` for **four** reasons and the row now says which — `held`, `transient`, `unattributed` (the sweep looked straight at the port and could not name it), `unswept` (the sweep ran before this listener started, which is this entry), `unknown` (no usable sweep). Driven against the live sweep: `of(5432)` and `of(8110)` are both `None` and read `unattributed` and `unswept`. **The discriminator was already stored and no consumer read it** — `as_blob` has emitted `unattributed_ports` since Session 26c, and `attribution_from_blob`, written later for a different consumer, ignored it. This is `ports_checked`'s rule and the **sibling** of the collapse Session 57 fixed one field over in the same function: that sitting made a session scope distinguishable from an unattributable socket, and left an unattributable socket indistinguishable from a port nobody looked at. The entry's "Why P3" bullet has asserted since 2026-08-17 that `details['holder']['observed_at']` says how old the evidence is; the check's own docstring calls that **vacuous**, because `holder` is `None` on exactly the rows that need it. It is not vacuous now — every reading carries `observed_at`
  - **A third closure was considered and refused, and the reason is not cost.** Quietening an unattributed breach *because* the sweep predates it is the obvious narrow fix and it inverts a posture `EstateJudgeAgent._attribution` states in writing — *"the enrichment is not allowed to become a dependency of the alert"*. `observe_listeners` failing returns the error and **no** listeners, so every port would read unswept and the whole ports family would drop below `tray.notify_min_severity`: a breach detected, correct, machine-readable and never said out loud, which is this family's founding defect (Session 26b-A) rebuilt at full scale as the fix for a seven-hour window. Gating on the sweep's `ok` removes that failure and not the objection, because the default for an unknown port would still be *"probably a dev server"* — a guess `attribution_from_blob` already refuses in a milder form, where a port held by two units is **dropped** rather than attributed to whichever sorted first. So the rung is untouched and a test pins that it is
  - **Two of this entry's own measurements were wrong, and the box says so** (2026-08-29). **(a)** The "Measured, not assumed" bullet reads the 2026-08-16 12:07 pair as *"attributable because the editor had been open across the sweep"*. Both rows are `warning` with `holder` NULL in `alerts`, and they could not have been anything else: `transient_ports` first appears in a stored blob at **2026-08-17 10:07:41**, a day later, so all four historic `warning` rows are explained by a blob key that did not exist yet rather than by a sweep's age. **This entry has therefore never observed its own class** — the six rows all-time are four pre-Session-57 rows and the two `info` rows the quietening produced. **(b)** *"the window is six hours wide"* is the **p90**, not the window. Measured over **83 inter-sweep gaps in 14 days**: median **1.30 h**, p90 **6.00 h**, max 145.60 h (the 08-18 → 08-24 box outage, during which the judge was down too and raised nothing). The sweep is nominally six-hourly and runs **10–15 times a day**, because `schedules.agent_first_run_delay_seconds: 60` re-runs every added job on each daemon start and this daemon's median life is 1.77 h (`SNAG-TRAY-008`'s measurement). Both errors have one root: the mechanism was costed from `config.yaml` and the code path rather than from `unit_audits`, which is `verify-ops-claims-live` applied to a snag's own arithmetic. So the ceiling is **~2.3 h typical against 7 h worst**, and the margin against `reminder_hours` is **10× at the median** rather than the 3.4× the ranking bullet states
  - **Still open, and the check said so rather than the sitting deciding it.** The annotation removes the indistinguishability the check's rule 2 is keyed on and moves nothing about the mechanism: a port the sweep missed is still raised at `warning`, still toasts once, and both named closures still stand. Driven either side of the write — with the third limb still in the verdict the check answered `mismatch`, which is a fix reporting itself as a closure; with the limb removed it answers `match` and the detail line records that the rows are now told apart. A cost that fell is not a mechanism that closed, and neither is an annotation
  - **Check**: <!--check:unswept_port_is_loud--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when a port the stored sweep does not name is told apart from an ordinary unclaimed listener, by either of the two routes still open to a fix: the judge acquires an attribution the sweep never gave it, or an unattributed breach is quietened on other evidence. **It had a third limb and it came out on 2026-08-29, the day it fired** — "the row merely gains something naming the window" is the annotation above, so the limb is true from here on and can never again say anything about the window it was pointed at; leaving it in would make the check answer `mismatch` for ever on evidence that has stopped discriminating, which is `a-probe-keys-on-identity-not-a-mutable-field` for the second consecutive time (`SNAG-ESTATE-010`'s `reached` hit the same shape on 2026-08-28). Narrow the clause, keep the observation in the note: `annotated` and `revalued` still run and reach the `detail`, and the test that pinned the limb was **inverted rather than deleted**. **The limb also had to be widened before it could be removed honestly**: it compared detail *key sets*, so it caught a key added to the unswept row alone and was blind to the same key added to **every** row with a varying value — which is the shape the fix took, because a key present only sometimes is `ports_checked`'s collapse one level down. Measured at the baseline before a line of the fix existed (`both rows carry the same detail keys: True`), so the check would have reported `match` over a landed fix, which is worse than flipping. `_detail_shape` compares values with each row's **own** port rendered opaque rather than by naming `port`/`fingerprint`/`audit_summary`, the three spellings of one number. **The eighteenth check, and the second that writes to the live database.** The population is a *timing accident* — whether a listener is attributed depends on which side of a six-hour boundary somebody opened an editor — so counting rows measures when a window was opened rather than whether the window exists, which is rule 1 refused for the fifth time and the fourth consecutive entry to run into it. The two rows this entry and `SNAG-ESTATE-010` were both filed from are the same two, and they have since resolved. The mechanism is drivable because `EstateJudgeAgent._attribution` reads **one** stored `unit_audits` row and argues in writing for reading no other: **a sweep that names one of two ports is a sweep taken before the second listener started**. So the probe stores exactly that and lets the production path do the rest — the same `httpx.MockTransport`, mounted judge and rolled-back transaction Session 95 built, sharing `findings_transport`, `mounted_judge` and `rolled_back_drive` with it, and differing only in the blob and in the fact that **nothing stands open**, which keeps the dedup branch that entry measures out of this path entirely. **The witness runs the other way round from its sibling's**: there an unmoved row needed a moved row beside it, here a *loud* row needs a *quiet* one, because a family whose quietening had been reverted raises the unswept port loudly for a reason that has nothing to do with the window and looks identical. Driven as the falsification — with `_attribution` returning an empty `PortAttribution` every assertion the `match` branch makes about the unswept port is still true, and the verdict is `unknown`. **And the assertion is wider than the rung**, because the entry's own phrasing is *reads as an ordinary unclaimed listener*, which is a claim about indistinguishability: the third fix shape moves neither the rung nor the holder, and it is detected by comparing the two rows' `details` keys **against each other** rather than against a field name written down here. Live on the first drive, 2026-08-27: port 65008 is named by the sweep and raised at `info` holding `transient: True`; port 65009 is not, `_attribution` returns `None` for it, and it is raised at `warning` with `holder=None` and an identical key set — `alerts_raised=2`. The quiet rung is derived from an attribution the drive never uses, so the expectation and the measurement cannot be one object read twice

- [P3] SNAG-RELOAD-001: **after a reload, the config object can hold a scheduler setting the running scheduler does not obey, and nothing says so twice** (2026-08-15, **fixed same day**)
  - **Symptom**: `sysadmin/reload.py` installs the whole `AppConfig`, so `get_config().agents.sysadmin.health_check_interval_seconds` reads the new value while the APScheduler job keeps the trigger built at startup. Measured live this sitting: edited to 999, the config object returned 999 and the job stayed on 300 s
  - **Before the reload existed this divergence could not occur** — startup built the config object and the scheduler from one read, so the two always agreed. It is a cost the reload introduces, not a pre-existing fault it exposes
  - **It is reported, once.** `ReloadReport.requires_restart` names every such leaf in the response body and in a `WARNING` log line. That is the whole mitigation, and it is the shape Session 39 spent itself removing: a warning that fires once is indistinguishable from one that got fixed. An operator who reloads and walks away has nothing that still says the scheduler is not obeying config.yaml
  - **Two candidate fixes, neither costed.** Store the last `ReloadReport` and serve it (cheap; but a field nobody polls is the `SNAG-CFG-001` shape), or raise it as an alert row so it reaches the tray, the DND windows and `/api/sysadmin/alerts` through plumbing that exists. The second is the right shape and needs a settled dedup and resolve lifecycle before it is written — a family added without one is how 51,924 rows happened
  - **The real fix is upstream of both**: `Scheduler` exposes no `reschedule_job`, so nothing re-times a job. Adding it would shrink `RESTART_ONLY` to the socket, the engine and the logging setup and make this snag mostly disappear, rather than reporting it better
  - **Found**: 2026-08-15 by running the mixed edit against the live config.yaml — one live field and two restart-only ones in one file — which is the case the apply-and-name decision was taken for
  - **Fixed 2026-08-15 (Session 50), by the third option.** Neither candidate mitigation was built: storing the report and raising an alert row both describe the divergence, and the entry's own last line says the divergence can be removed instead. `sysadmin/core/jobs.py` holds the plan (`plan_jobs`: an `AppConfig` -> the jobs it asks for) and `apply_jobs` reconciles a scheduler with it; `Scheduler` gained `sync_interval`/`sync_cron`/`remove_job`. The lifespan and the reload now call **the same function**, so the schedule at startup and the schedule after a reload cannot be produced differently. `RESTART_ONLY` went from **fifteen leaves to two prefixes** — `service` and `database`, covering the socket, the logging setup and the engine
  - **The obvious implementation was wrong, and in the direction that matters.** Re-applying every job on every reload is one line shorter and breaks the schedule: `reschedule_job` recomputes the next fire from *now*, so a 24-hour job would sit permanently 24 hours from the most recent reload — which on a box being poked at is never. That is `agent_first_run_delay_seconds`'s failure (SNAG-AGENT-003) with a reload standing in for a restart. A job whose trigger has not moved is therefore left **untouched**, decided by comparing against the **live** trigger rather than a remembered plan: a remembered plan is a second statement about what the scheduler is doing, which is what this entry was
  - **`jobs_synced` is the field that keeps the report honest.** `jobs_retimed: []` means "nothing needed re-timing" when it is true and "the scheduler was never looked at" when it is false — `UnitScanResponse.ports_checked`'s rule, one domain over. Without a syncer the reload falls back to Session 49's behaviour exactly, reporting every job leaf that moved, and that fallback reads its path list from the plan (`JOB_CONFIG_PATHS`) rather than restating it
  - **Verified live** on 2026-08-15 against the real `config.yaml` and a real `BackgroundScheduler`, in-process and touching neither the daemon nor the file: 999 s became `interval[0:16:39]`, `retention_purge` moved 03:00 -> 04:00, a disabled `estate_judge` had its job **removed** and re-enabling **added it back with the first-run delay**, and `requires_restart` came back `[]` where Session 49 reported three leaves. Reloading the same file twice re-timed nothing
  - **What it does not fix**: `RESTART_ONLY`'s two survivors are genuinely immutable in-process. A port or a database URL change still needs `sudo systemctl restart`, and the report still says so
- [P0] SNAG-DB-001: the live database was a migration behind, and it caused a **39-hour monitoring blackout** nobody saw (2026-08-10, fixed same day)
  - **Symptom**: **Zero rows were written to `service_health` between 2026-08-08 17:34:54 and 2026-08-10 09:07:25.** Not degraded — absent. `/api/sysadmin/status`, the tray grid and reliability scoring were all serving from a table that had stopped receiving data a day and a half earlier
  - **Cause, in three parts, each individually survivable**:
    1. `alembic current` reported **008** while the repository head was 009. Migration 009 — which adds `'skipped'` to `chk_health_status` — was written and committed on 2026-08-08 (Session 35 Phase 3) and never applied. **Nothing applies migrations**: no script, no `ExecStartPre`, no CI step. It is a manual `uv run alembic upgrade head` a human has to remember
    2. `sysadmin.service` restarted at 17:36:31 on 2026-08-08, two minutes after the last successful check, and picked up the new `services.yaml`. `venture-chat-large` is declared `kind: static` with `monitor: false`, which records `skipped` — the exact value the un-applied migration was meant to permit
    3. **One rejected row killed all nineteen.** `SysAdminAgent._execute` `session.add`s every service's result into one session and `BaseAgent.run` commits once, so the `CheckViolationError` on `venture-chat-large` aborted the whole transaction. A single deliberately-unmonitored service took the entire health check down with it
  - **Why nothing noticed**: the daemon logged `agent_run_failed` on every run — **29 times today alone**, once every five minutes from 06:37 to 09:02 — and nothing reads those. The alerting path is the thing that broke, so it could not report its own failure. `verify_connection` checks that the database answers, not that it is the schema this code was written for. The schema drift guard connects to the live database and would not have caught it either: it explicitly skips `alembic_version`, and `compare_metadata` does not diff CHECK constraints — verified 2026-08-10 by restoring the pre-009 constraint inside a rolled-back transaction and getting an empty diff, with the model declaring `'skipped'` and the database rejecting it
  - **Impact**: ~39.5 hours wall clock, of which roughly **18 hours the daemon was up and failing every five minutes** (the box was asleep for the rest). No health history, no alerts, no reliability data. `GET /api/services/reliability` computes live from `service_health`, so it scored the estate off a table with a day-and-a-half hole in it
  - **Fixed**: `uv run alembic upgrade head` on 2026-08-10 applied 009, 010 and 011. Checks resumed at 09:07:25, first `skipped` row written successfully. **This was found by accident** — while tracing why the drift guard had stayed green, not by anything designed to catch it
  - **The detection gap was the real defect, and is closed as of 2026-08-13 (Session 43)**, all three parts: (1) `sysadmin/core/schema_guard.py` compares `alembic_version` against the packaged head at startup and **refuses to boot** on a mismatch, so the unit enters `failed` and `sysadmin-failed.service` announces it — the Session 39 machinery's second caller; (2) one `session.begin_nested()` per service in `SysAdminAgent._execute`, with a rejected row recorded as `status="error"` rather than costing the run, so a blackout becomes one bad tile; (3) `sysadmin/monitor/failures.py` raises `{agent} agent failing` off consecutive `agent_runs` failures, on the shared ladder. Note **(3) was not implementable when this entry was written** — `_record_outcome` wrote `status='failed'` into the transaction the failure had already destroyed, so there were no failure rows to read; Session 41's three-transaction split created them. The fourth item on the list, a live-database test path for the shared snapshot query, is still open. See [tasks.md](tasks.md)

- [P2] SNAG-PROJ-013: `ImbaBots` heads its handoff with no ISO date, so the Stop hook will block its next code session (2026-08-11, **fixed** on ImbaBots by 2026-08-24, **closed here 2026-08-25** by Session 82)
  - **Filed first with the wrong diagnosis, corrected the same day.** The original entry read "commits without ever moving its handoff date — either the hook is not firing there or that work is not done through Claude Code sessions", and both alternatives are false. Recorded rather than quietly rewritten, because the mistake was to reason from an endpoint's output instead of opening the repository
  - **What is actually true**: ImbaBots' last session is `edbd8c2` (2026-08-07 12:03), which changed 22 `game/` files **and** `docs/handoff.md` in one commit — the habit was kept. `HANDOFF.md`'s mtime is still that timestamp because nothing has run there since. The only later commit, `7144fc5` on 2026-08-10, is the estate migration promoting `docs/handoff.md` to the root, run from *this* repository. ImbaBots' first scan in the momentum window falls after 12:03 on 2026-08-07, so that date is its **baseline** — a state, not a transition — and `sessions: 0` is the correct reading, not a measurement failure
  - **The residual defect is smaller and elsewhere**: the first heading is `# Handoff — M5 (Tier 2) · ⚠ …` with no `YYYY-MM-DD`. `~/.claude/hooks/require-handoff.sh` greps the first heading for today's date, so **the next code-changing session in ImbaBots will be blocked until someone adds one** — the hook cannot be satisfied by the document as it stands. Secondarily, an undated heading means `handoff_date_source` is permanently `mtime` there, so every session ImbaBots ever contributes reads `unverified`
  - **Fix, and why it must not be applied by hand**: an ISO date in the first heading resolves both halves — but it has to be written by the session that earns it. `require-handoff.sh` fires on *any* dirty tree or *any* commit that day, not only code, and demands **today's** date; so editing ImbaBots on a day nobody worked there forces a heading dated to a session that did not happen. On the next scan that is a handoff-date transition, i.e. **a phantom session in `GET /api/projects/momentum`** — and since `docs(…)` is not in `code_commit_ignore`, the doc commit itself would set `last_commit_at` to that day and the phantom would read as *landed*. The `unverified` machinery exists to stop mtime inventing a session; back-writing a date does the same thing deliberately. Considered and rejected 2026-08-11, along with adding `docs(handoff)` to `code_commit_ignore` to soften it — that changes staleness measurement estate-wide to accommodate one edit, and the row is still a phantom
  - **Therefore: no action here, and none in ImbaBots until it is next worked on.** The hook will stop that session, on a real date, and its block message already states that the first heading must carry today's date — the mechanism working, not a trap. Close this entry when a dated ImbaBots handoff appears. **Not** a change to this codebase either: the `commits_without_sessions` field considered in the original entry would have widened the contract to describe a defect that does not exist
  - **CLOSED 2026-08-25 (Session 82), measured rather than assumed.** `~/projects/ImbaBots/HANDOFF.md` heads `# Handoff — 2026-08-24 · M5 (Tier 2) …`, so the Stop hook's ISO-date requirement is met and has been for at least a day. Fixed by the owning repository, which is the correct party under the estate rules — this entry was never this repository's to fix, only to stop publishing

- [P1] SNAG-AGENT-003: the file organiser has run **once in its life**, and its own stall alert has been open for a day (2026-08-11, **fixed 2026-08-12** by Session 41, **closed 2026-08-25** by Session 82)
  - **Symptom**: `sysadmin.agent_runs` holds exactly **one** `file_organiser` row — `2026-08-06 12:05:41`, `completed`, 29.63s — against `log_aggregator` 31,431, `sysadmin` 6,052, `service_discovery` 26 and `project_organiser` 100. Every other agent ran on 2026-08-11; this one last ran five days earlier. `agents.file_organiser.enabled: true`, `scan_interval_hours: 24`. A `file_organiser agent stalled` alert has been open since 2026-08-10 08:07 and was **not acted on**, which is the second half of the defect
  - **Found**: by Session 36's `facts.stale_sources`, which flagged `filesystem` as measured 2026-08-06 — reached from the data side, independently of the alert that had already been raised from the agent side. Two routes to one fact, and neither had been read
  - **Cause**: unconfirmed, deliberately not chased in Session 36. The candidate is the interaction the config file already documents at `config.yaml:109` — *"this is why file_organiser, at 24h, had zero recorded runs"* — where an APScheduler interval job counts from process start, so an agent whose interval is longer than the gap between daemon restarts never reaches its own schedule. `agent_first_run_delay_seconds: 60` was added to work round exactly that, and the daemon restarted at 2026-08-11 11:21 UTC without producing a run, so **either the first-run delay is not firing for this agent or the run is failing before it records**. Both need checking against the live scheduler; the single recorded run is `completed`, so nothing has ever recorded a failure
  - **Impact**: `GET /api/files/*` has served five-day-old figures throughout — `reclaimable_mb: 34849`, `stale_project_dirs: 102` — with nothing on those endpoints saying so, and the weekly disk review narrates from the same stale audit. Same shape as SNAG-DB-001: a monitoring component silently stopped, and the estate reported the last thing it knew as though it were current
  - **Fix**: establish which of the two candidates it is before changing anything — a scheduler that never fires and an agent that dies silently need opposite fixes. Then make the difference visible: `GET /api/sysadmin/self` already reads `agent_runs` and the stall alert already fired, so the missing piece is that **nothing forces the alert to be seen**, not that nothing detected it
  - **Second half addressed 2026-08-11 (Session 39), first half still open.** The escalation ladder now re-raises a stall as `critical` once the `warning` has stood `escalate_after_hours` unresolved, and `critical` is the only severity the tray renders as a notification that does not expire — so this alert would have become persistent on 2026-08-11 09:07 rather than remaining a single toast from the day before. That fixes *being told*. **It does not fix the file organiser**, which has still run once in its life, and the two candidate causes above are still unseparated. Note what the ladder changes about the evidence: from now on a stall of this kind leaves two rows and a `details.first_alerted_at`, so the next occurrence records how long it stood rather than only that it happened
  - **Cause established 2026-08-12 (Session 41), and it is neither candidate.** Separating them took one `journalctl` call. The scheduler fires correctly and the agent *succeeds*; the transaction is killed underneath it. At the last daemon start the job was registered (`scheduled_interval_job file_organiser_scan {"hours": 24}` at 17:08:12), ran on `agent_first_run_delay_seconds` as designed, and logged `agent_run_completed duration_s 117.71 findings 25317` at 17:11:10 — followed immediately by `InterfaceError: connection is closed` on `UPDATE sysadmin.agent_runs SET status='completed'`. `SHOW idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` → **`1min`**. `BaseAgent.run` inserted the `running` row and **flushed** it, opening a transaction, then handed the *same* session to `_execute`, which spent 118 seconds in `asyncio.to_thread` touching no database. PostgreSQL terminated the backend at t+60s and every write of the run rolled back — the audit, and the run record itself
  - **This is a `BaseAgent` defect that only the slowest agent is slow enough to hit.** sysadmin 13 s, project_organiser 2.3 s, service_discovery 0.2 s, log_aggregator 0.3 s; file organiser 118 s. And **the one surviving run is the evidence, not the exception** — 2026-08-06 took `29.63s`, the only run in the agent's life to finish inside the timeout. The rule was already written down one layer up, in `files/review.py`: *"commit the read transaction before calling the LLM — this host sets `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout=1min` and inference takes longer."* Learned for the LLM, never generalised to the framework beneath it
  - **"Nothing has ever recorded a failure" was not reassurance, it was the second symptom.** The `except` branch sets `status='failed'` on a row living in the killed transaction, so the failure record dies with the run it records. An agent failing this way is indistinguishable from one that was never scheduled — which is what `GET /api/sysadmin/self` reported for five days, correctly, off a table that was being emptied
  - **Fixed 2026-08-12 (Session 41)**: `BaseAgent.run` now runs in **three** transactions — the `running` row commits alone, `_execute` gets a session that has never been flushed (so its transaction opens at its first statement, not 118 seconds earlier), and the outcome is an `UPDATE` by id from a third. Free, because `UUIDPrimaryKeyMixin` sets `default=uuid.uuid4` client-side. Rejected: raising the timeout on scheduler connections — one line, but it suppresses the host's deliberate guard on the connection that idles longest and still leaves a failed run unable to record itself. **Proven on the box the same day**: after the restart the file organiser recorded **3 completed runs plus one `running`**, against one run in the agent's entire life before today, and `filesystem_audits` went from a single 2026-08-06 row to four. The `running` row is the documented new behaviour — a run in flight is now visible rather than absent
  - **CLOSED 2026-08-25 (Session 82), measured against `agent_runs` rather than read off the body.** **45 runs**, latest `2026-08-25 16:22:03`, against the title's "once in its life". The stall alert is gone too: **2** unresolved rows in the whole `alerts` table and neither is this family. The fix landed on 2026-08-12 and **the body has said so ever since** — what kept the entry open for thirteen days is that the closure was written in a body bullet and never in the title parenthetical `read_snags` reads, which is `SNAG-ROADMAP-002`'s residue rather than a second defect

- [P1] SNAG-AGENT-004: **26,270 alert rows for four services that no longer exist can never be resolved or purged** (2026-08-11, **fixed 2026-08-12** by Session 41, **closed 2026-08-25** by Session 82)
  - **Symptom**: unresolved alerts, counted live — `personal-assistant` **9,748**, `personal-assistant-frontend` **9,748**, `redis` **4,950** (newest 2026-03-07), `ollama` **1,824**. None of the four appears in `config.yaml` or `services.yaml`; PA and ollama were retired 2026-07-24. `GET /api/sysadmin/alerts` and the tray badge carry them, and three `Critical disk usage on /` rows from July sit alongside them although the disk has been at 68 % since August
  - **Cause, two halves that only bite together**. `SysAdminAgent._handle_status` calls `resolve_alerts(session, service_name)` **inside the per-service check loop** ([agent.py:391](../../sysadmin/monitor/agent.py#L391)), so recovery is only ever observed for a service still being checked. A service removed from configuration is never checked again, so it can never be seen to recover. And `run_retention` purges alerts `WHERE ... AND resolved = TRUE` ([retention.py:85](../../sysadmin/core/retention.py#L85)) — deliberately, so an open incident is never deleted from under its reader. An unresolved row for a deleted entity therefore lives for ever
  - **This is a defect the project side already fixed, on the service side.** `ProjectOrganiserAgent._resolve_recovered` exists precisely because "a project deleted from disk never appears in a scan, so it can never be observed *recovering*, and a per-project loop leaves its alert unresolved forever" — that is how 1,664 rows accumulated. The monitor has the same shape at **16× the scale**, and the fix pattern is already in the codebase: ask the inverse question set-based (which open service alerts would this run *not* raise), rather than looping over the survivors
  - **Found**: by checking live state at the end of Session 39 while confirming the escalation ladder had fired. Nothing surfaced it — which is the point: these rows are indistinguishable from live incidents on every surface that shows them
  - **Impact**: worse than table size. A permanently-open `critical` trains the reader to dismiss the alerts list, which is the exact behaviour Session 39 spent a day trying to earn back. It also poisons any future "is anything wrong right now" check built on `resolved = false`
  - **Fix**: a set-based resolve for service alerts, mirroring `_resolve_recovered` — and a one-off backfill for the four retired services, which is a data change and needs an explicit call. Note the retention rule must **not** be relaxed to purge unresolved rows: that would hide the symptom and delete live incidents with it
  - **Scope was understated by about twenty times, found on re-counting 2026-08-12.** Retired services are **five**, not four — `nuxt-frontend` (224 rows) was missed — and `redis` is 6,283 rather than 4,950, so the service half is **27,827**. Beside it sit **24,097** resource-threshold rows with **no resolve path at any point in this application's life**: `Critical disk usage on /` is **13,971 open rows**, last raised 2026-07-26, against a disk that has been at 68 % since; `High disk usage on /` another 9,530. `_check_thresholds` raises unconditionally and nothing has ever resolved what it raises. A condition that recovered and could not be observed recovering is the *same* defect as a service that was retired, so it is closed by the same statement rather than filed separately
  - **Fixed 2026-08-12 (Session 41)**: `SysAdminAgent._resolve_recovered` asks the inverse question over `RESOLVABLE_TITLE_PATTERNS` — **patterns, not the configured service list**, because a set built from configuration cannot contain a deconfigured service, which is the entire defect. The per-service `resolve_alerts(session, service_name)` is gone, and with it a second unfiled bug: it was a substring `ilike`, so `venture-chat` recovering also closed `venture-chat-large`'s alerts. Three families are excluded because each has a lifecycle owner already — `% agent stalled` (`stalls.py` escalates *off* the quiet row staying open), `% failed` (`unit_failure.py` writes it dead, the lifespan resolves it alive), `Unusual % usage` (resolved by id). Verified against the live table: **51,924 rows in the population, and the only `agent='sysadmin'` row left open is the file organiser's stall**, which is still true
