  - **The obvious implementation was written first and refuted before it shipped.** Excluding only "titles this run raised" is the direct reading of the project-side pattern and is wrong here: `_degraded_counts` is in memory and resets on daemon restart, so the first run after one raises nothing for a service degraded for hours — the alert would be resolved as *recovered* and re-raised two checks later, announcing a recovery to the tray for a fault that never went away. Services are resolved only when this run measured them healthy; resource thresholds keep the raised/not-raised test, because a mount either breached or did not and no streak is involved. Two rules in one statement, and the docstring says which half is which
  - **Backfill ran itself, and the numbers landed**: **51,976 rows resolved** on the first runs after the restart, taking `agent='sysadmin'` unresolved from 51,925 to **43**. No script was written — chosen so the fix would be proven by doing exactly what it will do for ever after
  - **CLOSED 2026-08-25 (Session 82), measured across the whole table.** **2** unresolved rows against the 26,270 this entry filed — and the two named families are drained rather than merely aged out: `redis unreachable` holds **2,017** rows of which **0** are open, `Critical disk usage on /` holds **13,968** of which **0** are open. So `_resolve_recovered`'s pattern-based sweep reached rows whose subject no longer exists, which is the exact property the entry said no per-service loop could have. Same thirteen-day lag as `SNAG-AGENT-003` and the same cause — a closure the title never carried

- [P1] SNAG-ESTATE-001: **two retired PersonalAssistant units have restart-looped 52,178 times since the reorganisation, and hung the kernel once** (2026-08-12, units removed by the owner, **closed 2026-08-25** by Session 82)
  - **Symptom, live at the time of writing**: `personal-assistant-idle-watcher.service` **NRestarts=34,517** and `personal-assistant-worker.service` **NRestarts=17,661**, both `enabled`, both in `activating (auto-restart)`, both climbing at roughly 1.3 restarts per minute — the counters rose by 9 and 5 during a seven-minute investigation. PA was retired 2026-07-24 and its repository moved to `~/projects/archive/`; the unit files still name `/home/gaddi/projects/PersonalAssistant/scripts/run-worker.sh`, so every start fails `203/EXEC` and systemd immediately schedules another. **6,812 failure/restart journal entries today alone** for the worker
  - **Started 2026-08-04 21:13:18**, the first `Unable to locate executable` in the journal — the `~/projects` reorganisation (Phase 1, same day). Nothing was checking that moving a repository leaves no enabled unit pointing at the old path, and 8 days later nothing had noticed
  - **It has already cost something.** On **2026-08-08 04:10–04:14** the kernel logged RCU stalls followed by hung-task warnings: `INFO: task (idle-watcher.):658024 is blocked on a mutex likely owned by task (run-worker.sh):658025`, both blocked **>245 seconds**, alongside two wedged kworkers (`kworker/u128:0`, `kworker/11:1`) and `rcu_preempt detected expedited stalls`. A restart storm from two dead units deadlocked against each other and stalled RCU on a 32-thread box. That is 13 hours before `SNAG-DB-001`'s monitoring blackout began (17:34 the same day) — **no causal link is claimed**, and the shared date is recorded only so nobody assumes one later
  - **The detector found this perfectly and nobody read it.** `GET /api/units/status`'s latest sweep classifies both as `category: "orphaned"`, names the `dead_path`, resolves where the project went (`project_path: /home/gaddi/projects/archive/PersonalAssistant`) and states the cause in plain English: *"WorkingDirectory /home/gaddi/projects/PersonalAssistant does not exist — systemd fails the start job, so this unit cannot run"*. There is an open alert, `Unmonitored systemd units: 17 findings`, raised 2026-08-11. **The diagnosis was complete, correct, machine-readable and eight days old.** This is the estate pattern in its purest form to date — better evidence than any of the six instances estate-manager ADR-0001 cites — because here the measurement was not merely ignored, it was *articulate*
  - **`activating (auto-restart)` is exactly the invisible state Session 39 fixed on one unit and one unit only.** That session established that `Restart=always` without `StartLimitBurst` means a crash loop never reaches `failed`, so no `OnFailure=` can fire and `systemctl is-failed` says nothing is wrong. It fixed `sysadmin.service`. These two units are the same defect, on the same box, running the whole time — and the estate has **no rule** requiring the pairing of anything else
  - **Stopped 2026-08-12** on the owner's instruction: `systemctl --user disable --now personal-assistant-worker.service personal-assistant-idle-watcher.service`. Both went `inactive (dead)` / `disabled` with `NRestarts` reset to 0, and **no unit on the box is in `activating` any longer**. **All five `personal-assistant-*.service` files were then removed** from `~/.config/systemd/user/` (backend, frontend, idle-watcher, tauri, worker) and the user daemon reloaded: `systemctl --user list-unit-files` now matches nothing, with 0 activating and 0 failed, and every other user service still running. Copies are in the session scratchpad rather than the repository — they are the owner's systemd state, not this project's. Reading all five before deleting was worth it: **every one is `Restart=always` or `on-failure` with no `StartLimitBurst`**, so the invisible-loop shape Session 39 fixed on `sysadmin.service` was present five times over on this box, and the two that happened to have a dead `ExecStart` are simply the two that got the chance to demonstrate it
  - **The durable half was two things. The second is fixed (2026-08-14); the first is still open.** What remains is the retirement checklist that includes `systemctl disable` — a process, not code, and not this repository's to enforce
  - **"Make an orphan finding speak" — fixed 2026-08-14.** The sweep now measures whether an orphan is **armed** (systemd will start it) and the agent gives each armed one its own alert row naming the unit and scope, beside the roll-up rather than instead of it. `GET /api/units/*` stays GET-only and advice-only; what changed is that the dangerous subset no longer waits to be fetched
  - **The obvious rule for "will it loop" was wrong, and the live units are what proved it.** "`Restart=` with no `StartLimitBurst=`" reads as sound and is wrong in both directions. `personalassistant-backend.service` declares no start limit, so systemd's defaults apply (`DefaultStartLimitIntervalSec=10s`, `DefaultStartLimitBurst=5`) — a limit **does** exist. It also sets `RestartSec=10`, which puts starts ten seconds apart, so five can never fit inside the ten-second window: the limiter is unreachable and the unit restarted 34,517 times without once entering `failed`. In the other direction, a bare `Restart=always` restarts every 100ms by default, five starts fit easily, and the loop *is* terminal — the naive rule would have raised a critical on most of this box's healthy services on its first run. The real test is arithmetic: `RestartSec × (StartLimitBurst − 1) < StartLimitIntervalSec`. It is the same arithmetic Session 39 did by hand for `sysadmin.service` (`StartLimitIntervalSec=600` against `RestartSec=10` → 40s < 600s), which is why that fix works, and `alfred-backend.service` passes it too on a hand-written `StartLimitIntervalSec=60`
  - **Both signals are pure**, so `sysadmin/units/scan.py` keeps its no-subprocess promise: "enabled" is an enablement symlink under a `*.wants/`/`*.requires/` directory the sweep already walks (matched on link *name*, so a dangling link left by an `rm` without a `disable` still counts), and the restart arithmetic is four keys of text it already parses. Cross-checked against `systemctl is-enabled` on every unit on this box on 2026-08-14: they agreed
  - **Live on this box the day it landed: 1 armed orphan of 6.** `garmin-sync.service` (user, enabled, `WorkingDirectory` gone) → one `warning` row. The four `Restart=always` orphans are harmless *only because someone disabled them*, so they stay in the roll-up as debt with the shape recorded on the finding. Verified against the live database in a rolled-back transaction: 5 runs of one fault wrote **2** rows — raise, dedup, escalate `warning`→`critical` when the loop appears, hold, resolve once on clearing — with the roll-up untouched and 0 residue
  - **What is deliberately not fixed here** is the general case: 15 of the 18 units on this box with a `Restart=` policy cannot reach `failed`, including every live service except `sysadmin`, `alfred-backend` and `alfred-frontend`. Filed as `SNAG-UNITS-002` rather than alerted on, because 15 criticals is not "speaking". **Fixed 2026-08-15** and the judgement held: it shipped as ranked advice under `GET /api/units/actions` with no alert row of its own, and the population had grown to 17 of 20 by then
  - **CLOSED 2026-08-25 (Session 82), measured by `find` over both unit directories.** **0** files matching `personalassistant*` under `/etc/systemd/system` or `~/.config/systemd/user`, so neither unit exists to be armed, let alone to loop. Removed by the owner as part of `SNAG-UNITS-005`'s five system-scope orphans, which `SNAG-ESTATE-008` recorded as done on 2026-08-16 — **nine days before this entry stopped being published as a live P1**, and in a document that was itself the evidence

- [P1] SNAG-AGENT-005: **598,091 log-aggregator alert rows can never be resolved, because they are events wearing a state's clothing** (2026-08-12, **fixed 2026-08-12**)
  - **Fixed** in Session 42, as a *raise* rule. The alert is now keyed on a **normalised fault signature** rather than on the source, so one row is raised per distinct fault and repeats bump `details['occurrences']` instead of inserting. Measured against the live storm the same afternoon: **2,000 kernel error lines in ten minutes → 2 alert rows**, and the titles finally say what broke (`Log error: kernel — Bluetooth: hciN: Failed to load firmware file (-N)`) where before every one of them read `Log error: kernel`. The 598,091 existing rows were resolved as `superseded` in one statement, taking the whole table's unresolved count to **2**
  - **Why the key is the signature and not the source, which is the part worth keeping.** Deduplicating on the existing title was the obvious version and it is wrong: `Log error: kernel` is shared by every kernel error whatever it says, so the Bluetooth storm would have held the single open row while the RCU stall and the USB enumeration failure — **both in the same live 30-day window** — went unannounced. Trading half a million rows for a mask over every other kernel fault is not a fix. `sysadmin/monitor/log_signature.py` strips digit runs and hex literals, which collapses 594,779 Bluetooth lines to 2 signatures and 110 USB lines to 2, while leaving all six distinct faults distinguishable. Normalisation loses `error -110` versus `error -71`; nothing is actually lost, because the alert's `message` column carries the last verbatim line and `occurrences` carries the count that used to be expressed as row volume
  - **Resolution is time-based, and it had to be.** `_resolve_quiet` closes a row whose signature has not been logged for `alert_quiet_minutes` (15, i.e. 15 polls). The `SNAG-AGENT-004` inversion — "which open alerts would this run not raise?" — is meaningless for an event, since the answer is "all of them, one run later". Anything the run observed is excluded by **exact title** as well as by age, which cannot race the clock that stamped it; `COALESCE(details->>'last_seen_at', created_at)` is the fallback, without which every row raised before this change would have been permanently unresolvable — the defect preserved by its own fix
  - **The obvious query would have OOMed on the backlog.** `_open_alerts` first read every unresolved row this agent owns, which on the live table is 593,814 ORM objects on the first run. It is now bounded by the titles the run is about to raise — single digits — which is the more natural query as well as the survivable one
  - **The double-ingest is fixed and the mechanism is a cursor, not a narrower window.** `read_journal` resumes from `__CURSOR`, so overlap is decided by journalctl rather than by a clock; narrowing `since` instead would have traded the duplicate for a gap whenever a run ran long, which is the worse failure for a monitor. Proven live: two back-to-back runs ingested **96 entries then 0**, where the old code re-read the whole window every poll. The cursor advances over **every** entry read, not only those surviving the severity filter — advancing only past kept entries leaves the resume point behind a run of info-level noise and rebuilds the defect one layer down. It is in memory, so a restart falls back to `_resume_floor`: the newest `logged_at` already stored for that source, passed as `--since @<epoch>` because journalctl reads a bare datetime as **local** time and everything here is UTC
  - **The `-n 500` cap is still there and is now reported.** A ceiling is unavoidable at 8.5 kernel messages a second; what was missing is that hitting it was invisible — `findings_count` sat at exactly 200 on every run and nobody read it as a symptom. `details['truncated_sources']` names the sources at their ceiling rather than counting them, because which one it is decides whether it matters. `-n` returns the *newest* entries, so a truncated read stays current and drops the middle, which is the right trade for a monitor and is now stated rather than inferred
  - **What this does not fix, deliberately**: the host fault. `BT_RAM_CODE_MT6639_2_1_hdr.bin` is still absent, the kernel retry loop is still running at ~8.5/s, and `rfkill list` now prints nothing at all — the adapter no longer registers a soft-block switch, so even the reversible stop recorded below is gone. The point of this change is that none of that matters to the alerts table any more: the storm costs 2 rows
  - **Symptom**: unresolved alerts under `agent='log_aggregator'`, counted live — `Log error: kernel` **547,814** (2026-03-24 → 2026-08-10), `Log critical: ollama.service` 29 for a service retired 2026-07-24, `Log error: alfred-backend.service` 19, plus five smaller titles. That is **91 % of every unresolved alert in the table**, and `GET /api/sysadmin/alerts` and the tray badge carry all of them
  - **Cause, and why it is not the one Session 41 just fixed**: `SNAG-AGENT-004` was a *state* alert with no observer of its recovery, and the fix was to ask which open alerts a run would not raise. That question is meaningless here. A log line that was written cannot un-write itself, so there is no run at which "this would not be raised" becomes true, and widening `_resolve_recovered` to cover these titles would resolve half a million rows on the first pass and then resolve every new one on the run after it raised it — an alert type with a lifetime of five minutes by construction
  - **The real defect is upstream of resolution**: `log_aggregator` raises **one alert row per matching log entry**. 547,814 rows for one recurring kernel message is not a backlog, it is a design that mistook a log for an incident. The same mistake the monitor made with `service_health` — *"that table records one row per failed check — 123 rows for one internet outage"* — recorded in `GET /api/services/reliability`'s docstring as the reason incidents are derived from transitions rather than counted from alerts
  - **Found**: 2026-08-12 while counting the population for `SNAG-AGENT-004`'s fix. Deliberately left out of that change: one mechanism resolving two different kinds of thing is how a row gets closed while still being true, which is the rule the three exclusions in `RESOLVABLE_TITLE_PATTERNS` exist to hold
  - **The volume is genuine, which was checked rather than assumed.** The obvious guess is that a poller with no cursor re-reads its own window; measured instead, `log_entries` holds 546,325 kernel events at **one** copy and 232 at two, so the rows are distinct events and not re-reads. **546,579 of the 547,814 (99.8 %) are two Bluetooth messages** — `Failed to load firmware file (-2)` and `Failed to set up firmware (-2)`, ENOENT — emitted in a retry loop **every ~0.58 s** across four days only (2026-07-15, -24, -27, -30; 175,000 rows on the worst). A missing firmware file produced half a million alert rows, and the alerting design turned a hardware annoyance into 91 % of the table
  - **The underlying host fault, run down 2026-08-12 and *not* what it looks like.** The working hypothesis was "the motherboard's wireless is unsupported by Linux". Half right, and the half that is wrong changes the fix. `modinfo btmtk` on kernel 7.1.6 declares `firmware: mediatek/mt7927/BT_RAM_CODE_MT6639_2_1_hdr.bin` — **the driver knows this exact chip and names the exact file**. `/lib/firmware/mediatek/mt7927/` (owned by `linux-firmware-mediatek 20260622-1`) contains only `WIFI_RAM_CODE_MT6639_2_1.bin.zst` and `WIFI_MT6639_PATCH_MCU_2_1_hdr.bin.zst`. The BT blob is on no path on this box, while **every sibling chip has one** (MT7902, MT7922, MT7961, MT7925). So it is a **firmware packaging gap, not a driver gap** — waiting on a newer kernel achieves nothing; the file is what is missing. *(Amended 2026-08-12 — see below: it is not a packaging gap either. The file does not exist upstream.)* The card is a MediaTek MT7927 (Filogic 380) at `09:00.0`, and the Wi-Fi half is the mirror image: firmware present, **no driver bound at all** (`lspci -k` prints no `Kernel driver in use`), which is the genuinely-unsupported part
  - **It came back the same afternoon, and disabling `bluetooth.service` did not stop it.** Measured 15:52 on 2026-08-12 with that service `inactive`: **816 Bluetooth kernel messages in two minutes (~6.8/s)**, twice the July rate, 81,422 this boot, the adapter having re-enumerated at 12:32:45. `bluetooth.service` is `bluetoothd`, **userspace**; the retry loop is `btusb`/`btmtk` in the **kernel**, which probes the adapter whether or not anything in userspace wants it — so the obvious lever is the wrong one. The kernel also now states the diagnosis itself: `Direct firmware load for mediatek/mt7927/BT_RAM_CODE_MT6639_2_1_hdr.bin failed with error -2`, character-for-character the file `modinfo btmtk` declares. **This promotes the entry from P2 to blocking**: the `SNAG-AGENT-004` fix does not touch these rows, so restarting the daemon while the storm runs refills the table Session 41 emptied, at 175,000–580,000 rows a day. `rfkill block bluetooth` is the reversible stop (`hci0` reads `Soft blocked: no`, so it is untried) and the firmware blob is the real fix *(both claims superseded — see the amendment below: `rfkill list` now prints nothing at all, and there is no blob to install)* — but both treat the symptom, and any recurring kernel error reproduces this until the raise rule changes
  - **Was dormant, which is why it read as harmless.** The last Bluetooth message of any kind is **2026-07-30 22:53**; there have been five boots since and `/sys/class/bluetooth/` does not exist, so the device is not enumerating at present. But `bluetooth.service` is still `enabled`, nothing was changed to stop it, and if the adapter reappears the loop resumes at ~3.4 lines/second — **~175,000 alert rows per day**. Guaranteeing it cannot recur means disabling the service (and optionally blacklisting `btusb`/`btmtk`); making it *work* means dropping `BT_RAM_CODE_MT6639_2_1_hdr.bin` from upstream linux-firmware into `/lib/firmware/mediatek/mt7927/`. **Neither is this repository's to decide**, and neither removes the defect above: one alert row per log line turns any recurring kernel error into a table like this one
  - **Amendment, 2026-08-12 (after the fix landed): the recorded host fix does not exist.** The entry above concluded that the blob was a packaging gap and that installing `BT_RAM_CODE_MT6639_2_1_hdr.bin` from upstream linux-firmware was the real fix. It is not available. Checked against the upstream tree and its manifest rather than inferred:

    ```
    git.kernel.org  linux-firmware  /mediatek/mt7927/
      WIFI_MT6639_PATCH_MCU_2_1_hdr.bin
      WIFI_RAM_CODE_MT6639_2_1.bin
    ```

    `WHENCE` declares those two files for the MT7927 and nothing else. **MediaTek has not published the Bluetooth firmware at all**, so this is a vendor *publication* gap, not a distribution one — `linux-firmware-mediatek 20260622-1` is current with upstream and `pacman -Ql` confirms it ships only the two WIFI blobs. The kernel driver was merged declaring a file its vendor has not released. Nothing to install, no newer package to wait for, and no way to date a fix
  - **Why the original reasoning failed, which is the part worth keeping.** "The driver names the exact file, every sibling chip has one, therefore the file is merely missing here" is sound and wrong. A `modinfo` firmware line is a **request**, not evidence of existence; sibling chips having blobs shows what the *packager* would ship if it existed and says nothing about whether the vendor published it. The one place that settles it — the upstream tree — was never checked, and checking it took one `curl`
  - **What actually stops it, and the cost is genuinely nil.** De-authorise the device so the USB core never configures it and nothing probes for firmware. It is at `0489:e13a` (Foxconn, the MT7927's Bluetooth half), on port `1-11`:

    ```
    # /etc/udev/rules.d/99-mt7927-bt-no-firmware.rules
    ACTION=="add", SUBSYSTEM=="usb", ATTR{idVendor}=="0489", \
      ATTR{idProduct}=="e13a", ATTR{authorized}="0"
    ```

    Bluetooth does not work either way, so nothing is lost; undoing it is one `rm` and a reboot. Blacklisting `btusb`/`btmtk` is the blunter fallback if the port still logs enumeration failures, and it costs any future USB Bluetooth dongle. **Not applied** — `sudo` on this box wants a password and the change belongs to the owner
  - **The two USB faults are one device.** `usb 1-11: device descriptor read/64, error -110` and `usb usb1-port11: unable to enumerate USB device` are port `1-11`, which is exactly where `0489:e13a` sits: the same adapter failing at an earlier stage of the same reset loop, not a second fault. The RCU stall used to argue for signature-keyed dedup is unrelated, so that argument stands, but two of the six "distinct faults" in the table above share a root cause
  - **What the storm still costs after the fix.** The alert table is bounded now — 2 rows, not 43,000 a day — but the lines are still ingested: `log_entries` holds **610,941 rows / 622 MB**, taking on **64,047 today**, against 30-day retention; journald is at **4 GB** with **165,670** kernel lines today. Not urgent, not free, and the reason the udev rule is still worth installing
  - **Recheck whether the blob has appeared** (there is no other trigger — nothing on this box watches for it):

    ```
    curl -s https://git.kernel.org/pub/scm/linux/kernel/git/firmware/\
    linux-firmware.git/tree/mediatek/mt7927 | grep -o 'BT_RAM_CODE[^"<]*'
    ```
  - **A second, smaller defect measured on the way**: `read_journal` is called with `since="2m ago"` on a **60-second** poll, so every unit-journal event is ingested **exactly twice** — confirmed at 2 copies for all 18 `venture-assistant-backend` and all 15 `sportsanalyser-frontend` events. Kernel does not show it and the reason is not established, so it is recorded as an open question rather than explained. Small in rows, but it doubles every count anything downstream computes from `log_entries`
  - **Fix, and the decision it is blocked on**: not a resolve rule but a *raise* rule. Options, none chosen: alert on a **burst** (n occurrences in a window) and resolve when the source goes quiet, which turns this into a genuine state; or drop alerting from the log aggregator entirely and let `log_entries` be the record, with `GET /api/logs/stats` the surface. The second is smaller and probably right — the tray has never been the place anyone reads kernel messages. Either way the existing rows are a data change needing an explicit call, and retention will not touch them while they are unresolved

- [P2] SNAG-ESTATE-002: **the estate's nudge title and message never reach the wire, so this repository built its own** (2026-08-13)
  - **Symptom**: `estate_service/projects/nudges.py` carries a comment reading "The one place a nudge's title is built" over `nudge_title`, and `Nudge.title` / `Nudge.message` are `@property`. `GET /api/projects/attention` serialises with `dataclasses.asdict`, which emits **fields only** — so neither property is in the payload. The producer's single source of truth for a nudge's wording is unreachable from the consumer that renders it
  - **Consequence, as landed**: `sysadmin/estate/judgements.py` builds `Project <name> next action idle` and the message text itself, from the published fields (`days`, `threshold`, `at_window_edge`, `next_action`). That is a second implementation of a format the producer believes it owns — the copy-drift the estate manager exists to remove, in the one place both repositories have a comment claiming otherwise
  - **Why it was accepted rather than fixed**: the title is the identity key in **this** repository's `alerts` table — dedup, the resolve and the tray's `{severity}:{title}` fingerprint all read it — so it is arguably ours to own regardless. What is not defensible is that both sides think they own it and neither says so
  - **The fix is the estate's, not ours**, per estate-manager ADR-0002: a cross-repo requirement is recorded there and executed there. Either publish `title` and `message` as fields (cheapest: convert the two properties, or add them in `oversight.attention` beside the `asdict` call), or delete the properties and the "one place" comment so the producer stops claiming a role it does not fill. Deciding which is theirs
  - **Handed over 2026-08-14**, recorded in estate-manager as its **`SNAG-ESTATE-010`** (snag_list.md) with a backlog task pointing at it. **The IDs do not correspond and must not be quoted interchangeably**: that repository already has a `SNAG-ESTATE-002` of its own — an estate rule cited by name and written nowhere, fixed 2026-08-11 — because the scheme is per-repository. Nothing was changed in its code, which is the whole point of the delegation
  - **The drift is not prospective; it has already happened.** `Nudge.message` puts the action through `_shorten` at `_MESSAGE_ACTION_CHARS = 120`, and `sysadmin/estate/judgements.py` interpolates `next_action` in full. The two messages differ **today**, and neither side can observe the difference — the producer's copy is never serialised and the consumer has never seen a populated payload. This upgrades the entry from a duplication that might drift to one that already has
  - **The strongest evidence for the delete option was not available when this was filed**: `nudge_title`, `NUDGE_TITLE_LIKE` and `NUDGE_TITLE_SUFFIX` have **no production caller anywhere in estate-manager** — the only reference outside their own module is one test. The comment claims "Raise, escalate and resolve all derive from it", and none of that machinery is in that repository at all; it stayed here at the Session 4 move. `TestTitleHasOneSource` pins a consistency with no consumer. So the producer is not merely failing to publish a format it owns, it is maintaining a format nothing on its side uses. The comment even justifies itself by citing `_alert_title` in `estate_service.projects.agent` — **a symbol that does not exist in that module**, having stayed here at the move, so the reasoning behind the claim left before the claim did
  - **A third property is dropped too** — `Nudge.details`, unmentioned when this was filed. Any fix that converts `title` and `message` and stops there leaves the same defect one field over
  - **Found**: 2026-08-13 by Session 45, by reading the producer rather than trusting its dataclass. Nothing catches it: `asdict` dropping a property is silent, and the estate's own suite asserts only `payload["nudges"] == []`
  - **The related gap this leaves here** is that `judge_attention` has never been exercised against a real populated payload — `/api/projects/attention` has answered `{"health": [], "nudges": []}` every time anyone has looked, on both sides of the seam. Its rules are pinned against literals built from the producer's dataclass fields, which is the strongest evidence available and is not the same as a capture
  - **Checked again 2026-08-14 07:30 UTC and still empty**, this time *after* an overnight `run_type: scheduled` scan (03:31 UTC, 26 projects, 0 parse failures) rather than the single manual scan that was all the estate had before. That was the precondition that made the check worth waiting for, so the negative result is worth more than the earlier ones: the payload is not empty merely because the producer had never really run
  - **The related gap is closed on this side (2026-08-16, Session 52); the cross-repo half stays delegated and open.** A populated payload cannot be captured from the wire — checked a fourth time, still `{"health": [], "nudges": []}` — so one was made from the producer's own code, driven read-only in its own venv against the live estate database with `effective_threshold` forced to 101 and `nudges.evaluate(default_days=0)`. Everything else is the estate's: 26 real snapshots, 26 real manifests, 5 real streaks, `dataclasses.asdict` over the real `Nudge`. It is committed as `tests/fixtures/estate_projects_attention.json`, and the seam is now guarded in `tests/test_estate_project_contracts.py` beside the two 8400 routes that already were — recorded half asserting the keys the consumer reads, live half asserting the envelope with the per-entry assertions **pre-staged** to start running the day the estate publishes anything
  - **Two defects the literals could not express**, both fixed: the family raised **31 rows in one poll** (26 breaches + 5 nudges, one tray fingerprint each) where the ports family has had `port_breach_max_rows` since Session 26b-A — now `attention_max_rows`, per family; and the message ran to **469 characters** into a notification body, now cut with `truncate_at_word` at 120 with the full text kept in `details`. Neither is the drift this entry is about, and the second **narrows** it: this repository now shortens the action with its own marked helper, so the two messages differ by wording and by a marker rather than by one side silently keeping 120 characters and the other 469
  - **A test now fires when the estate fixes its half.** `PRODUCER_DROPPED_NUDGE_KEYS` asserts `title`, `message` and `details` are *absent* from a published nudge, with a failure message naming the next move — take the producer's wording and delete the assertion. That is the pre-staged-trigger shape rather than a task parked on another repository
  - **That pre-staged test cannot fire, which is what the check below is for (measured 2026-08-26, Session 87).** `PRODUCER_DROPPED_NUDGE_KEYS` is asserted *inside* `for nudge in payload["nudges"]`, so it runs once per nudge and never on an empty list. The **live** half calls `_assert_attention_usable(..., require_populated=False)` against a route that has answered `{"health": [], "nudges": []}` on every occasion anyone has looked, so the loop body has never executed; the **recorded** half passes `require_populated=True` against `tests/fixtures/estate_projects_attention.json`, a static file captured 2026-08-16 that cannot change when the producer does. Neither half can witness the fix. The pre-staged-trigger shape was right and its trigger was wired to the entry's *population* rather than to its *mechanism* — this document's own rule 1, met from the inside
  - **Check**: <!--check:nudge_wording_unpublished--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when the wording the producer computes reaches the wire, when it stops being computed at all, or when the two part company field by field. **The eleventh check, and the first written against an entry this registry's own convention said not to check**: the note under `SNAG-ESTATE-014` gives "delegated… so a check would be a cross-repo read of a thing that repository is already fixing" as the reason four `SNAG-ESTATE-*` entries and `SNAG-LOG-012` carry none — and `SNAG-ROADMAP-001`, also delegated, was closed on exactly such a read one sitting after the check that noticed. Being delegated is what makes a check *worth* writing, because the closing move happens in a tree nothing here watches
  - **The mechanism is asked and the population deliberately is not.** A `Nudge` is built rather than looked for, as `check_dropin_blind_spot` builds the drop-in its entry could not find — and its arguments come from `dataclasses.fields(Nudge)` rather than from a field list held here, because converting the properties into fields changes the constructor's signature and a probe carrying its own copy of it would raise `TypeError` and report `unknown` for ever. That is the removed check's own defect — a probe coupled to the implementation it measures — avoided by having been paid for once
  - **All three remedies this entry offers are reachable, including the delete.** Publishing the properties as fields shows up in `asdict`; deleting them shows up as the wording being *absent from the object*, which is a refutation rather than a failure to measure, because the complaint is that both sides think they own the format and a producer that has stopped claiming it has answered that; augmenting the payload beside the `asdict` call cannot be seen in `asdict` at all, so the route's serialisation is read too and a route that no longer hands its list out of a bare `asdict()` is `unknown`. A **partial** fix is `mismatch` with the residue named, because this entry's own body says converting two and stopping there leaves the defect one field over
  - **One observation for the estate, not raised as a defect here**: `IdleNudgeConfig.days` has no lower bound (its per-project sibling `idle_nudge_days` is validated `>= 1`), so a global `days: 0` yields nudges reading "unchanged for 0 days (nudges after 0)" — the `standing_days: 0.0` artefact Session 26b-A found one surface over. Visible in the committed fixture because the capture forced exactly that; this repository renders the producer's numbers faithfully and does not launder them

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-003: **the estate-invariant alert families are raised once and then stay silent, with no escalation** (2026-08-13, **fixed 2026-08-16**)
  - **Symptom**: `EstateJudgeAgent` raises at `warning`, deduplicates on an open row, and never escalates. A scan that has been stale for six hours and a scan that has been stale for six days produce the same single `warning` row, spoken once
  - **This is exactly the failure Session 39 was written to fix**, one family over. That session's argument — *"a warning that fires once is indistinguishable from one that got fixed"* — applies unchanged here, and `sysadmin/core/escalation.py` is already in `core` with `Ladder` and `step_for`, reused by both `monitor/stalls.py` and (before the cutover) the project nudges
  - **Why it was left**, stated rather than forgotten. The loud rung is `critical`, which breaks through the DND windows by configuration and is the only severity the tray renders non-transient — reserved, by `stalls.py`'s own docstring, for a fault on **this box** that has already been announced and ignored. The estate being a day behind on a scan is not that. And the family most in need of escalation already has it *from the producer*: an idle nudge arrives pre-escalated on the estate's own ladder (`info` at 7 days, `warning` at 14), which this agent takes verbatim
  - **What would settle it**: a third rung, or a `warning`-that-repeats mechanism that is not `critical`. Neither exists, and inventing one to hold this family alone is the wrong shape — if a repeat-without-critical rung is right, `collation.py` and the service families want it too
  - **Found**: 2026-08-13 by Session 45, while deciding whether to wire `core/escalation.py` into the new agent
  - **Fixed 2026-08-16 (Session 53), and the entry's own "what would settle it" was answered in the negative first.** It proposed a third rung, and STATUS.md's recommendation sharpened that to *a third rung in `sysadmin/core/escalation.py` with three callers*. **That rung cannot be heard, and the measurement is the session's first deliverable.** `NotificationPolicy.fingerprint` is `{severity}:{title}` and `_FingerprintState.notified_this_episode` clears only when that pair is **absent from a poll** — which a resolve-and-re-raise inside one agent run never produces, because the tray sees only unresolved rows and both rows are unresolved-then-resolved between two polls. Driven against the real policy: a resolved row replaced by a fresh one carrying a **new message** produced **no notification at all**, where the same fault escalated to `critical` spoke, and a forked title spoke. So the two audible repeats are a severity change and a forked title — and four separate rules in this repository forbid the second, the title being the identity key for dedup, for the resolve and for the tray
  - **Therefore the repeat is a *notification* decision, not a lifecycle one**, and it went where notification policy already lives: `reminder_hours` in `sysadmin_tray/notifications.py`. That is the division this repository already states — *"the tray polls `GET /api/sysadmin/alerts` and owns the notification policy (dedup, flap cooldown, coalescing, digest)"* — and it fixes **every** deduplicating family at once rather than the estate's five surfaces alone, which matters because the estate family is the one place the defect has never actually fired: `estate_judge` has produced **two rows in its life**, both resolved. The live instance on 2026-08-16 was `service_discovery`'s `Unmonitored systemd units: 8 findings`, the **only** unresolved row on the box, open 24 hours and spoken once
  - **The interval is derived, not picked.** `reminder_hours: 24` matches `self_monitor.escalate_after_hours`, the only escalation gap configured on this box, so a family that *owns* a ladder escalates to a different fingerprint — a new episode, announced immediately — before any reminder of its quiet rung is due. Shorten it and the reminder becomes the first thing you hear twice, which demotes the loud rung from news to repetition. The reminder is thus what families with **no** ladder get, which is exactly the population the entry names
  - **Four rules, one of them the opposite of the first draft.** The clock runs from **when the tray last spoke**, not from `alert.created_at` — `stalls.py`'s rule (*the thing that failed was the telling*), and it keeps the single injected clock that makes every window in that module testable without sleeping. A reminder is **never transient**, because the failure it fixes is a toast in an empty room and `transient=False` is what keeps it in the notification history — `flush_digest`'s rule. The **fingerprint is unchanged**, so it updates in place and shares one snooze key: snoozing a fault silences its reminders without a second control. And **reminders fold separately from new alerts**, into `FP_REMINDER` with their own wording, because reporting a fault announced yesterday inside a summary headed "N new alerts" is the one thing a reminder is not — and `attention_max_rows` lets the estate judge put five rows on screen at once, so the volume is real
  - **A defect the fixtures could not have caught, found by a probe.** `state.first_notified_at or state.last_notified_at` reads a monotonic clock reading of exactly `0.0` as absent and falls back to the field every reminder resets — so each reminder reported the *interval* ("24 hours") rather than the age, for a fault that had stood three days. `FakeClock` starts at `1000.0` and every existing fixture with it. Fixed to `is None`, and `test_reminder_reports_the_episode_age_not_the_interval` starts its clock at zero on purpose
  - **Two limits stated rather than discovered later.** In `digest_mode` a non-critical is never reminded, because that mode's contract is that warnings never interrupt and a reminder is an interrupt — making the digest itself periodic is a separate question about a mode that is `false` here and has no live observations behind it. And the policy state is **in memory**, so a tray restart re-announces every open fault as new and restarts the cadence. The third limit is its own entry, `SNAG-TRAY-007`
  - **Fix**: `sysadmin_tray/notifications.py` (`reminder_hours`, `_reminder`, `FP_REMINDER`, `_coalesce` parameterised), `sysadmin_tray/config.py`, `tray_icon.py`, `app.py`, `config.yaml`, plus corrections to the two docstrings whose premise had moved — `sysadmin/estate/agent.py` and `sysadmin/core/escalation.py`, both of which said the omission was deliberate and now say why the alternative was refused. 10 tests in `tests/test_tray/test_notification_calm.py`, 2 in `test_config.py`. Suite **1802** green

- [P3] SNAG-TRAY-007: **the desktop understudy still speaks once — a standing fault repeats only while the tray is running** (2026-08-16, **fixed same day**)
  - **Symptom**: `sysadmin/monitor/desktop.py` is subscribed to `alert.raised` and speaks once per *incident*, staying silent while another unresolved row with that title is open. `SNAG-ESTATE-003`'s fix is `NotificationPolicy`'s, and the understudy shares none of it — so on a box where the tray is not running, which is the only circumstance the understudy exists for, a standing fault is still announced exactly once
  - **Why it is P3**: the tray is the primary speaker and runs here (`sysadmin-tray.service`, user scope). The understudy covers the window where it is not, and the defect it inherits is the pre-fix behaviour rather than a regression
  - **Why the fix is not a copy**: the understudy is **event-driven**, subscribed to `alert.raised` with no poll of its own, so there is no moment at which it could notice that a fault it announced six hours ago is still open. A reminder there needs a periodic read of the open rows — which is a scheduled job or a hook into an existing agent, i.e. a second owner of a lifecycle the tray already owns whenever it is up. That is the "second owner" defect this repository has now found at four scales, so it needs a decision about precedence (`tray_grace_seconds` already answers it for the raise path and would have to answer it for the repeat path too) rather than a patch
  - **Found**: 2026-08-16 by Session 53, while stating what the tray-side fix does not cover
  - **Fixed 2026-08-16 (Session 55)** by giving the understudy a clock of its own: `DesktopNotifier.sweep_reminders`, scheduled as `desktop_reminder_sweep` in `sysadmin/core/jobs.py`. The decision the entry asked for was taken in both halves it names
  - **The precedence answer is `tray_grace_seconds`, and it is the same window for both paths — but it is applied differently.** On the raise path the window *skips*: the tray is about to show this, so say nothing. On the repeat path a skip is wrong, because it leaves `last_spoken_at` sitting at the opening notification, and the first sweep after a tray outage then restates a fault the tray itself restated ten minutes earlier. So a watching tray **stamps the clock forward** instead — while something is polling the route, the last thing said was said by it. The entry's doubt ("the two answers are not obviously the same") was right: the window is the same and the *action* is not, and a test pins the middle window, where a skip and a stamp are the only thing that can be told apart
  - **The second-owner objection is answered by ownership, not by avoidance.** The sweep is a `JobSpec` — `core/jobs.py` owns *when*, `main.py`'s `JOB_TARGETS` owns *what*, and the notifier owns the lifecycle end to end. Hooking it onto the end of `SysAdminAgent._execute` was rejected: that makes an agent responsible for a lifecycle `monitor/desktop.py` holds, which is the defect this repository has now found at four scales and the reason the entry refused a patch
  - **The interval is derived twice over.** `reminder_hours: 24` is the tray's number for the tray's reason (it matches `self_monitor.escalate_after_hours`, so a laddered family reaches its loud rung as news before any reminder of its quiet rung is due) — and two speakers with *different* cadences would make the interval depend on which of them happened to be running, which is exactly what the understudy exists to hide. The sweep's own cadence has **no config leaf**: it is `max(60, tray_grace_seconds)`, because the sweep asks the two questions that window already answers and a second number beside it would be invented rather than derived. Floored so a grace window tuned to a few seconds cannot turn a derivation into a hot loop
  - **The population is what this process announced, never the open rows.** A sweep over `resolved IS false` adopts every fault the tray was speaking for and announces the lot on the first sweep after the tray dies — `SNAG-AGENT-005`'s unbounded `SELECT` wired to a notification each. The spoken set is in memory, so the query is `title IN (:titles_we_said)` and a sweep that has said nothing issues no query at all. What that costs is `SNAG-TRAY-008`, filed rather than left implied
  - **Two rules borrowed rather than rediscovered.** A roll-up takes the **loudest** rung it swallows and folds at two (Session 52's rule and the tray's `coalesce_threshold`) — `notify-send` has no `replaces_id`, so six due reminders would be six toasts. And a reminder that did not land does **not** move the clock, so it goes out on the sweep after the session bus returns rather than a full interval later; the same reason nothing is recorded as spoken until the opening notification has actually landed
  - **Verified live, because the unit tests mock every session and the `IN` clause had therefore never reached PostgreSQL** — Session 44's argument. Driven against the real `alerts` table: `_still_open` selected the live title and refused one never raised; the sweep restated once and then held; a synthetic row inserted, restated inside a roll-up of 2, resolved, and dropped from the spoken set; residue after rollback **0**. `apply_jobs` was driven against a real `BackgroundScheduler` and the real config.yaml — added at `interval[0:03:00]`, re-apply retimed nothing, widening the grace window to 600 retimed it to `interval[0:10:00]`, and `enabled: false` removed it
  - **Fix**: `sysadmin/monitor/desktop.py` (`_SpokenFault`, `sweep_reminders`, `_sweep`, `_still_open`, `_restate`, an injected clock), `sysadmin/core/jobs.py` (`desktop_reminder_sweep`, `MIN_REMINDER_SWEEP_SECONDS`), `sysadmin/core/config.py` (`reminder_hours`), `sysadmin/main.py`, `config.yaml`. 20 tests in `tests/test_desktop_notifier.py`. Suite **1854** green

- [P2] SNAG-DOCS-001: **`CLAUDE.md`'s Contract Registry documents fifteen project endpoints this service has not served since 2026-08-13** (2026-08-16, **fixed 2026-08-17**)
  - **Symptom, measured live 2026-08-16**: `GET /openapi.json` serves **one** route under `/api/projects` — `/api/projects/managed`. `CLAUDE.md` lists **fifteen** in its Contract Registry table, each with a contract model and an "Enforcement" column claiming `response_model`. `/api/projects/overview` and `/api/projects/board` both return `{"detail":"Not Found"}`. The directory `sysadmin/projects/` **does not exist** in this repository, and the file names five of its modules by path — `snapshots.py`, `momentum.py`, `nudges.py`, `branch_actions.py` and `next_action` — as the owners of rules it states in the present tense. `api/projects` appears 26 times
  - **Cause, and it is not a mistake anyone made twice**: the project domain moved to estate-manager on 2026-08-13 (ADR-0002's founding-extraction exception, closed the same day). The *estate* half of `CLAUDE.md` was updated and says so plainly; the **Contract Registry and the architectural narratives beneath it were not**, so the file simultaneously records that the domain left and describes it as present
  - **Why it is P2 rather than P3**: this is the file loaded into context at the start of **every** session in this repository. A stale roadmap document costs a session that reads it; a stale `CLAUDE.md` costs every session by default. It is the `SNAG-ROADMAP-002` failure — a document disagreeing with the live system — on the one surface where the disagreement is read before anything else
  - **What is *not* wrong, so the fix does not overshoot**: the narratives are good writing about real decisions, and several are cited by name from other entries and docstrings. `tests/test_estate_project_contracts.py` correctly remains, because this repository still *consumes* three 8400 project routes. The fix is to move each block behind a pointer to estate-manager the way the estate section already does — **not** to delete the reasoning, which is the estate's now and is worth reading there
  - **Found**: 2026-08-16 by Session 55, while reading the live state for the next-session ranking rather than trusting the documents' account of themselves — the rule those documents ask for, applied to the documents
  - **Fixed 2026-08-17 by Session 58.** The title's "fifteen" is wrong, and it is left standing because *how* it is wrong is the finding. The table held **twelve** `/api/projects` rows, and the population splits three ways rather than two: `GET /api/projects/managed` **is served here** (`sysadmin/monitor/routers/projects_managed.py` — ADR-0005 relocated it *within* this repository and kept its path, because its substance is live `service_health` wearing a project-shaped URL); `/overview` and `/{name}` are **consumed** from 8400, fetched by the tray and parsed with this repository's own models under `tests/test_estate_project_contracts.py`; only the remaining **nine** are neither. A blanket "point them all at estate-manager" — which is what the entry above literally asks for — would have deleted a live route's contract and relabelled a live seam as absent
  - **The narratives were nine blocks, not five.** Measured by grepping the tree rather than counting the paths this entry names: `snapshots.py`, `build_narrative_history`, `/next`, `momentum.py`, `nudges.py`, `/stale`, `status: archived`, the marker scan and `branch_actions.py`. Six further *sentences* compared a live thing to a departed one — "the file-organiser mirror of `/api/projects/actions`", "the third scorer, after the project organiser's repositories", "the two weekly reviews" (there is one), "both project sections read **one** snapshot query" (there are none) — and that is the shape a block-level sweep leaves behind, because each one reads correctly in isolation
  - **Two blocks were rewritten rather than pointed away, because the argument is still ours.** `SysAdminAgent._resolve_recovered` was written as the second half of "both scoring agents resolve alerts set-based": the project organiser made the argument, this repository still runs the statement, and pointing the whole block away would have left a borrowed rule looking invented. The escalation ladder's stated reason for living in `core` — `monitor` may not import `projects` — **expired with the domain**; it now has four climbers instead of two, so the placement outlived its reason, and the text says that rather than leaving a correct conclusion resting on a dead premise. That is exactly the trap `SNAG-AGENT-006` records one entry over, arriving in a document instead of a docstring
  - **Four things the entry did not name, found by sweeping rather than by reading it**: `sysadmin/registry/` is also gone (now `estate.registry` in `estate-lib`) and was still named as the manifest parser in the Database Configuration block, which is the block a new session reads before touching config; the ADR index listed only 0001 and 0002, so **ADR-0005 — the pointer target this entire fix points at — was not linked from `CLAUDE.md` at all**, and 0003 and 0004 were not either; the retention narrative justified `KEEP_LATEST_PER` with a route this service no longer serves, over two tables that are now frozen; and `tests/test_import_boundary.py` still forbids importing a package that cannot exist, which is a guard against bringing it back rather than a live constraint and is now described as one
  - **Verified against the box, not the tree.** `GET :8500/openapi.json` serves **one** route under `/api/projects`. Every claim about which contract models still have a caller was measured with a grep over `sysadmin/`, `sysadmin_tray/` and `tests/` with `contracts.py` itself excluded — the eight that came back zero are `SNAG-DOCS-002`, filed rather than deleted. `CLAUDE.md` is 1,684 lines from 1,774. Suite **1866** green: a documentation change cannot break it, which is the reason to run it rather than not to

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-010: **a judgement that gets *quieter* cannot reach a row that is already open** (2026-08-17, blob half fixed 2026-08-28 by `SNAG-AGENT-009`, **FIXED 2026-08-28** by Session 117 — the rung half)
  - **Symptom, live while it was found**: Session 57 shipped `TRANSIENT_HOLDER_SEVERITY = "info"` at 10:02 and the daemon restarted onto it at 10:06:38, so the fix is running. The two rows it was written for — `Estate port 3110 registry breach` and `Estate port 8110 registry breach`, both dev servers in `app-code-oss-26348.scope`, both listeners still bound — are still `warning`, with `details['holder']` **null**. They were raised at 2026-08-16 12:07, and `EstateJudgeAgent._execute` skips a judgement whose title is in `open_titles` before it looks at anything else, so neither the new severity nor the new `holder` blob reaches them
  - **The dedup is correct and is not what to change.** Raised-once-per-open-row is what stopped the pile-ups this file spends half its length on. What is missing is the *other* direction of Session 39's rule: escalation resolves the quiet row and raises a louder one, because the tray fingerprints on `{severity}:{title}` and an in-place change is never spoken. Nothing does the reverse, so **a fix that quietens a family is silent on every fault standing when it ships** — it applies only to breaches raised afterwards
  - **Why it is P3**: it self-clears. Close the editor, the listeners go, the sweep resolves both rows, and the next dev server is judged by the new code. The cost is bounded to the window in which a reclassified fault happens to be open — here, however long a VS Code window stays up — and to what the tray restates at `reminder_hours` in the meantime, which is `warning` for a fault Session 57 decided is `info`
  - **Not simply "resolve and re-raise on a severity mismatch"**, which is the obvious fix and rebuilds the flip-flop `monitor/collation.py` refuses: the judged severity is computed from `details` the estate republishes every hour, so a producer that wobbles between two rungs would clear and re-open the row each time, and every flip clears the tray fingerprint and notifies. Any fix needs a reason the new severity is *durable*, which the dedup deliberately does not have
  - **Half of it closed on 2026-08-28, by a fix filed against a different entry.** `SNAG-AGENT-009`'s remedy makes a held judgement rewrite the standing row's `message` and `details`, so `details['holder']` — one of the two things this entry says nothing reaches — now arrives on every run. Driven live through this entry's own probe: the standing row comes back holding `{'unit': …, 'transient': True}` with its severity unmoved at `warning`. **What is left is the rung**, in place or by resolve-and-re-raise, and it is left on purpose: Session 39 bans an in-place severity change because the tray fingerprints on `{severity}:{title}` and would keep a fingerprint it has already suppressed, and the fourth bullet above records why the obvious alternative rebuilds `monitor/collation.py`'s flip-flop. So the entry is **narrower, not weaker** — the annotation reaches a standing fault and the reclassification still does not
  - **Found**: 2026-08-17 by Session 58, by looking at the two rows Session 57 was written for instead of at the code that was written for them — a documentation sitting checking whether yesterday's ship is visible on the box
  - **Check (retired 2026-08-28 with the entry)**: `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when a quieter judgement reaches a row that is already open, in any of the three shapes a fix could take: the severity moved in place, the row was resolved and re-raised, or the `holder` blob arrived on its own. **The seventeenth check, and the first that writes to the live database.** The entry's own population predicted itself away — its third bullet says it self-clears when the editor closes — so counting the two dev-server rows would measure whether a VS Code window is open, which is rule 1 refused for the fourth time and the first against an entry that names its own expiry. The mechanism is driven instead, end to end through `EstateJudgeAgent._execute`: an `httpx.MockTransport` supplies the estate's answer through the real `pull_all`, a `unit_audits` row supplies the sweep's attribution the way `_attribution` reads a real sweep, and one already-open row at `warning` with `holder: null` supplies the state the entry was filed from — all inside a transaction that is rolled back unconditionally. **The assertion is the *reach*, never the rung**, because the entry's fourth bullet records that resolve-and-re-raise is the obvious fix and rebuilds `monitor/collation.py`'s flip-flop, so two of the three shapes a fix could take leave the severity column alone. **And the unmoved row is evidence only beside a row that moved**: the same `_execute` call judges a second synthetic port with nothing open under its title, which must land at the quieter rung carrying a transient holder before either verdict means anything — a judge that had stopped computing `info` leaves the standing row exactly as untouched as the dedup does. Live on the first drive, 2026-08-27: port 65010 stood at `warning`/`holder=None` and was unmoved, port 65011 was raised at `info` carrying `transient: True`, `alerts_raised=1`. Both rungs and both titles are read off `judge_audit_findings` run purely on the same payload rather than written down, so a reworded title moves the probe with it instead of leaving it opening a row the run never judges. **Narrowed 2026-08-28**: the third shape landed, so `QuietenReading.reached` stopped reading the `holder` blob — keeping the clause would have reported this entry refuted by a different entry's fix and returned `mismatch` whatever the rung did, which is a control that fix broke. The blob is still carried in the detail, because "the correction reached the row and the rung stayed put" is a stronger statement of the surviving claim than "nothing happened". The probe also had to stop finding its standing row by `message`, which is now the value the run rewrites, and key on the title instead

  - **Fixed 2026-08-28 by Session 117, and the mechanism was already in this repository one domain over.** `sysadmin/monitor/log_aggregator.py`'s `_record_recurrence` has quietened a held row in place since Session 66 and argues why: **Session 39's ban on in-place severity changes is asymmetric, and the reason it exists is what makes the reverse safe.** The ban is about an escalation needing to be *heard* — the tray fingerprints on `{severity}:{title}`, so bumping the column keeps a fingerprint already suppressed and the escalation is recorded and never spoken. A quietening wants precisely that outcome. So the rule moved to `core/escalation.py` as `may_quieten_in_place`, beside the ban it depends on, and `BaseAgent.refresh_alert` gained an optional `severity=` that asks it. All three deduplicating callers now name the rung they judged
  - **"Downward is safe" is the obvious reading of that asymmetry and it is too broad by one rung.** `critical` → `warning` in place hands the tray a fingerprint it *will* speak, so the quietening would arrive as a fresh, less urgent notification about a fault that has not improved — which is the refusal `escalation.step_for` already states in its own docstring, met from the other side. Only the **floor** of `SEVERITY_ORDER` is inaudible-or-asked-for: it is below `tray.notify_min_severity` on this box, and an operator who lowers that knob to `info` has asked to hear reclassifications. `QUIETEST_SEVERITY` is derived from the ordering rather than written as `"info"` — `max_priority_for` against `PRIORITY_MAP`'s rule — and a source-reading test pins the provenance, because a literal and a derivation both *read* `info`
  - **The tray's threshold is deliberately not consulted, and it could not have been.** The obvious gate is "quieter than `notify_min_severity`", which makes the daemon a second reader of a policy the tray owns; measured, the backend cannot see that key at all — `AppConfig` parses `notifications.tray:` (`TrayNotificationsConfig`, "the slice the *backend* needs") while `notify_min_severity` lives in the top-level `tray:` section the tray parses for itself. Adding it would be a config leaf whose only reader is a rule that does not need it
  - **The entry's fourth bullet asks for a reason the new severity is *durable*, and the answer is that the transition is one-directional rather than that the rung is stable.** A producer wobbling between two rungs cannot flip-flop here: down is in place and silent, and back up is refused and belongs to `step_for`'s resolve-and-re-raise. No row is resolved, none re-raised, the count of standing faults does not move, and the tray's fingerprint for the old pair simply leaves the poll
  - **The gate order is where this would have shipped green and inert.** `refresh_alert`'s existing test is "has the text moved"; the founding case is a breach the estate republishes *identically* every hour with only the rung changed, so a quietening asked behind that gate never fires. It is asked **before** the text comparison and can carry a write on its own — `test_a_quietening_lands_even_when_the_sentence_has_not_moved` is the single test that catches it, and it was falsified against exactly that mutation
  - **It reaches the second speaker too, which nothing had noticed.** The tray is fixed for free — the old `{severity}:{title}` pair leaves the poll and the new one is dropped below `notify_min_severity`. `monitor/desktop.py` is not: `_SpokenFault.severity` is the rung it *announced*, and `_still_open` asked only which titles were open, so the understudy would have gone on restating at `warning` a fault the judge had decided is `info` — the founding entry surviving inside the fix for it, in the one component that exists for the case where the tray is down. That read returns `{title: severity}` now and the sweep takes the row's rung, loudest wins for the beat in which an escalation has two rows open
  - **Populations, measured rather than assumed.** The estate judge is the only family with a live one: its own rung varies (`TRANSIENT_HOLDER_SEVERITY` against `DEFAULT_SEVERITY`) and it holds **131 judgements across 77 of 240 runs**. `SysAdminAgent._raise_judged`'s is empty **by construction** — every family there pairs a rung with a *title kind*, so `service_alert_title(name, "degraded")` is `warning` and `…(name, status)` is `critical`, and a disk breach at the two thresholds is two titles rather than one row at two rungs. The port family's is empty because its rung is a constant, now named once as `PORT_ALERT_SEVERITY` rather than as a literal in the raise and nothing at all in the held branch. Both are wired regardless: the entry *is* what happens when a family gains a quieter rung and its held branch was never told what rung it judged
  - **`log_aggregator` was refactored onto the shared predicate rather than left as a second statement of it.** The narrowing costs that family nothing measured — `alert_title` interpolates the entry's own severity, so one title carries one rung and its only downward move is to `NOISE_SEVERITY`, which *is* the floor
  - **The check retired with the entry and the detector did not.** Its drive is re-homed as `tests/test_quietened_judgement_live.py` (`FROZEN_TABLES`' rule) and is **stronger than the check**: that check asserted a *disjunction* on purpose, since a fix could legitimately have landed as any of three shapes, and now that the shape is known a resolve-and-re-raise would satisfy it while rebuilding `monitor/collation.py`'s flip-flop. The live test asks which shape — rung moved in place, one row, still open, nothing raised and nothing resolved — and a mutation implementing the resolve-and-re-raise turns three of its four tests red where the retired check would have said `mismatch` and called it fixed
  - **One falsification passed against deliberately broken code**, which is the part worth carrying: the "loudest of two open rows wins" test yielded its rows loud-*last*, so a last-one-wins implementation with no `_loudest` call in it answered correctly by accident. The query has no `ORDER BY` — which is the whole reason `_loudest` is there — so it drives both orderings now
  - **Verified live and untriggered**: the box holds **0** open alert rows, so nothing on it was reclassified by the deploy. The refutation is the retired check's own drive against the live database — `warning → info`, one row, unresolved, `alerts_raised=1` for the witness port only

- [P3] SNAG-DOCS-002: **eight project contract models survive with no reader, and four are re-exported to the tray** (2026-08-17, opened by Session 58; **scope re-measured 2026-08-24 by Session 76**; **FIXED 2026-08-25 by Session 77 — and the population is fifteen, not eight**)
  - **Re-measured 2026-08-24 (Session 76) before ranking it, and it is wrong in both directions.** Three of the eight are re-exported, not four — `PortfolioActionsResponse`, `ProjectRecommendationsResponse`, `ProjectReviewResponse`. And the population is **wider than eight**: `PortfolioAction`, `RecommendationInfo` and `ProjectHealthInfo` have no reader either, `RecommendationInfo`'s only reference outside `contracts.py` and the tray's re-export list being a *docstring* in `units/recommendations.py` that contrasts it with `UnitRecommendationInfo`. So the session is larger than the entry states, which is the direction worth finding out about before ranking rather than after — the failure `SNAG-LOG-010` demonstrated one sitting over
  - **Symptom, measured 2026-08-17**: `sysadmin/core/contracts.py` still defines `StaleProjectsResponse`, `ProjectBoardResponse`, `NextProjectResponse`, `ProjectMomentumResponse`, `BranchCleanupResponse`, `PortfolioActionsResponse`, `ProjectRecommendationsResponse` and `ProjectReviewResponse`, with their members. A grep over `sysadmin/`, `sysadmin_tray/` and `tests/` excluding `contracts.py` itself finds **zero** readers of any of them. `sysadmin_tray/models.py` re-exports four, which is a name in an import list and not a caller
  - **Why it is the `SNAG-CFG-001` shape**: a pydantic model that validates nothing and is read by nobody is indistinguishable from one that is load-bearing. That entry was a config leaf parsed and read by nothing, and it looked exactly like the knob that decides whether an alert speaks. These are eight of them, in the file `CLAUDE.md` calls the contract registry
  - **Why it is P3 and not higher**: they cost nothing at runtime and they are not *wrong* — the routes they describe exist, on 8400, and three of the eight have a plausible future reader if the tray ever renders the estate's board directly. The risk runs the other way: someone reads `NextProjectResponse` here and concludes this service serves `/next`, which is `SNAG-DOCS-001` reconstructed from the code instead of the document
  - **Why it was not fixed in the same sitting**: removing a name from `sysadmin_tray/models.py` changes the tray's public surface, and Session 58 was a documentation sitting that touched no code. It also needs a decision this entry deliberately does not take — delete them, or keep them and give them the thing that would make them honest, which is a *consumer*: the seam test that already covers `/overview` and `/{name}` extended to whichever routes the tray intends to parse. Deleting is cheap and reversible; pre-staging the test is `pre-stage-blocked-cross-repo-work` applied to a decision rather than to another repository
  - **Found**: 2026-08-17 by Session 58 while fixing `SNAG-DOCS-001`, by measuring which of the registry table's rows still had a reader instead of deleting every row the live route table said was dead
  - **Fixed 2026-08-25 by Session 77, and the entry was wrong a third time in a third direction.** Both earlier measurements were greps for the model's name, which answers "does anything mention this" and is not the question. The property is **reachability from a reader**, and computing it moves the count in both directions at once: 32 models have no external reader and **17 of those are field types of a live `response_model`** — `RamInfo` inside `ResourceResponse` and so on — while `RecommendationInfo` looked alive off a single line of *prose* in `units/recommendations.py`. Session 76 put `ProjectHealthInfo` in the dead set on exactly that evidence; it is a field of `ManagedProjectInfo`, the `response_model` of the one `/api/projects` route this service still serves. **15 classes** are unreachable — the 8 responses plus exactly their 7 exclusive members — and they sat in **two contiguous regions**, which is what the file's own "keep this whole block together — it is appended, never interleaved" comments bought. 83 classes → 68, 1,846 lines → 1,460
  - **The re-export count is five, not four and not three.** `PortfolioAction`, `PortfolioActionsResponse`, `ProjectRecommendationsResponse`, `ProjectReviewResponse`, `RecommendationInfo`. The decision the entry deliberately left open was taken by the owner: **delete from the registry, deprecate on the tray.** Ten go outright; the five that `sysadmin_tray/models.py` re-exports move to `sysadmin_tray/_deprecated_contracts.py` and resolve through a PEP 562 module `__getattr__` that warns on **access**, because `sysadmin_tray` ships in the wheel and an import list is a published surface. Warning at *import* would fire on every tray start whether or not anything touched a deprecated name — `judge_audit_findings` rule 3, one package over. The five are closed under their own field references, so the move cannot strand a served payload, and a test asserts it rather than the docstring claiming it
  - **The durable half is `tests/test_contract_reachability.py`, and it is an AST walk rather than a grep for one reason**: a root is a name **used**, never a name **imported**. That is the distinction Session 58 stated in prose — "a name in an import list and not a caller" — and then measured with a tool that cannot draw it. Skipping `ast.Import`/`ast.ImportFrom` draws it exactly; docstrings are `ast.Constant` and fall out for free, needing no prose heuristic; and `response_model=` needs no special case, being already an `ast.Name` in a keyword, so there is no second statement of one fact
  - **Falsified against the real pre-fix file, which is the only way one limit was visible.** Re-adding a fresh unreachable model trips the guard and reports exactly it. Driven at the **pre-fix** registry it reports **12** of the 15, not 15: `tests` is a consumer package on purpose — a model exercised only by its round-trip test is consumed — so `PortfolioAction` and `RecommendationInfo` leak in as roots from the shim's own annotations and base class, and `PortfolioActionsResponse` from `models.PortfolioActionsResponse` in the new test. Those three can no longer be judged by reachability, and `test_none_of_them_are_defined_in_contracts` is what covers them. A synthetic name is not subject to it, so the synthetic falsification passes cleanly and would have shipped the gap unnoticed
  - **Verified on the box, not only in the tree.** Suite **2238** green, `ruff` clean, `mypy sysadmin` clean; `create_app()` serves 52 routes with `/api/projects/managed` still the only one under `/api/projects`; daemon restarted 07:58:56 and `/health` answers 200 with 11 jobs scheduled


- [P3] SNAG-DOCS-003: **five contract models are importable, deprecated and read by nothing, and removing them needs a fact this repository cannot check** (2026-08-25, opened by Session 77)
  - **Symptom**: `sysadmin_tray/_deprecated_contracts.py` holds `RecommendationInfo`, `ProjectRecommendationsResponse`, `PortfolioAction`, `PortfolioActionsResponse` and `ProjectReviewResponse` solely so `sysadmin_tray.models` keeps resolving them. Nothing in this repository reads any of the five; each access emits a `DeprecationWarning` naming this entry
  - **Why they were not deleted with the other ten**: `pyproject.toml` builds the wheel with `packages = ["sysadmin", "sysadmin_tray"]`, so the module's import list is a published surface rather than an internal tidy. That is the owner's call recorded as taken, not an omission
  - **What closing it needs, and why it is not a code question**: confirmation that nothing outside this repository imports the five names. Every importer measured today is inside it, and the wheel is only ever installed on this box — so the evidence is an *operational* fact about where the artefact went, which is the class of claim `verify-ops-claims-live` says to measure rather than assume
  - **The removal is two deletions and a test edit**: drop the module, drop the `__getattr__` in `sysadmin_tray/models.py`, and drop `TestDeprecatedNamesLeftTheRegistry`'s three behaviour tests. **Do not drop `test_none_of_them_are_defined_in_contracts`** — it is the only cover for the three names `test_contract_reachability` can no longer judge, for the reason that file's docstring measures
  - **Found**: 2026-08-25 by Session 77, as the stated cost of `SNAG-DOCS-002`'s fix rather than by observation — the population is zero readers today, by construction
  - **Check**: <!--check:deprecated_contracts--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when the five names stop being defined, or when anything here reads one. It cannot reach the operational half — where the wheel went — and says which half it measured, which is `ports_checked`'s rule

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-013: **`check:expires` takes a naive instant, so a prediction copied from a UTC-stamped producer is an hour out here and the check cannot say so** (2026-08-25, opened by Session 77, **FIXED 2026-08-28** by Session 114)
  - **Symptom, on the marker's first live expiry**: `STATUS.md`'s block said the estate scan row was "the estate's **stored** scan of 03:32 today" and predicted it "clears at 03:32 tomorrow with nothing done", marked `<!--check:expires 2026-08-25T03:32 the estate scan row clears-->`. The row cleared **with nothing done** — the prediction's substance was right — at **05:32:07**, two hours after the instant named
  - **Cause**: the producer publishes `started_at: "2026-08-25T03:32:17.538288+00:00"` — UTC with an explicit offset — and the block rendered it as a local wall clock. Local is **04:32:17 BST**, which is when `estate-manager-scan.timer` actually fired (measured with `systemctl --user list-timers`), and the judge polls hourly, so the sweep landed at 05:32. **This is `SNAG-LOG-009`'s defect one document over**: a UTC instant read as local, in the block whose checker Session 76 built
  - **The check behaved correctly and that is the point**: it reported `unknown`, never `mismatch` — "the block predicts a moment that has passed and nobody re-measured it" — which is exactly what Session 76's rule 2 reserves `unknown` for. So the checker did not fail; what it cannot do is notice that the *instant itself* was written in the wrong zone, because the marker carries no offset to disagree with
  - **Shape of a fix, so a later session does not re-derive it**: require an offset in the marker (`2026-08-25T03:32+01:00`, or `@<epoch>` as `journal.since_timestamp` already renders for the same reason) and make `ops_claims.py` refuse a naive one — `since_timestamp`'s fail-closed posture, whose empty population is by construction there and would **not** be here, since every marker written so far is naive. Rule 3's pin — the wall clock must also appear in the prose — then has a zone to pin *against*, which is the half that makes it more than a spelling check
  - **Why P3**: one marker exists, its prediction came true, and being two hours out cost nothing. It is filed because the next marker copied off an estate surface is wrong the same way and nothing would say so
  - **Found**: 2026-08-25 by Session 77, by running `./scripts/check-ops-claims.sh` at the top of the sitting and then measuring *why* the moment it named had passed, rather than rewording the sentence
  - **Fixed 2026-08-28.** `EXPIRY_FORMAT` is `%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M%z`, so an `expires` marker carries an offset; `EXPIRY_NAIVE_FORMAT` **recognises** the old shape without accepting it, which is what lets the refusal say *"carries no offset, so it names two instants — 03:32+01:00 if the sentence is in this box's clock, 03:32+00:00 if it was copied from a UTC-stamped surface"* rather than *"not an instant of the form"*; and `check_expiry` refuses a naive `now`. `schema_guard`'s rule that every way of not-knowing fails closed **with its own message**, since the generic form would report this entry's own founding case as a typo
  - **The format was the smaller half; rule 9's pin is the half that makes it more than a spelling change.** The pin now renders the marker's instant **into this box's zone** before looking for it in the prose. Naive, the entry's own block satisfied the pin — the marker said `03:32` and the sentence said 03:32, and both were an hour from the moment predicted, because there was nothing for the two statements of one fact to disagree *about*. With an offset they visibly disagree and the note names which of them is in which clock. Falsified by pinning against `moment.strftime` instead of `moment.astimezone().strftime`: the marker's own rendering is then what is looked for, it is in the prose, and the pin passes exactly as it did before
  - **`@<epoch>` was refused, though the entry names it and `since_timestamp` renders it for this identical fault.** The difference is the reader, not the instant. There the consumer is journalctl, whose zone is the reader's and unknown and whose `--since` has **no offset syntax at all**, so an epoch is the only unambiguous thing it takes; here the consumer is `check_expiry` and the author is a human who must also write the instant's wall clock into the sentence beside it. An epoch is unambiguous and unreadable, so accepting one would buy rule 8 by deleting rule 9 — the pin would become checkable by the checker alone
  - **⚠️ The entry's "two hours" is two mechanisms and only one of them is the marker's**, which its own Cause bullet contains without separating: the timer fired at 04:32 local (the offset — the marker's error) and the hourly judge swept at 05:32 (the poll interval — not). Driven at the entry's own producer stamp through the real `check_expiry` before the fix, the displacement is **1 hour**, exactly this box's offset, and the check that measured it said so in those words. The distinction is not pedantry: a fix sized to two hours would have looked for a second cause that is not there
