  - **The guard ships with an empty live population and a full historical one**, measured rather than assumed either way. `git log -S 'check:expires'` finds **one** marker ever written to `STATUS.md` and it is naive — 1 of 1 — and the block carries **none** today, so nothing in the document is refused on the day the refusal lands. That is the reverse of `since_timestamp`, whose population is empty *by construction* because every caller reads a `timestamp with time zone`; here it is empty by circumstance, and the next marker anyone writes is the one it exists for
  - **The check retires with the entry and the detector does not.** Every member of `CHECKS` names an *open* entry, so `expiry_naive_instant` and its marker are gone; the three-zone drive is re-homed as `TestTheInstantCarriesItsZone` in `tests/test_ops_claims.py`, `SNAG-LOG-006`'s treatment one sitting on. Driven at the fix before it was removed, it reported `mismatch` naming **both** halves of the shape-of-fix above — the offset-bearing instant parsing and the zoneless one no longer being read as a moment — which is the entry refuted by the instrument built to watch it rather than by its author's say-so
  - **Seven mutations, each red on exactly the right test, and one of them is the entry's own blindness.** Reverting `EXPIRY_FORMAT` breaks 13; dropping the `now` guard breaks the one test that drives a marker carrying **no instant at all** (the path that never reaches the subtraction, which is the whole argument for guarding at the entry point rather than at the arithmetic); rendering the pin in the marker's zone breaks the founding case at `Europe/London` and `America/New_York` and **passes at UTC**, because at zero offset the two renderings are the same string. So the parametrised zone test carries a `displaced` flag naming which of its three rows are witnesses and which is a control, rather than letting three green rows read as three pieces of evidence

- [P3] SNAG-SVC-001: **the advice endpoint's `check_interval` row recommends looking at a fault less often, which is `known_noise` rule 3's opposite** (2026-08-25, opened by Session 78)
  - **Symptom**: `GET /api/services/actions` emits a `check_interval` row when a service produced `flap_min_episodes` outages that each lasted a single check. `tasks.md`'s Session 25 Tier 2 scoped it in those words — *"consider raising its check interval"* — and it is built as specified, at the owner's direction after the tension was raised and the request reaffirmed
  - **The tension, stated precisely**: `agents.log_aggregator.known_noise` rule 3 says nothing is recommended as noise on volume alone, because **volume is what makes a fault worth looking at, not evidence it is harmless**. Widening a check interval is the same move in a different register: it does not fix a flap, it reduces the number of times the flap is observed. A row that ends "raise the interval" is a row that closes a fault by looking away
  - **What was done instead of deciding it**: the row is narrowed so it can only fire when *every* episode lasted one poll (`longest_outage_minutes == 0`, which `_outage_episodes` makes exact rather than approximate), it is `RATE_ARGUED` so a gappy window suppresses it, and its wording refuses to name a remedy — it asks the reader to establish which of two things is true and says in the `detail` that "a longer interval hides a real fault just as well as it hides a false one". A test pins both sentences
  - **Why that is a narrowing and not a fix**: the row still exists, and a reader who skims to the action still ends up considering `services.yaml`. The honest resolutions are two and both are the owner's: delete the kind, or give it evidence it currently has not got — a correlation between the blips and the service's own logs, which is `log_actions.group_incidents`' machinery pointed at a different table
  - **Population**: **zero on this box today**, measured. `searxng` is the only service whose episodes are all single-check and it has 2 against a threshold of 3; driven as a counterfactual at `flap_min_episodes: 2` the row appears and is then suppressed by the confidence gate, which is how the gate was verified at all
  - **Found**: 2026-08-25 by Session 78, raised before implementation and filed at the owner's explicit direction to build `tasks.md` as written and record the conflict here
  - **Check**: <!--check:check_interval_looks_away--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when the row stops firing for the shape it was narrowed to, when it argues from more than the blip count, or when `known_noise` rule 3 stops refusing volume alone; the note names **which side moved** and never which of the two resolutions to take, because this entry's fourth bullet reserves that to the owner and nothing measurable here prefers either. **The twenty-fourth check, the fourth built on a synthetic subject, and the first whose claim is a conflict between two rules rather than a fact about one.** Rule 1 decided the shape and the entry stated its own population for the checker — zero on this box, `searxng` being the only all-single-check service at 2 episodes against a threshold of 3 — so a check that looked for the row would report the entry refuted on every day the box behaved and live the first afternoon a health path went slow, which measures the weather. The subject is therefore built: a fully-covered 7-day window carrying exactly `flap_min_episodes` outages of one 300s check each, scored by the real `score_service` and passed to the real `recommend`. **Three instruments, and they refute the entry in three directions.** The row's own `evidence: rate` / `0 points recoverable` is the first resolution's surface; the advice module's **import set** is the second's, since a correlation with the service's own logs cannot be computed by a module that has not got the data — and that is rule 7's fifth and sharpest instance, `service_recommendations.py` naming `log_actions` twice in its module docstring, `log_trends` in `_flapping_row`'s and `known_noise` in the very docstring that filed this snag, so a grep reports all three as already wired and an `ast` import walk sees none. The third is `known_noise` rule 3 itself, **driven rather than read**: one signature at 200 occurrences is offered to `log_actions.recommend` four times — old and flat it *is* noise (the witness), `NEW` it is `new_signature`, `SURGED` it is `surge`, and below the floor it is silent. **A third way for this entry to stop being true, which it does not anticipate**: rule 3 relaxing dissolves the conflict with nobody having touched the row, so a dissolution and a fix must not read alike and the note says which. **The narrowing going is `unknown`, not either verdict** — driven at a two-check episode, which must produce no row; if both drives produce one the headline claim is *more* true and the third bullet false, which is an entry to rewrite rather than a verdict to grade. **One falsification passed against deliberately broken code**, the fourth in this registry and the first whose cause was a rule stated twice in the module being driven: `_is_noise_candidate` carries rules 3 and 4 in its docstring and is the obvious place to model "rule 3 relaxed", and a stand-in aimed there leaves the verdict at `match` — because `recommend`'s loop does `if trend.change is ChangeKind.NEW: continue` **before** the predicate is called and takes `SURGED` in the branch above it. The change-kind rule is stated by the loop *and* by the predicate's admitted tuple, which is `SNAG-DB-003`'s shape inside a module nobody had driven from outside. The check was right; only the stand-in was aimed at the wrong function, and both are pinned now

- [P3] SNAG-SVC-002: **`timer_stale` asks "has it run?" about a timer while `stalls.py` asks it about an agent, and neither knows the other exists** (2026-08-25, opened by Session 78)
  - **Symptom**: `service_recommendations._timer_stale_row` reports an armed timer that has stopped firing. `monitor/stalls.py` reports an agent whose last recorded run is older than `interval x stall_grace_multiplier`, and escalates it on `core/escalation.py`'s ladder. These are the same question about two subjects, answered by two modules with different vocabularies, different thresholds by coincidence rather than by derivation, and no cross-reference
  - **They do not overlap today, and that is a property of this box rather than of the design**: no agent is a systemd timer here — the five agents are APScheduler jobs inside one process — so the populations are disjoint. The day a scheduled job moves to a `oneshot` + `.timer` (which `monitorable-project.md` requires of any *new* scheduled job) the two families would both speak about it, and the second owner would raise a row the first is already escalating
  - **Why it is P3 and not higher**: the `timer_stale` population is **zero on this box today**, measured. All nine declared timers are within their derived cadence; the two weekly ones (`estate-manager-review-timer`, `paccache-timer`) yield no cadence at all at 2 observed firings each and are structurally invisible to the rule until roughly 21 days of history accumulate
  - **The thresholds agree by construction, which mitigates it and does not close it**: `timer_stale_multiplier` is `stall_grace_multiplier`'s 3.0, imported as an argument rather than as a value — the config docstring says so and a reader changing one is told to change the other. What is *not* shared is the ladder: `stalls.py` escalates a standing stall to `critical` after 24 hours and this family owns no ladder at all, so a stale timer rings once at `advice` and is then silent, which is Session 39's founding defect in a new family
  - **Shape of a fix, so a later session does not re-derive it**: give one module the question and the other the subject — most likely `stalls.py` gaining a timer-backed population fed by this module's `_observed_fires`, since the ladder is the part that is expensive to duplicate and it already exists there. Do **not** give `timer_stale` its own ladder; that is the second owner arriving with more machinery
  - **Found**: 2026-08-25 by Session 78, raised before implementation and filed at the owner's explicit direction to build `tasks.md` as written and record the conflict here
  - <!--check:timer_agent_two_owners--> **Checked 2026-08-27 (Session 99) — the twenty-first check, and the third built on a synthetic subject.** This entry has been runner-up three times because the obvious check measures the disjointness the second bullet reports, and that is a property of this box: a scheduled job moved to a `oneshot` + `.timer`, which `monitorable-project.md` requires of every new one, would report the entry fixed on a day nobody had touched either module. So the *mechanism* is reproduced — `check_timer_agent_two_owners` builds the thing this box has not got, an agent whose schedule is a timer, and hands one fact (a daily schedule, one last-run instant) to `summarise_agent`/`stalls.evaluate` as an agent that has not run and to `recommend` as a timer whose `LastTriggerUSec` stopped moving. Both speak: `snagcheck_timer_agent agent stalled` at `warning`, escalating to `critical`, beside `armed but has not fired for 3.0 days` at `advice` and staying there two escalation gaps later. `check_dropin_blind_spot`'s treatment and `check_unmarked_sentence_invisible`'s, now three checks built the same way. **Three instruments, because the two fixes this entry names move different things**: one family going silent is the fix it asks for, a rung appearing on `timer_stale` is the fix it forbids by name, and its headline claim — neither knows the other exists — is measured as the *importer sets*, which are disjoint today (`monitor/agent.py` against `health_review.py`, `reliability_history.py` and `routers/services.py`) and are the one thing a composing caller could not avoid moving. Note that `service_recommendations.py` already carries the word `stalls`, in `_timer_stale_row`'s own docstring, so a grep reports the cross-reference as already existing and an import walk does not — rule 7 for the fourth time. **Two witnesses, because a family gone silent and a family the probe cannot reach report identically**: a schedule that ran one cadence ago must come back not stalled, and a still-firing timer whose last run failed must yield a `timer_failed` row. **The ladder half was wrong when written and a falsification found it, and the stated reason was wrong too until it was measured**: the two drives straddle a rung only when its gap falls in `[overshoot, overshoot + 2 x escalate_after_hours)`. The fresh drive stood one *cadence* past the threshold, making that window **24h to 72h** here — so every rung shorter than a day read loud at both drives, the check saw no movement, and it passed against code deliberately given a ladder. A rung clocked off the fault's own age is the only shape available to a family that is recomputed per request and holds no "when the alarm rang". The overshoot is one check interval now, widening the window to **5 minutes to 48 hours**, and a rung below the monitor's own resolution is one it could not observe in any case

- [P2] SNAG-API-004: **`GET /api/sysadmin/status` counts a declared-unmonitored service as unhealthy, so `all_healthy` can never read true on this box** (2026-08-25, opened by Session 79, **fixed 2026-08-25** by Session 80)
  - **Symptom**: the route computes `"all_healthy": all(r.status == "ok" for r in rows)`. `services.yaml` declares `monitor: false` on three services that are inactive by design — `searxng-upstream`, `sysadmin-tray`, `venture-chat-large` — and the agent writes those checks as `skipped`, so the flag is `False` whatever the box is doing. Measured live 2026-08-25: `all_healthy: False`, three skipped, two genuinely unreachable
  - **It is masked today, which is why it survived**: two services *are* down right now, so the flag is false for the right reason. It has been false for the wrong reason on every day this box has been healthy since `monitor: false` was first declared
  - **The identical defect is already fixed one router over, with the reasoning written down.** `briefing/data.py` computes `all(s["status"] in ("ok", SKIPPED) for s in items)` and its comment says why: *"four units on this estate are skipped by design, so Alfred's grid could never read healthy however well the box was running"*. The fix was applied where it was noticed and never generalised — `SNAG-AGENT-008`'s shape, and Session 78's `skipped`-as-outage defect for the **third** time in one column
  - **Shape of a fix, so a later session does not re-derive it**: import `SKIPPED` from `monitor.services` (the route already has the import path available) rather than writing the string, and do the same audit for the remaining readers of `service_health.status` in one pass — `self_monitor.py` reads `agent_runs.status`, a different column, and `files/actions.py`'s `status` is a file-operation field, so the real population is small and enumerable. The value of doing it as an audit rather than a one-line patch is that this is the third instance and nobody has yet counted the second
  - **Why P2 rather than P3**: it is a wrong answer served by a live route to the tray and to anything else polling it, and the fix is a line. It is not P1 because the per-service statuses beside it are correct, so no consumer is misinformed about *which* service is unwell — only about whether anything is
  - **Found**: 2026-08-25 by Session 79, while ranking the next sitting rather than while building — the `skipped` audit Session 78 recommended on a hypothesis was measured, and the population is not empty
  - **Fixed 2026-08-25** by `STATUS_READINGS` on `models/service_health.py` — the seven values the CHECK constraint admits, classified `well`/`fault`/`unwatched`, read through `is_fault()` and `is_unwatched()`. It sits beside the constraint for `max_priority_for`'s reason, and `tests/test_service_health_status.py` asserts it is **exactly total** over the constraint's own `sqltext` rather than over a list re-typed beside it. So a status added by a future migration fails the suite instead of reclassifying itself at every call site at once, which is what migration 009 did
  - **⚠️ The entry understated its own population, and the instance it did not name is the sharper one.** It says "the real population is small and enumerable" and names one route. There were **three**: `GET /api/sysadmin/status`, `GET /api/summary` (never named by anyone) and `GET /api/projects/managed`. The last is the only one that was **not masked** — measured 2026-08-25, it reported `venture-assistant` and `sysadmin_assistant` unhealthy with every real service `ok`, because each has exactly one `monitor: false` service beside its live ones. The two the entry reasoned about were both false-for-the-right-reason on the day it was written; the one it did not look for was wrong on the page
  - **`/api/projects/managed` had no test at all**, which is why its version was visible and unnoticed. The other two were tested and the tests could not see it: the suite covered a healthy box and an unhealthy one, and never a healthy box with a declaration on it. Every route now carries three cases — a `skipped` row alone reads healthy, a real fault beside a `skipped` row still speaks, and an `error` row is a fault rather than a declaration
  - **`reliability.py` was fixed without an import, on purpose.** Its docstring promises purity, and the vocabulary's owner is an ORM model, so its two status strings stay typed and a test drives both sides against the constraint — `syslog_priority` against `journal.PRIORITY_MAP`. It also stopped negating `ok`: `DOWN_STATUSES` names the four measured faults positively. That changes **no number today** — both readers run over `measured`, which has already dropped the unmeasurable rows — and changes the failure mode, since a newly-admitted status would otherwise land in `measured` and be charged as an outage in exactly the way `skipped` was for eighteen days
  - **The guard that keeps it closed is an AST sweep**, `TestNoReaderNegatesOkByHand`, over every module that reads `ServiceHealth`. What made this the third instance rather than the first is that nothing looked for the others; a rule stated in a docstring would have been the fourth thing to say so
  - **One falsification passed against deliberately broken code**, and it is the failure this document keeps finding in itself. `assert SERVICES_SKIPPED is SKIPPED` was meant to prove `services.py` re-exports the literal rather than restating it, and CPython interns short string literals — so it is True either way. It asserts a *value* where it means *provenance*, which is the shape recorded after Session 59's six guards, and only the source can answer provenance. It is an AST check now, and re-falsified
  - **Verified live over real HTTP after a restart**, and the counterfactual is what proves it rather than the reading. `/api/sysadmin/status` still reads `False` today and correctly — `alfred-frontend` is genuinely unreachable, which is the masking the entry describes — so the fix was driven over the live row set with that one service removed: old `all(status == "ok")` → `False`, new `not any(is_fault(...))` → `True`, across 29 services of which 3 are declared. `/api/projects/managed` moved two projects `False` → `True` with their `skipped` rows still in the grid, and `Alfred` stayed `False` on the genuine outage
  - **What was deliberately left**: a service with **no** health row still makes a project unhealthy on `/api/projects/managed`. A missing row and a `skipped` row are both absences of a measurement and only one of them is a *decision* — nobody declared anything about a service nothing has checked, so there is no evidence to claim health from. Empty population today (every managed service has a row), and pinned by a test so the distinction is not generalised away by the next reading of this fix

- [P3] SNAG-DB-006: **`chk_run_status` admits `cancelled` and nothing has ever written one** (2026-08-25, opened by Session 80)
  - **Symptom**: `agent_runs.status` is constrained to `('running', 'completed', 'failed', 'cancelled')`. Measured 2026-08-25: no module writes `cancelled`, no migration backfills it, and the live table holds **34,362 `completed`, 5 `running`, 2 `failed` and 0 `cancelled`** across the column's whole life
  - **It is `SNAG-CFG-001`'s shape at the size of a constraint value**: a vocabulary member parsed by the database and produced by nothing, which a reader takes as evidence that some path cancels a run. None does — `BaseAgent.run` has two outcomes and `_record_outcome` writes one of them
  - **Why it is P3 and not P2**: nothing reads it wrongly. `self_monitor._failure_streak` is written **positively** (`== "running"` skips, `== "failed"` counts, anything else ends the streak), so an unexpected value breaks a streak rather than being charged as a failure — the allow-list shape, which is why the sibling column does **not** carry `SNAG-API-004`. That is a property of how that function happens to be written, not a guarantee
  - **Shape of a fix**: either drop `cancelled` from the constraint in a migration, or find the path that should write it — a run killed by a `SIGTERM` mid-execute currently leaves a permanent `running` row (Session 41's stated cost), and `cancelled` is plausibly the value that row was meant to carry. Those are opposite fixes, so the decision is which one was intended, and nothing records it
  - **Found**: 2026-08-25 by Session 80, while pricing the next sitting rather than while building — the `service_health.status` audit was extended one column over to see whether the defect repeated, and it does not, which is the answer that was worth measuring
  - **Check**: <!--check:run_status_cancelled--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when `chk_run_status` stops admitting `cancelled`, or when a row carries it — which are this entry's two opposite fixes, so the check names which one was taken

- [P3] SNAG-CFG-002: **`schedules.review_hour` and `review_minute` are parsed by pydantic and read by nothing, and have been since the projects domain left** (2026-08-25, opened by Session 79)
  - **Symptom**: `SchedulesConfig` carries `review_hour: int = 5` and `review_minute: int = 30` under a comment reading *"Weekly project review — before the Monday briefing so the briefing can carry the fresh narrative"*. That review moved to estate-manager on 2026-08-13 (ADR-0005). Measured 2026-08-25: no module, test, `JobSpec` or `config.yaml` line reads either leaf. `review_day_of_week`, in the same block, **is** read — by both surviving weekly jobs — so the block cannot simply be deleted
  - **It is `SNAG-CFG-001` exactly**: a knob parsed and read by nothing, sitting beside knobs that are read, and named closely enough (`review_*` among `disk_review_*` and `log_review_*`) that a reader edits it expecting an effect. That entry's own summary is *"read by nothing, and looked exactly like the knob that decides whether an alert speaks"*
  - **What this sitting did instead of fixing it**: nothing, deliberately. Session 79 added `health_review_hour`/`health_review_minute` beside them and left these in place, because deleting a config field is a change to a published surface and the sitting's scope was a roadmap tier. The new leaves are named for what they schedule, so the vacated `review_*` pair was **not** reused — a leaf whose name says "review" generically, scheduling one specific review among three, is the ambiguity this entry is about, reproduced deliberately
  - **Shape of a fix**: delete both leaves and their comment, keep `review_day_of_week`, and check `config.yaml` sets neither first (it sets neither today, measured). One line of the reload's `RESTART_ONLY`/`LIVE_AT_STARTUP` classification test needs no change, because a leaf read at no point is not classified by it — which is itself worth noting: **the guard that exists to catch unclassified config paths cannot see a path nothing reads**, so the mechanism that would have caught this is blind to it by construction
  - **Why P3**: it misleads a reader and costs nothing at runtime. It rises if anyone edits it expecting the health review to move
  - **Found**: 2026-08-25 by Session 79, while looking for a free Monday slot — the search for one turned up a leaf that already named the slot and drove nothing
  - **Check**: <!--check:review_schedule_unread--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when anything reads `schedules.review_hour`/`review_minute`. An `ast.Attribute` walk rather than a grep, because `grep review_hour` matches the three `*_review_hour` leaves that *are* read and would refute this entry on its first run

- [P3] SNAG-DOCS-004: **two Tier 3 modules document their prompt as containing no digit by construction, and both prompts contain digits** (2026-08-25, opened by Session 79, **fixed 2026-08-25** by Session 81)
  - **Symptom**: `log_review.build_review_prompt` and `files.review.build_review_prompt` both say *"Contains no digit by construction"*. Measured 2026-08-25, both prompts contain `1`, `2`, `3` and `150` — the section numbers and the word limit inside their own `REVIEW_INSTRUCTIONS`, which is appended to every prompt they build
  - **The behaviour is right and the claim is not.** Rule 2 exists because dria-agent-a-3b restated figures it was handed *and derived a quotient from them*; a section number and a word limit are instructions to the model rather than measurements about the machine, and no live generation has ever restated either. So this is a false sentence rather than a leak, which is why it is P3 and not higher
  - **Why it is filed rather than shrugged at**: this repository's `figure_free` gate, its `SIGNATURE_DETAIL_CHARS` cap and its whole live-verification habit rest on that sentence being checkable. A reader who checks it finds digits, and the next reader to check will either weaken the rule or add a filter the instruction block does not need. `SNAG-DOCS-001`'s shape at the size of a docstring
  - **What this sitting did**: stated the narrow claim correctly in `health_review.build_review_prompt` — *no digit reaches this prompt **from the data*** — and pinned it for all three modules in `tests/test_health_review.py::TestPromptIsFigureFree`, which partitions each prompt at its own `REVIEW_INSTRUCTIONS` and asserts the data half is digit-free. So the property is now **tested** everywhere and **misdescribed** in two places
  - **Shape of a fix**: reword the two sibling docstrings to the narrow claim and move the partition helper somewhere all three tests can share it. Do **not** strip digits from the instruction block; the model needs the section numbers to produce sections and the word limit to stop
  - **Found**: 2026-08-25 by Session 79, by printing the digits in the prompt it had just built rather than by reading the docstring it had just copied
  - **Fixed 2026-08-25** by Session 81. Both docstrings now state the narrow claim — *no digit reaches this prompt **from the data*** — and each names the half it does not cover, so a reader who checks finds the digits already accounted for. `health_review`'s docstring was reworded too: it carried a present-tense description of the siblings' defect, which would have become the stale sentence the moment the defect went away
  - **The helper was stated three times, not once, and the three had diverged** — which the entry's shape-of-fix did not know, because it had only looked at two of them. `test_disk_review` and `test_log_review` held `_data_lines` (split, then strip API paths); `test_health_review` held `_data_half` (partition, and assert the marker was found). **Each disagreement has a right side**, so `tests/review_prompts.py` is the **union** rather than any one of them. Deduplicating onto either sibling would have silently dropped the boundary assertion; onto the health copy, the API-path exclusion
  - **The API-path strip is a no-op today and is kept as policy**, measured rather than assumed: across all three live fixtures exactly one data half contains an API path at all (`POST /api/files/clean/downloads`) and it is digit-free. It earns its place the day a route is versioned — `/api/v2/…` is not a leak
  - **`split(instructions)[0]` was not a false green, and saying so is the point.** With no instruction block there are no instruction digits to exclude, so the digit test would pass for a *stricter* reason. What it did was return the whole prompt while being called "the facts half" — `ports_checked`'s rule one directory over, where zero-because-clean must not be served as zero-because-blind. `data_half` asserts the marker instead, and `tests/test_review_prompts.py` pins the old form's behaviour beside the new one so the reason survives the copy that carried it
  - **Nine tests exist so the shared assertion can be seen to fail**, one per rule plus the two boundary cases: a shared guard that never refuses anything is worth less than the three copies it replaced, because a copy at least had a reader. Each was driven at something that must break it before it was written down
  - **One correction the sitting made to itself**, worth recording because it is this entry's own defect in miniature: the note explaining why the strip is left greedy first read *"no route on this service takes a query string"*, written from plausibility. One `grep Query(` refuted it — `/api/logs/recent` alone takes five. The note now states what was measured: the executors that reach a prompt are hand-written literals in `files/recommendations.py`, every one a bare path followed by a space
  - **`CLAUDE.md` carried the wider claim too** and was corrected in the same sitting — it said the guard asserts "no digit reaches the model", which is the sentence this entry is about, one document up
  - **Deliberately not done**: no digit was stripped from any `REVIEW_INSTRUCTIONS`. The model needs the section numbers to produce sections and the word limit to stop, which is the entry's own instruction and remains right

- [P3] SNAG-TRAY-008: **the understudy forgets what it announced, and never adopts what it did not** (2026-08-16, opened by Session 55, **FIXED 2026-08-28** by Session 115 — both faces)
  - **Symptom**: `DesktopNotifier._spoken` is an in-memory dict, and the reminder sweep's population is exactly its keys. Two standing faults are therefore never restated by the daemon. (1) **A fault raised while the tray was up.** The understudy was correctly silent, so the title is not in `_spoken`; if the tray then dies, that fault is never restated — only faults raised *after* the tray went away are. (2) **Anything at all, after a daemon restart.** The set is rebuilt only by `alert.raised` events, so an open fault that was announced before the restart is silent until it happens to be re-raised
  - **Why it is P3**: it is the *narrowing* that makes `SNAG-TRAY-007`'s fix safe rather than an oversight in it. Widening the population to `resolved IS false` is the unbounded `SELECT` that made `SNAG-AGENT-005` fall over on the backlog it existed to end, and it would announce every standing fault at once on the first sweep after a tray outage. The tray carries the same in-memory limit for the same reason (`SNAG-ESTATE-003`'s second stated limit), so this is the understudy inheriting a known cost, not acquiring a new one
  - **Why it is not simply "persist the spoken set"**: a table would make the daemon's notification state durable and therefore *answerable to a retention policy*, and it would still not fix (1), which is a scoping question rather than a persistence one. The two faces have one root — the population is what this process said — so a fix that addresses only the second half leaves the first looking fixed
  - **Shape of a fix, so a later session does not re-derive it**: adopt a fault the daemon did **not** announce only when the tray has been absent for a full `reminder_hours`, and cap the adopted set the way `attention_max_rows` caps the estate judge's. That makes the first sweep after a tray outage quiet by construction and bounds the query without needing the spoken set to be complete
  - **Found**: 2026-08-16 by Session 55, as the stated cost of `SNAG-TRAY-007`'s rule 1 rather than by observation — the population is zero today
  - **Fixed 2026-08-28** by `desktop_notifications` (migration 018) plus `DesktopNotifier._adopt` — rules 5 and 6 of the module. One row per fault the daemon is speaking for, keyed on the title and written only when something is **said**; and a capped, SQL-bounded scan that carries a standing fault this daemon never announced
  - **The number that reranks the entry from P3 is the restart cadence, and nobody had measured it.** `sysadmin.service` started **111 times in 28.26 days** — median uptime **1.77 h**, mean 6.17 h, and **5 of 110** lives reached the 24 hours `reminder_hours` asks for. So `SNAG-TRAY-007`'s fix was structurally unavailable on **95 %** of this daemon's lives. The entry read this as a narrowing with two named costs; it is also a reminder that could almost never fire, and that half was invisible because the population it was measured against was zero
  - **The entry's own shape-of-fix is unreachable as written, and the same measurement is why.** It asks for adoption *"only when the tray has been absent for a full `reminder_hours`"*. `TrayPresence` is in-memory and monotonic by deliberate design — its docstring argues for both — so a process can observe 24 h of absence only if it has lived 24 h, which is one life in twenty-two. Shipped as a **refusal** the fix would have been correct, green, and inert. It ships as an **anchor** instead: the absence sets the adopted fault's `last_spoken_at` *back*, capped at one interval, so a fault adopted the moment the tray leaves still waits a full interval — the quiet-by-construction property the entry asked for — and one adopted after a day of silence speaks at once. The retired check's timeline had to manufacture two intervals of absence with a supplied clock precisely because the box cannot produce one
  - **The two faces are multiplicative, not independent** — `SNAG-AGENT-008`'s shape, and the mechanism the entry describes without naming. Adoption alone re-adopts on every restart and re-arms its own anchor, so on a 1.77-hour daemon it would never speak; the store alone leaves face 1 exactly as filed. Neither half is worth shipping without the other, so a fix for one is not half the benefit, it is none
  - **The clock became a wall clock, and that is a change of reading rather than of storage.** No monotonic value survives a process — and on Linux `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` does not survive a suspend either, so a workstation asleep overnight paid nothing towards an interval that is precisely about elapsed human time. `TrayPresence` keeps monotonic for its own 180-second question, where a suspended box correctly counts nothing because neither process was running. The two now differ on purpose
  - **The store records what was *said*, never what the tray's presence implied.** Rule 2 stamps a watching tray's clock forward *in memory* and does not write it, which bounds writes at one per notification — and is safe because a restored stale stamp cannot act (a reminder needs the tray absent) and that same gate corrects it on the first sweep after a restart, inside one grace window. The cost is stated: a restart while the tray is up, followed by the tray dying inside that one window, produces one early toast
  - **The old cheapest gate could not survive persistence and was replaced rather than kept.** *"A sweep that has said nothing issues no query at all"* is exactly the entry — not knowing what the last process said is indistinguishable from it having said nothing. The bound moved from *per sweep* to **one query per process**, and on a box where the tray runs the tray gate returns before anything else is read
  - **Retention takes both halves in migration 018**, migration 011's rule, at **30 days** — derived rather than picked, and cheap precisely because the two halves make each other cheap: forgetting a live row costs one re-adoption, so the window need only comfortably exceed one reminder interval
  - **A test that pinned the defect as correct behaviour had to be inverted.** `test_a_fault_the_tray_announced_is_never_adopted` asserted the sweep's population *is* what this process said — the cost this entry was filed to record — and had been green since Session 55. Twenty-eight mutations were driven against the new guards and every one lands red on a test about its own rule; **six passed against deliberately broken code first**, four of them upsert columns a fake could not witness because a fake replaces the whole row on conflict and therefore agrees with an `ON CONFLICT` that keeps the old value
  - **The harness had to be hardened before the check could be run, and the leak was not hypothetical.** `rolled_back_drive` rolled back a plain session, which holds only while nothing inside commits — and `_remember` must commit, since a store write without one did not happen. The first full-suite run after the fix committed the probe's transaction: **three rows into `alerts` and three into `desktop_notifications`**, found by counting either side and deleted by hand. The session now joins the connection's transaction by savepoint, so an inner commit releases a savepoint and the outer rollback is still the only writer of record — a harness that cannot survive the code it drives is a control the next fix breaks
  - **Deploying it found a second, independent reason the reminder path was inert, and no amount of reading would have.** `DesktopNotifier` resolved `get_session_factory()` — the *application's* pooled engine — while every call it makes runs on a loop that is not the application's: the sweep is an APScheduler job and `scheduler._run_async` wraps each firing in its own `asyncio.run`, and `on_alert_raised` is published from inside an agent's run, which is another. A pooled asyncpg connection belongs to the loop that opened it, so the first query out of a restarted daemon raised `RuntimeError: got Future … attached to a different loop`, followed by `InternalClientError: got result for unknown protocol state 3`. **`_still_open` has carried that defect since Session 55 and has never once executed on this box**, because the sweep's old first gate — *"a daemon that has announced nothing issues no query at all"* — returned before reaching it. So `SNAG-TRAY-007`'s reminder could not have worked here even for a fault the daemon *had* announced, and this entry's own symptom was hiding it. `_factory()` returns `get_scheduler_session` now — `NullPool`, an engine per call, which is what every agent on this box already does — and the commit belongs to that context manager rather than being restated. Found by restarting the daemon and reading **three `Log error: sysadmin.service` rows** out of the live table
  - **Its first live exercise adopted a real fault**, which is the strongest evidence available that face 1 was costing something: the drive picked up `High VRAM usage on AMD Radeon RX 7900 XTX`, open on this box since 15:57 and never announced by this daemon because the tray was watching. Under the old code nothing here would ever have restated it. It is also why the live test asserts a **floor** on the restated count rather than an equality — the population is the box's and it moves
  - **Check retired 2026-08-28 with the entry**, every member of `CHECKS` naming an open one — and **the detector did not go with it**. `tests/test_desktop_store_live.py` is the same two-sweep timeline against the live database, `FROZEN_TABLES`' rule and Session 112's precedent. Its last live verdict before retirement was **`mismatch`**, with **0 rows of residue**: the first sweep restated 2 (its own witness *and* the fault raised while the tray was watching), the sweep after the restart restated 3 (its own and both of its predecessor's). The retired wording is kept below for the record

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-004: **the "never take a tool's default port" rule is written in the estate's own guide and enforced by nobody** (2026-08-14, **delegated**, **fixed by estate-manager 2026-08-27**, closed here 2026-08-27)
  - **Symptom**: `monitorable-project.md` §2.1 states the rule in prose — "8080 is llama.cpp's default… never take a tool's default port" — and annotates the one live violation by hand ("venture-assistant holds 8080 today only because it was allocated before this rule existed; it should move to 8301 when next touched"). Nothing checks it. The audit's `ports` check reconciles the table against `ss` in both directions and has no concept of a *contended* port, only an unclaimed or a silent one
  - **Consequence**: the rule degrades at the speed the guide is read. A hand-written annotation on one row is not a control — it says a violation was noticed once, not that the next one will be
  - **This was Session 26b's third checkbox and is being handed over rather than built here**, which is a change of owner and worth the reasoning. Detecting it is *filing a finding*, not alerting, so it is squarely the audit's remit (estate ADR-0003: conformance checks, never alerting and never fixing). It needs no privileges — the markdown table plus `ss -tln`, both already in `checks/ports.py`. And it is a rule about *their* document, in *their* guide, which the estate rules make theirs to enforce. Building it here would have this repository re-parse a cross-repo convention document, the same inversion that killed Session 26b's first checkbox
  - **Shape of the fix**, so the estate session does not re-derive it: a third branch in `run_check` beside `unclaimed_listener` and `claimed_but_silent`, over `WELL_KNOWN_DEFAULTS = {3000, 5000, 8080, 8888, 9000}` intersected with `claimed_ports`, at `SEVERITY_INFO` — **advisory, not `warn`**, because it has not collided and the remedy is a port move, which is work rather than a correction. Live population today: exactly one, 8080
  - **Not yet handed over.** Unlike `SNAG-ESTATE-002` this has no counterpart entry in estate-manager yet; it needs an estate-manager session to record it. **The IDs will not correspond** — that repository numbers its own `SNAG-ESTATE-*` series, which is how `SNAG-ESTATE-002` here became its `SNAG-ESTATE-010`
  - **Check (`default_port_uncontended`, removed 2026-08-27 with the entry)** — **written 2026-08-27 (Session 100) — the twenty-second check, and the fourth driven across a repository boundary.** The first whose claim is entirely about another repository's *surface*: the three before it ask what estate-manager's code computes — what a dataclass offers, what a function returns — and this one asks what their audit **publishes**. So the instrument is `CheckResult.findings` and never the source of the branch that would fill them. An `ast` walk for the `WELL_KNOWN_DEFAULTS` name this entry proposes is the obvious probe and is wrong in three directions at once: a fix that inlines the set, renames it, or files from a different check module would each read as *still holds*, the entry reported true by a probe looking in the one place the fix happened not to land. All three are driven here as real stand-ins. **The mechanism is asked and never the population** — rule 1, and this entry is where the difference is starkest, since the live violation is *one port* whose own registry row says it should move to 8301 when next touched, so a check that looked for a contended port on the box would report the entry refuted the day somebody moved one service and would have measured nothing about whether the rule acquired an enforcer. The contention is **built**: a registry document claiming all five of this entry's default ports, each answering, driven through their real public `run_check` with a stub `runner=` — so no privilege, no socket, and nothing written into their tree. Live, the five go unremarked and the two witnesses fire, `8080 (venture-assistant)` is carried as evidence, and §2.1 still states the rule. **The listener witness is load-bearing in the direction easiest to miss**: with the listener set empty every probed default is *claimed and silent* and their existing branch names all five, which a check asking only "does a finding name a default port" reads as the fix having landed — driven as its own test off the raw probe, so the gate is shown to be standing in front of something. **The code vocabulary is learned from the witnesses and never typed here**, so a fix filing under any slug at all is seen; the test that says so had to be rewritten, because the obvious one passes against a typed copy too. **The rule half refutes this entry for the opposite reason to the enforcement half** and is reported apart — the sentence leaving §2.1 is the premise dying with the complaint intact, and an unreadable guide is `unknown`, since *still holds* asserts a rule exists to go unenforced. Not measured, deliberately: whether the handover has happened, because the entry says their ids will not correspond and matching one would be prose similarity dressed as a measurement. **A falsification passed against broken code and deleted a gate rather than fixing one** — the draft carried a witness per loop, and removing the claimed-half gate broke nothing: deleting that loop leaves every probed default unreached, so the reachability drive always answers first and the symmetric gate was unreachable by construction. That drive replaced a gate that was a second implementation of their fact, a standalone `parse_registry` call `run_check` can diverge from — a stub narrowing the parser to the audited ranges walked straight through it, five rows seen against three
  - **Found**: 2026-08-14 by Session 26b-A, while checking which of Session 26b's four checkboxes the estate's 2026-08-13 audit had already overtaken
  - **⚠️ The check went RED on 2026-08-27, during Session 102's postflight, and this entry is recorded as closeable rather than closed.** `check_default_port_uncontended` reports **refuted**: the estate's audit now files `claimed_tool_default` for all five probed defaults — 3000, 5000, 8080, 8888, 9000 — with `claimed_but_silent`, `dormant_but_listening` and `unclaimed_listener` observed beside it, which is the enforcement this entry says nobody provides. Their live population today is one row, 8080 (`venture-assistant`), and §2.1 of the guide still carries the rule the audit now enforces. **Not closed here, deliberately.** Session 102 was writing `SNAG-SVC-001`'s check and judging two others; closure is a *judgement* — this document's own convention says a red line in that family is news rather than a fault, so nothing in it edits a document and neither script blocks on it — and a check leaves the registry with its entry, which is a second edit in the same sitting on a decision nobody asked for. Recorded at the moment it was observed so the next sitting does not have to notice it twice, which is the whole reason `SNAG-ROADMAP-001` left a mechanical trigger rather than an intention
  - **Closed 2026-08-27 by Session 104, on the estate's published surface and not on their source.** `check_default_port_uncontended` reports **refuted**: driving their own public `run_check` over a registry document that claims all five of this entry's defaults, each answering, files `claimed_tool_default` for **3000, 5000, 8080, 8888 and 9000**, while the three witnesses fire beside it (`claimed_but_silent` on the silent port, `unclaimed_listener` on the listener half, `dormant_but_listening` on the dormant row) — so the instrument is standing in front of something and the vocabulary the check compares against was learned from that run rather than typed here. `estate_module_state()` reads **committed**, which is the mechanical trigger `SNAG-ROADMAP-001` was closed on and the one thing that separates a landed fix from an edit in flight.
  - **Committed *and* deployed, which is stronger than the precedent needed and is deliberately not the reason.** `SNAG-ROADMAP-001` closed against a fix their daemon was demonstrably not yet serving, and named that reason discharged rather than met — holding a delegated entry open on the owner's restart state is the second owner the estate rules exist to prevent. Here it is met by observation anyway: `GET :8400/api/audit/findings` carries `ports:port 8080:claimed_tool_default` at `info`, `first_seen_at` **2026-08-27T15:00:13Z**, `runs_observed: 4`, naming venture-assistant and quoting §2.1 in its summary. Recorded as evidence, never as the test.
  - **The delegate did the work and closed the delegation.** estate-manager's `8f8821d`, *"the default-port rule gets an enforcer, twenty-one days late"*, names this repository's cross-repo message `926a8c61` and their ADR-0055. The entry dies the right way round: §2.1 **still states** *"Never take a tool's default port"*, so the complaint went and the premise did not — which is the distinction the check reports apart and the reason a single boolean over the two halves would have filed a deleted rule as a job well done.
  - **The delegated constant changed shape, and that is the one occasion the check's design could be tested.** Their branch is a **dict** keyed port → tool names, not the `WELL_KNOWN_DEFAULTS = {3000, 5000, 8080, 8888, 9000}` set this entry's *shape of the fix* bullet proposed, and they measured the set before typing it: nine of seventeen registry rows sit on some tool's default depending entirely on which tools are counted, so the five named here were *right today by luck* and 1883, 8384 and 22000 are absent by decision rather than by oversight. An `ast` walk for the name this entry proposed — the obvious probe, refused in writing in the check's own docstring on the grounds that a fix which *inlines the set, renames it, or files from another module* would each read as **still holds** — would have reported this entry true against a fix that renamed **and** reshaped it. Reading `CheckResult.findings` instead is what saw it.
  - **What the closure found on this side is older than the fix.** `sysadmin/estate/judgements.py`'s `JUDGED_AUDIT_SEVERITY` enumerated their ports vocabulary as two codes — `breach` for an unclaimed listener, `warn` for a claimed port that is silent. It is **four**, and the one it omitted, `dormant_but_listening` at `info`, has been in their check since it was written on 2026-08-13: the sentence was incomplete the day it was typed rather than made stale by today's branch. Corrected in this sitting. Neither `info` code reaches `judge_audit_findings`, which filters on `breach`, and that is the **right** outcome twice over — this entry's own fix bullet asked for `SEVERITY_INFO` *advisory, not `warn`*, and `info` sits below `tray.notify_min_severity` here, so a judged row would be silent anyway. What was missing was the record of the decision, which is `SNAG-CFG-001`'s shape; it is written down now rather than answered by judging a finding this repository asked to be advisory.
  - **Cited repo-qualified, because the id is poisoned in exactly one direction.** estate-manager's message `153c1c96` (their Session 60, their `SNAG-ESTATE-073`, their ADR-0055 §2) measures the collision: they define 71 `SNAG-ESTATE-*` entries to this repository's 14, **all 14 collide**, and every colliding pair names a different defect. Their `SNAG-ESTATE-004` is an abandoned January `projects.project_meta` prototype, closed **2026-08-13 — the day before this delegation was filed** — so an id-shaped lookup answers plausibly and stops the session, and the more diligent the session the more reliably it stops. This entry's *Not yet handed over* bullet knew the ids would not correspond and said so **in this document**, which reached a reader of the snag list and not a reader of their inbox. The counterpart is therefore named here as **estate-manager ADR-0055** and **estate-manager `SNAG-ESTATE-073`**, reached through cross-repo message `926a8c61`, and never as a bare number.

- [P2] SNAG-AGENT-006: **a sustained fault still writes one alert row per run — 60 rows for one dead timer in five hours** (2026-08-13, **fixed 2026-08-14**)
  - **Symptom, live while it was found**: `sysadmin-organiser-timer critical` held **60 unresolved rows** created between 07:41 and 12:36 on 2026-08-13 — one every five minutes, exactly `health_check_interval_seconds`. One fault, sixty rows. `venture-chat unreachable` shows 85 rows across 36 hours and `estate-broker-provision critical` 42, the same shape at whatever rate the fault recurred
  - **Cause**: `BaseAgent.raise_alert` inserts unconditionally, and `SysAdminAgent._handle_status` and `_check_thresholds` both call it on every run the condition holds. There is no open-row check on either path. `_resolve_recovered` protects by **title**, so the previous fifty-nine rows are protected alongside the newest one and none of them resolve until the fault clears
  - **This is SNAG-AGENT-004's other half, and naming it that way is the point.** That entry fixed the *resolve* side: 51,924 rows that could never be closed because recovery was observed inside the loop over configured services. It did not touch the *raise* side, which is what generated the volume in the first place — `redis unreachable` reached 6,283 rows one five-minute poll at a time. Session 41's fix means those rows now close and `run_retention` purges them at 30 days, so the leak is **bounded** where it used to be immortal. It is not gone
  - **What it actually costs now**, stated rather than assumed. The tray does *not* re-notify: `sysadmin_tray/notifications.py` fingerprints on `{severity}:{title}`, so sixty rows produce one toast. The cost is that `GET /api/sysadmin/alerts` and every count built on `resolved = false` read sixty times high, and a week-long outage would carry 2,016 unresolved rows for one fault. That is a legibility defect rather than a storm, which is why it is P2 and not P1
  - **Found**: 2026-08-13 by Session 44, while establishing whether the new collation check could safely follow the `_check_thresholds` pattern. It could not — a stale collation persists for weeks, so eight databases × 288 runs would be 2,304 rows a day — so `collation.py` dedups on an open row instead, and the divergence is what surfaced this
  - **The fix is not "add dedup to `raise_alert`", and that is the trap.** Dedup and `RESOLVABLE_TITLE_PATTERNS` are mutually exclusive: the sweep closes every owned row the run did not re-raise, which is sound *only* for a family that re-raises every run. Making the threshold and service families dedup without also removing them from that tuple makes their rows flip-flop — resolved on the run that holds, re-raised on the next — and each flip clears the tray fingerprint, so it starts notifying on every poll. A pile-up is loud; that would be loud *and* read as recovery. Whichever way it is fixed, both halves move together
  - **Left unfixed deliberately.** Session 44's subject was the collation check, and changing the lifecycle of the service and threshold families is the larger and riskier half of this — the families that carry `critical`, the ones the tray acts on. `sysadmin/monitor/collation.py` is the worked example of the pattern to copy, with the mutual exclusion documented against `RESOLVABLE_TITLE_PATTERNS` and pinned by `tests/test_collation_check.py`
  - **⚠️ The bullet above about the trap was superseded on 2026-08-14, and it is the part that would have produced a worse fix.** It says dedup and `RESOLVABLE_TITLE_PATTERNS` are mutually exclusive, so these families must be removed from the tuple. **That was true only of the exclusion set as it then stood** — it held the titles the run *raised*, so a family writing nothing on its second run had its still-true row swept. Removing the patterns would have undone SNAG-AGENT-004 and stranded every deconfigured service's rows again, since a set built from configuration cannot contain a service that has left it. **The patterns stayed.** Only the exclusion set changed, to the titles the run **judged** — the third option `sysadmin/estate/agent.py` had already proved and Session 45's handoff named
  - **Fixed 2026-08-14** by `SysAdminAgent._raise_judged`: it records the title in `_judged_titles` *first*, then asks whether a row is needed against `_open_titles`, one snapshot read at the top of `_execute`. Dedup suppresses the raise, never the judgement — so a fault that persists is in the protected set on every run and is never swept, and a fault that clears leaves it exactly once and resolves exactly once. `_raised_titles` and the `raise_alert` override that filled it are **deleted**: with the exclusion set no longer built from raises it had no reader, which is the SNAG-CFG-001 shape
  - **`% auto-restarted` is exempt, via an explicit `dedup=False`.** It is an event, not a state: `_failure_counts` resets to zero the moment `restart_unit` returns, so the title fires once per restart *cycle*, and a second restart three hours later is a second piece of news that dedup would swallow. It is still *judged*, so the sweep leaves it alone and it resolves with the rest of its family when the service is next measured healthy. Same distinction `log_signature.py` draws between a log line and an incident
  - **Suppression is deliberately not folded into `raise_alert`.** Five families — anomalies, stalls, agent failures, unit failures, collation — already own their own lifecycles, and changing all of them silently from one override is the "second owner of one lifecycle" defect at a sixth scale, which is the defect that produced this fix in the first place
  - **Every side effect had to be moved above the raise decision**, and `_threshold_keys` was the one that would have broken quietly: `_check_anomalies` reads it to suppress a duplicate anomaly for a resource that already has a threshold row, so a suppressed raise that also skipped the `add` would have fired an anomaly alert for a resource whose threshold row was open. The restart `systemctl` call, `_degraded_counts` and `_failure_counts` are all likewise unaffected by suppression
  - **The stated reason `collation` stays out of the sweep was itself wrong, and was corrected in passing.** The docstring said mutual exclusion; the actual reason is that collation resolves its own rows by id and contributes nothing to the judged set, so a sweep from here would be a second owner. The old sentence was a correct conclusion drawn from a premise that has now moved — the dangerous kind, because the next person removes the guard when the premise changes and reintroduces the bug the real reason was preventing
  - **Verified against the live database on 2026-08-14, in a transaction that was rolled back** — necessary because the unit tests stand in for PostgreSQL's `LIKE` with a Python matcher and so cannot prove that the patterns plus `NOT IN (judged)` select the right rows in real SQL. Run 1 wrote; run 2 with the same fault wrote nothing and resolved nothing (the flip-flop case); run 3 resolved on clearing; run 4 re-raised; ten sustained runs of one threshold fault left **1** row where the old code wrote 10; three auto-restarts left 3. Residue after rollback: 0
  - **Two changes of meaning, stated rather than discovered later.** `alerts_raised` in `agent_runs` now reads 0 on a run where a sustained fault is still true — that is its intended meaning (rows written), but anything trending it over time will show a step change at deploy, so `details['standing']` carries `judged` and `suppressed` beside it. And a `critical` that fires once now genuinely fires once: the volume that used to mask the absence of escalation is gone, which makes these families the same shape as `SNAG-ESTATE-003`

- [P3] SNAG-AGENT-007: **`_active_alerts` is now read four times per sysadmin run, and it loads whole ORM rows** (2026-08-14, **fixed 2026-08-27**)
  - **Symptom**: `SysAdminAgent._active_alerts` issues `SELECT Alert WHERE agent='sysadmin' AND resolved IS false` and materialises full ORM objects. It is called by `_check_anomalies`, `_check_agent_health` and `_check_collation`, and since SNAG-AGENT-006 also once at the top of `_execute` for the dedup snapshot. Four unbounded reads of the alerts table per 300-second run
  - **Why it is P3 and not higher**: it was already three before this change, and the fourth caller is the one *shrinking* the table it reads. The query is also bounded in practice by the same fix — the row counts that made this dangerous (598,091 for the log family, 51,924 for services) are exactly what dedup and the set-based resolve now prevent
  - **Why it is worth filing anyway**: this is the `SNAG-AGENT-005` shape. `_open_alerts` there was first written as "every unresolved row this agent owns" and pulled **593,814 ORM objects** on its first live run — the fix falling over on the backlog it existed to end. Nothing structurally prevents a repeat here; what prevents it today is that the table is small, which is a state rather than a guarantee
  - **The fix is not free, which is why it was not taken mid-change**: the dedup caller needs only titles, so a `select(Alert.title)` projection would fix it — at the cost of a second definition of "this agent's open rows" sitting beside `_active_alerts`, which is the copy-drift `sysadmin/projects/snapshots.py` exists to argue against. The other three callers genuinely need the rows (id, severity, created_at, details) to run their ladders off, and must see what the run has already written, so they cannot share the `_execute` snapshot
  - **Found**: 2026-08-14 while fixing SNAG-AGENT-006, and deliberately left — splitting a shared query in the same sitting as a lifecycle change would have made both harder to verify
  - **Fixed 2026-08-27 (Session 105). Four reads is what the entry counted and the count was never the cost.** It ranked itself P3 on "the table is small", and the table is **666,936 rows** — what is small is the *result*. `alerts` has carried `idx_alerts_active … WHERE resolved = FALSE` since it was created, and no reader could reach it: `Alert.resolved.is_(False)` renders `resolved IS false`, PostgreSQL matches a partial index **structurally**, and a `BooleanTest` is not an `OpExpr`. Driven against the live table with zero open `sysadmin` rows, the read the entry describes is a **parallel sequential scan: 41,644 buffers, 33.3 ms**, four times per 300-second run. The same query spelled `= false` is an index scan of **13 buffers and 0.03 ms**. So the entry costed the read by what it returns and the cost was in what it touches — which is why nothing it proposed would have moved the number
  - **The index and the readers that could not reach it were eight lines apart in one file**, which is the part worth carrying. This is not a missing index and it is not a query someone wrote carelessly; it is one fact stated in two languages — a `text()` fragment PostgreSQL parses and a Python expression SQLAlchemy renders — with nothing holding them together. `SNAG-DB-003`'s shape in a spelling rather than in a copied dict
  - **The tension the entry names is real and dissolves once the two halves are named apart.** It says a `select(Alert.title)` projection for the dedup caller would cost "a second definition of *this agent's open rows*". A **projection** is what a caller wants back; a **predicate** is which rows it is asking about. Only the second is a definition. `unresolved()` on the `Alert` model is the one statement of *open* — beside the column and the two partial indexes, for `STATUS_READINGS`' reason — and `SysAdminAgent._open_alert_criteria` composes the agent scope on top, so `_active_alerts` and the new `_open_alert_titles` differ in projection and cannot differ in population
  - **The population was nineteen, not four.** Every reader in the repository spelled the predicate by hand — `core/agent.py`, `core/unit_failure.py`, `briefing/`, `estate/agent.py`, `units/agent.py` (five), `monitor/desktop.py`, `monitor/log_aggregator.py`, the sysadmin router, `snag_claims.py` — so fixing the four this entry names would have left fifteen sequential scans behind and a fresh copy of the definition beside them. The substitution is **provably** meaning-preserving rather than safe-looking: `IS false` and `= false` differ on exactly one input and `resolved` is `NOT NULL`, which is asserted against the column rather than remembered
  - **Migration 017 is the second half and would have been a no-op alone.** `idx_alerts_active` bounds the read by the *whole* open set rather than by the asking agent's share of it, which is this entry's own "nothing structurally prevents a repeat" stated as a plan: driven with 100,000 open `log_aggregator` rows inserted in a rolled-back transaction — `SNAG-AGENT-005` reached 598,091 — the read scanned **100,001 index entries** to return zero rows (1,935 buffers, 6.7 ms), and **2 buffers, 0.02 ms** with `idx_alerts_open_by_agent`. Run at that same storm with the index present and the spelling unchanged, it seq-scanned exactly as before: **41,644 buffers, 31.4 ms**. The obvious reading of a performance snag is that the database is missing something, and here the database was missing nothing anybody could use
  - **Today the planner still prefers the older index** — one open row, so either is free — and the new one is insurance that engages precisely when the entry's worry materialises. Stated rather than implied, because an index nothing chooses looks identical to an index that does not work
  - **A test was pinning the defect.** `test_it_is_scoped_to_this_agents_unresolved_rows` asserted the string `alerts.resolved IS false` and was green for the life of the module — `TestJournalCommand`'s defect in a second family. It composes the assertion from `unresolved()` now, so it pins *provenance* (the resolve uses the shared predicate) while the new file pins the *value* (that predicate renders the index's own string): the split this repository has been bitten by three times, applied deliberately
  - **`tests/test_alert_dedup.py`'s stand-in could not tell a projection from a row read** and answered `select(Alert.title)` with `Alert` objects, so the fix arrived as four red dedup tests — the exact defect they exist to catch, wearing the fix's clothes. It discriminates on `selected_columns` now, modelling the database rather than the one call site that happens to project
  - **Fix**: `sysadmin/core/models/alert.py` (`unresolved`, `idx_alerts_open_by_agent`), `alembic/versions/017_add_open_alerts_by_agent_index.py` (applied 2026-08-27, daemon restarted 21:00:56), `sysadmin/monitor/agent.py` (`_open_alert_criteria`, `_open_alert_titles`), fifteen call sites across five domains, `tests/test_open_alert_predicate.py` (12 tests, **ten falsifications each firing on the test that names it** — one of which passed against deliberately broken code, the seventh here, because it asserted a statement the test itself had written rather than asking the producer)
  - **The check leaves the registry with the entry**, and its own wording predicted this fix: *"a third is the projection it asks for"*. `method_calls` goes with it, having no other caller

- [P2] SNAG-PORT-001: **estate-manager widened `audited_ranges` and this repository's port family did not, so two ports the estate now governs are unwatched here** (2026-08-27, opened by Session 105, **FIXED 2026-08-27 by Session 106**)
  - **Symptom**: `audit.ports.audited_ranges` gained `[1000, 1999]` in estate-manager's `e5c639c` on 2026-08-27, taking their band to `[(1000,1999), (3000,3999), (8000,8999)]`. `PortCheckConfig.audited_ranges` here still reads the old pair, so `sysadmin/units/ports.py`'s four comparisons — `wrong_unit`, `port_shared`, `duplicate_claim`, `wrong_project` — do not look at anything below 3000
  - **The duplication is deliberate and it worked.** `PortCheckConfig` restates the estate's list rather than importing it, for `EstateJudgeConfig.base_url`'s reason: a cross-repository lookup goes silently quiet the day the other side reorganises its config. `tests/test_unit_ports.py` is the reader that makes the copy honest, and both of its cross-repo tests went red on a clean tree the same day. This entry exists because the mechanism fired, not because it failed
  - **Live population is two, measured rather than assumed**: the registry now claims **1716** (`kdeconnectd`, a session-scope listener) and **1883** (mosquitto — estate-owned shared infrastructure, ADR-0002/0003). Neither is inside our band, so neither can produce a finding here today. 22000 is claimed and outside every band on both sides, which is a separate question and is the estate's
  - **Why it is not fixed in the sitting that found it**: widening the band changes what an *alert family* raises, and 1883's socket is root-owned so `ss` will not name its holder — an unattributed listener inside an audited range is precisely the case `ports_checked` and `SNAG-ESTATE-009` are about. That needs driving live against the real `ss` and the real registry, which is a sitting, not a constant. Bundling it into `SNAG-AGENT-007` would have made both harder to verify, which is the reason that entry was itself deferred in 2026-08-14
  - **Filed at the owner rather than absorbed**: cross-repo message `9022e358` to estate-manager, carrying the cost — the suite red on a clean tree, and ~25 minutes of an unrelated sitting spent establishing the red was theirs before any of it could be trusted. It is a message and not a finding: they are not at fault for widening their own jurisdiction, and the fix is ours
  - **Found**: 2026-08-27 by Session 105, running the full suite before touching the documents

  - **FIXED 2026-08-27 (Session 106), and the entry's own account of the blast radius was wrong in the same way estate-manager's was.** It says the widening reaches *"`sysadmin/units/ports.py`'s four comparisons — `wrong_unit`, `port_shared`, `duplicate_claim`, `wrong_project`"*. `in_range` has **two** production call sites, and only one of them is a comparison: `judge_ports`' `wrong_project` gate, and `PortReport.unit_ports(audited_only=True)`. The other three comparisons never consult the band at all — `port_shared` and `wrong_unit` judge whatever `ss` attributes, and `duplicate_claim` judges whatever the table claims twice. So the same overstatement was made independently on both sides of the same copy, and the other side had already caught and corrected theirs the same day (their ADR-0054 §5 → their `SNAG-ESTATE-070`, *"`audited_ranges` gates only `unclaimed_listener`"*). Reading the entry finds the ranges; grepping the callers ranks them
  - **What the widening actually changes here, measured live rather than argued**: `ServiceDiscoveryAgent._check_ports` was driven through its own code path against the real `ss`, the real `services.yaml` and the real registry document, at the old band and the new one. **Findings 0 → 0, collisions 0 → 0, advice 0 → 0.** The one observable difference is `unit_audited_ports`, 12 units → 13, gaining the holder of 1716. So the entry's stated reason for deferring — *"widening the band changes what an alert family raises"* — is true in principle and had an **empty population** on the day it was written, which is `SNAG-LOG-002`'s shape and is why the drive had to happen before the constant could be trusted rather than after
  - **1883 changes nothing and could not have, which is the half worth keeping.** Its socket is root-owned, so `ss -p` names no holder, so it is `unattributed` — and `judge_ports` compares an unattributed listener against nothing by construction (`collation.py`'s rule 1, this module's founding posture). Widening a band over a port nobody can attribute buys jurisdiction and no observation, which is exactly what `ports_checked` exists to keep distinguishable. `SNAG-ESTATE-009` is untouched: the estate judge reads the holder out of `unit_ports`, which was never gated on the band
  - **1716 is not a session scope, and that mattered.** The entry calls it *"`kdeconnectd`, a session-scope listener"*. It is a D-Bus activated **service** — `dbus-:1.2-org.kde.kdeconnect@0.service` — so `Listener.transient` (which tests for a `.scope` suffix) does not exclude it, and it lands in `unit_ports` like any unit. Establishing that is what turned up `SNAG-PORT-002` underneath it, and left `SNAG-PORT-003` behind
  - **The band now has three statements and all three are pinned, because one of them is what failed.** `PortCheckConfig.audited_ranges` is the production value; `ports.DEFAULT_AUDITED_RANGES` is the pure module's fallback, which may not read `config.yaml` and so cannot derive it (`syslog_priority` against `PRIORITY_MAP`'s treatment — import where you can, pin where you cannot); and `tests/test_unit_ports.py` compares both against estate-manager's. The self-pin is new and is not the same assertion as the cross-repo one: a widening applied to `config.py` alone would leave every bare `judge_ports` call in the suite judging the old band while production judged the new one, green in both places
  - **Their two halves are both read, because leaning on their test is the lookup this copy exists to avoid.** The check now parses `service/audit.yaml` — the block their running audit actually reads — as well as the `config.py` default it was reading before. Their own `test_the_shipped_ranges_and_the_code_default_agree` pins those together, and one repository's guard is not another repository's evidence. Empty population today (their two halves agree); witnessed by driving the test against doctored copies of their yaml in both directions, behind and ahead
  - **Fix**: `sysadmin/core/config.py` (`PortCheckConfig.audited_ranges`), `sysadmin/units/ports.py` (`DEFAULT_AUDITED_RANGES`, and `judge_ports`' default now naming it), `tests/test_unit_ports.py` (both red tests rewritten, plus `test_the_pure_default_matches_the_config_default`). The live-document test now reads the band from config rather than restating it, so it can no longer drift from the constant independently — the same defect one repository smaller. Suite 2732 → 2737, all green; daemon restarted 22:26:57, `/health` 200
  - **The stored sweep still carries the old band and that is normal operation, not a residue.** `service_discovery` runs six-hourly and the sweep in `unit_audits` was written 20:02, before the restart; the next one picks the widening up. `ports_checked` reporting `false` on a pre-26c sweep is the same fact and is already documented as correct


- [P2] SNAG-PORT-002: **the cgroup path was read from its last colon, so every D-Bus activated unit was attributed to the wrong scope** (2026-08-27, opened by Session 106, **FIXED 2026-08-27** the same sitting)
  - **Symptom**: `_unit_from_cgroup` took `line.rpartition(":")[2]` as the cgroup path. `cgroup(5)` is `hierarchy-ID:controller-list:cgroup-path` and only the first two fields are colon-free — the *path* may hold as many as it likes, because systemd escapes a unit name's `/` and leaves its `:` alone. Measured live, `kdeconnectd` sits at `…/user@1000.service/app.slice/app-dbus\x2d:1.2\x2dorg.kde.kdeconnect.slice/dbus-:1.2-org.kde.kdeconnect@0.service`, and taking the last field returned `1.2-org.kde.kdeconnect@0.service`
  - **Two things go wrong and the second is the load-bearing one.** The unit name loses its `dbus-` prefix — visible, and merely wrong. With it goes the whole `/user@1000.service/` prefix, so the very next line (`SCOPE_USER if "/user@" in path`) cannot see it and stamps a **user** unit `system`. This module's own docstring says scope is part of a unit's identity and `services.yaml` keys on it; `wrong_unit` compares `f"{scope}:{unit}"`, so a mis-scoped listener is a different unit as far as the alert family is concerned
  - **Population, measured rather than assumed**: **1 of 30** attributed listeners on this box, and **4 of 694** processes have a colon inside the cgroup path. Small, and it is precisely the listener `SNAG-PORT-001` brought into jurisdiction — which is why a parse that had been wrong since Session 26c became visible on the day the band moved rather than on any of the ninety before it
  - **Found**: 2026-08-27 by Session 106, by driving `ServiceDiscoveryAgent._check_ports` live at the new band and reading the attribution back. Not by the tests: every existing cgroup test uses a hand-written fixture, and nobody had typed a path with a colon in it — `test_excludes_health_endpoint`'s defect in a fifth module
  - **No live consequence today, and the entry says so rather than dressing the fix up.** 1716 is not declared in `services.yaml`, so `wrong_unit` had nothing to compare; it holds no second unit, so `port_shared` was silent; and its holder matches no project on disk, so `wrong_project` returned before reaching the mis-scoped key either way. Findings were 0 before the fix and 0 after. What the fix buys is that the **next** such listener is compared against the right identity
  - **Fix**: `sysadmin/units/ports.py:_unit_from_cgroup` — `line.split(":", 2)[2]` behind a `line.count(":") >= 2` guard, so a truncated or v1-shaped line degrades to "no attribution" instead of raising `IndexError` inside the sweep. Four tests in `tests/test_unit_ports.py`
  - **The scope half needed its own witness.** The live specimen breaks the unit name and the scope *together*, so it cannot say which half a candidate fix repaired — a fix that stripped a `dbus-` prefix, or matched the unit with a regex, would pass it. `test_the_scope_half_is_witnessed_where_the_unit_name_survives` uses a shape where the colon is in the *slice* and not the leaf: `rpartition` then returns the right unit and loses only the scope. Three falsifications — the old parse fires two tests, the removed colon-count guard fires the third. A fourth stand-in (`split(":", 2)[-1]`) **passes, correctly**: `split` with `maxsplit=2` returns at most three fields, so behind the guard those two spellings are the same code and its passing is not a hole

- [P3] SNAG-AGENT-009: **a deduplicating family writes its message once and never refreshes it, so a standing row describes a state that has moved** (2026-08-28, opened by Session 108, **measured and decided 2026-08-28** by Session 109, **FIXED 2026-08-28** by Session 110)
  - **Symptom**: every family that deduplicates on an open title takes the `held` branch and `continue`s, leaving `alert.message` as whatever the *first* run wrote. `Alert.title` is the identity and must not move — that is settled — but the message is the sentence a reader acts on, and nothing keeps it true
  - **One family already solved this and the rule was never generalised.** `LogAggregatorAgent._record_recurrence` sets `alert.message = fault["message"][:500]` on a held row and reassigns `details` so `last_seen_at` moves — deliberately, because that family's resolve depends on it. *(The entry called it `log_aggregator._refresh`; no such symbol exists, and reading the real one is what showed the precedent reassigns **both** fields.)* `units/agent.py:_maintain_port_alerts` and `estate/agent.py:_execute` both `continue` past the row instead
  - **The mechanism is real in both named families, driven rather than argued.** Against the live database in a rolled-back transaction: `_maintain_port_alerts` raises naming `user:alpha.service`, then the same port is handed `user:beta.service` — `held: 1, raised: 0` and the message still names alpha. `EstateJudgeAgent._execute` with a score moving 42 → 31 and a streak 8 → 9 days under two held titles — `raised=0`, both messages unchanged. **`details` is frozen too**, which the entry does not say: the ports `findings` blob still carried alpha, so a fix that moves only `message` leaves half the row lying
