  - **Live population is 1 open row, and 0 of it is this entry.** The box holds exactly **one** unresolved alert. Its message *is* stale — `Weekly disk review ready`, open **270 h**, saying the disk "crosses 90% around 2026-08-23" five days after that date passed with the disk at 80%. It is **not this mechanism**: `files/review.py:553` writes it with a bare `session.add(Alert(...))`, no dedup and no held branch. Filed separately as `SNAG-AGENT-010`. Both families this entry names hold **zero** open rows
  - **The entry's two halves are not comparable, and one is empty all-time.** Held-branch events on this box: `_maintain_port_alerts` **0 in 65 runs**; `EstateJudgeAgent._execute` **131 across 77 of 240 runs**. Giving them equal billing is the framing defect `SNAG-PORT-003` was closed for, one entry later
  - **The largest population is a family the entry never mentions.** `SysAdminAgent._raise_judged` has suppressed **838** raises across 751 of 2,303 runs. Full table: threshold+service 838, collation 386, estate judge 131, armed orphans 5, ports 0 — **1,360 held events all-time**
  - **A held message goes stale iff it interpolates a quantity that moves while the fault stands**, and that predicate splits the 1,360 cleanly. *Immune*: collation (386) states two collation versions, a condition; the units roll-up puts its count in the **title**, so a state change mints a new row — twelve rows on this box, twelve different counts; the service family puts the status in the title likewise; an armed orphan's `finding.reason` is a condition. *Stale-prone*: the **threshold** family, whose title `High VRAM usage on …` is stable while its message is nothing but `VRAM at 90.1% (threshold: 90%)`; the **estate judge**, where **18 of 21** judgement templates interpolate an age, score, streak or count; and **ports**, with an empty population
  - **The drift is 100% of every held poll that could be measured.** Joining `resource_snapshots` to the held window of each of the 23 post-dedup `High VRAM usage` rows: **42 polls fell inside a hold and 42 carried a different figure** from the one frozen in the message, mean absolute drift **8.15 pp**, max **43.8 pp** — one row raised at 91.5% while the card ran 51.0–92.0%. **19 of the 42** read *below* the 90% threshold the message was asserting. The first attempt at this measurement reported 0 moved because the JSON path was wrong and `NULL <> x` is never true: a constant observation with no discriminating witness, caught by `min_seen` coming back empty
  - **Decision: keep `P3`, re-scope, and take candidate 1** — refresh when the text differs, `details` with it. `P3` survives because the consequence surface has an empty population *to date*: `sysadmin_tray/notifications.py:617` re-speaks the frozen message every `reminder_hours: 24` beside a live "Still open N hours", and **no dedup row has ever been held past 24 h with a figure-bearing message** — the four that outlived it are condition-messages or title-coupled. Order of work is `_raise_judged` (838), estate judge (131), ports (0, and say so)
  - **The other two candidates are refuted by the measurement, not out-ranked.** *Carry the state in `details` and leave `message` alone* fails twice: the drive shows `details` is frozen identically, so it is the same write and not a cheaper one, and **3 of 3 tray render sites read `message`** (`notifications.py:478, 517, 617`) plus the briefing envelope (`briefing/router.py:102`) while none reads `details` — the current state would land where nothing looks. *Say in the docstring that a message is a first sighting* cannot survive a sentence being **republished as current every 24 h**, and reaches the threshold family not at all, whose message has no content but the measurement
  - **The objection against candidate 1 does not transfer, and that is the number the entry was missing.** `SNAG-AGENT-006` was about `INSERT`s accumulating rows — 60 for one dead timer in five hours. This is an `UPDATE` to a row that already exists: it accumulates nothing, and its ceiling is the held-event rate, **1,360 across ~5,200 runs ≈ 0.26 per run**, lower again once gated on the text actually differing
  - **Not**: an in-place *severity* change, which Session 39 banned for the reason that it keeps a fingerprint the tray has already suppressed. A message change is invisible to `{severity}:{title}` and so is safe in the direction that rule is about — but it is also, for the same reason, never *announced*, which is half the argument for not bothering
  - **Fixed 2026-08-28 by Session 110, in the order the measurement ranked**: `SysAdminAgent._raise_judged` (838), `EstateJudgeAgent._execute` (131), `_maintain_port_alerts` (0, and its docstring now says so). `BaseAgent.refresh_alert` owns the comparison and the write; **finding the row stays with each caller**, because the three reach it three different ways for reasons of their own — the estate judge already holds the ORM rows, the port family bounds its read by a title prefix, and the sysadmin agent keeps the title-only snapshot `SNAG-AGENT-007` gave it. A base class taking a *title* would own a predicate its subclasses state three ways
  - **The 838 needed a read the snapshot deliberately cannot supply, and lazily is the right answer rather than the cheap one.** Widening `_open_alert_titles` to carry `message` and `details` is bounded by *the table* — this agent's own families reached 51,924 open rows before `SNAG-AGENT-004` — while a read at the hold is bounded by *the judgements the run made*. Live at 665,937 rows it is **84 buffers and 0.098 ms**, and it lands on `idx_alerts_active` rather than the agent-scoped index the first draft of the docstring claimed: one open row makes either partial index free and the planner takes the older one, which is what `SNAG-AGENT-007` recorded about its own pair. Reading the code would have shipped the wrong index name; `EXPLAIN` is what corrected it
  - **`details` is compared through a JSON round trip, and without that the gate is decorative.** `JSONB` has no tuple: a caller building `details` with one gets a list back next run, so a plain `!=` reports a difference no write can settle and every held poll rewrites the row for ever — the gate switched off by a type, with the counter reporting corrections that corrected nothing. What is *stored* is still the caller's dict, so a raise and a refresh handed one input write one row
  - **`_written_titles` is a second set, and the entry did not name the case that needs it.** `_open_titles` gained the titles this run raised, so that two identically-named GPUs dedup against the row just written. Refreshing on that branch would make the *last* of two same-titled judgements overwrite the first, and which of the two is current is undefined — so only a row open **before this run** is refreshed, and the run does not answer that question twice
  - **The gate is a floor, not a promise of quiet.** For a family whose `details` is a live measurement — the service family carries the check's own response time — every held poll differs and every held poll writes. Bounded anyway at the held rate, ~0.26 per run, and it is an `UPDATE` to a row that already exists: `SNAG-AGENT-006`'s objection was about `INSERT`s accumulating and does not transfer
  - **It half-closes `SNAG-ESTATE-010`, which that entry's own check said out loud.** Driven live, the standing row's `details['holder']` now arrives while its severity stays `warning` — the third of the three shapes that check enumerates. The clause had to come out of `QuietenReading.reached` rather than the verdict being accepted: a `reached` still reading the blob answers `mismatch` whatever happens to the rung, which is a control this fix broke. The falsification asserting the blob alone was a mismatch is **kept and inverted**, so the day it stops arriving this says so
  - **Verified live rather than only against fixtures**, through the real `_raise_judged` against the real database in a rolled-back transaction: a row raised at `VRAM at 91.5%` judged again at 51.0% gives `raised=0`, **one** row, message and `details` both moved, `suppressed=1 / refreshed=1`, a second identical judgement `refreshed=0`, and **0 rows of residue**. Suite 2750 → 2764 with 14 added; every one of the five falsifications fires — the refresh made a no-op (7 tests), the gate removed (7), the JSON round trip removed (1), `details` left frozen (7), and the two sets merged (1)
  - **The stand-ins were what had to change, in four files.** `tests/test_unit_ports.py` answered the dedup read with *titles*; `tests/test_estate_judge_agent.py`'s `FakeAlert` had no `message`; `tests/test_alert_dedup.py`'s fake ignored the WHERE clause and could not answer `.first()`; and `conftest.mock_session`'s bare `AsyncMock` returned a coroutine from `.scalars()`, failing in a way no database does. `SNAG-ESTATE-010`'s own probe located its standing row by `message == PROBE_MESSAGE` — the value this fix rewrites — so it now keys on the title, which is the identity the entry it checks turns on
  - **Found**: 2026-08-28 by Session 108, by measuring what `SNAG-PORT-003` actually predicted rather than repeating it. **Population measured and remedy decided** 2026-08-28 by Session 109. **Fixed** 2026-08-28 by Session 110
  - **Check**: none, and deliberately not written now that the fix has landed. A check here would assert that a held row *is* refreshed, which is the fix's own tests at `tests/test_alert_refresh.py` and in the three callers' files — not a claim about the box that goes stale between sittings. What does watch the box is `SNAG-ESTATE-010`'s check, whose witness half now depends on this fix running: `test_the_blob_arrives_without_any_stand_in_at_all` fails if it is reverted

- [P2] SNAG-AGENT-010: **the weekly review alert is raised by nothing that can resolve it, so the only open row on this box is immortal and already wrong** (2026-08-28, opened by Session 109, **FIXED 2026-08-28** by Session 111)
  - **Symptom**: `Weekly disk review ready` has been open **270 hours** and says the disk "crosses 90% around 2026-08-23" — five days past, against a disk at 80% that did not cross. It is the **only** unresolved alert on the box, so every count built on `resolved = false` is this row
  - **Nothing can close it, and three mechanisms each miss for their own reason.** `files/review.py:553` writes it with a bare `session.add(Alert(...))` — no dedup, no lifecycle. Its title matches no `RESOLVABLE_TITLE_PATTERNS` entry. And `_resolve_recovered` scopes on `Alert.agent == self.name`, while the row's agent is `file_organiser` — the same scoping that makes the sysadmin agent's sweep safe is what puts this row out of its reach
  - **Retention cannot reach it either**, so "immortal" is literal rather than rhetorical: `core/retention.py:118` purges `alerts` with `WHERE created_at < :cutoff AND resolved = TRUE`. An unresolved row is never deleted at any age
  - **Distinct from `SNAG-AGENT-009` and found while measuring it.** That entry is about a *held* row's message going stale; this row was never held, because the family has no dedup branch to hold it. Same visible symptom, opposite mechanism — which is why the population measurement had to separate them rather than count them together
  - **The obvious fix is a lifecycle, not a wording change.** A review-ready notice is an *event* — `_raise_judged`'s `dedup=False` case, `% auto-restarted`'s shape — so the candidates are: resolve the previous notice when the next review is generated, or stop using an alert row for what is a notification and let the tray read `/api/files/review` directly. Not decided here
  - **Decided 2026-08-28 (Session 111): the row goes.** The second candidate, and the entry's own framing of it was the one thing wrong with it — it proposed *"let the tray read `/api/files/review`"*, which is a tray feature that does not exist and did not need to. `sysadmin_tray/` contains **no** reference to any review endpoint, and the consumer that actually mattered is `briefing/data.py`, whose `_gather` reads `disk_reviews`, `log_reviews` **and** `health_reviews` directly and has done all along. So the fix is a deletion with nothing to build behind it
  - **The population was three writers, not one, which is the entry's scoping wrong in the direction this repository keeps finding.** `files/review.py:553` is named; `monitor/log_review.py:575` and `monitor/health_review.py:1055` write the identical bare `session.add(Alert(...))` at `info`. The symptom was one row because only the disk review's scheduler path has ever fired — `disk_reviews` holds 4 rows, three of them the manual `POST` route on 2026-08-06 which never announced, and `log_reviews`/`health_reviews` hold one apiece, both generated by hand. Grepping from the symptom finds one writer; grepping the *shape* finds three
  - **The first candidate would not have closed the observed row, which is what ruled it out rather than taste.** "Resolve the previous notice when the next review is generated" anchors the lifecycle to the next run. The row was written 2026-08-17 05:45 and the next generation was due **08-24 05:45, when the daemon was down** — the first `agent_runs` row that day is 07:00. So under that fix the row is open today at 271 h with the same wrong sentence: a lifecycle bounded by the thing that already failed
  - **Neither speaker on this box could ever have said it.** `tray.notify_min_severity` and `notifications.desktop.min_severity` both read `warning`; the row is `info`. This is not an announcement that was being missed — it is one that was structurally inaudible, so deleting it costs no notification that was ever delivered
  - **The clinching observation is one payload disagreeing with itself, and it is live.** `briefing/data.py` expires a review older than `_REVIEW_FRESH_DAYS` (8). Measured 2026-08-28, `GET /api/sysadmin/briefing/preview` rendered `['Infrastructure Status', 'Overnight Logs', 'Filesystem', 'Weekly Log Review', 'Weekly System Health Review']` — **no Weekly Disk Review, dropped as stale at 11 days** — while the same envelope's alert digest carried `Weekly disk review ready`. The briefing declined to show the review and went on announcing it. A second owner of one announcement, with only one of the two owners holding a freshness rule
  - **A notice was being counted as a fault by this repository's own review.** `health_review._gather_alerts` takes **no severity filter** across its four queries, and `build_review_prompt` phrases the figure as *"Distinct faults alerted: N this period against M last"*. So each week's three notices would be narrated as three faults — and the health review's own notice lands in the **next** health review's `new_titles`. The repo's fixture had already normalised it: `tests/test_health_review.py` used `"new_titles": ["Weekly disk review ready"]` as its example of a fault. Filtering the query was refused as the fix: it leaves three immortal rows on the box and teaches the count to ignore a severity other families use legitimately (`known_noise`'s quietening, `COVERED_SIGNATURES`)
  - **Nothing consumed the payload either.** `details['review_id']` and `details['endpoint']` are written by three modules here and read by none — `SNAG-CFG-001`'s shape, three times over, and the check worth running before adding any field to an alert nobody reads
  - **The suite was pinning the immortality as intended behaviour**, which is why this was found by counting the table and not by a red test. `tests/test_health_review.py::test_the_announcement_escapes_the_sysadmin_resolve_sweep` asserted the title matched no `RESOLVABLE_TITLE_PATTERNS` entry, reasoning that an announcement filed under `sysadmin` was *"one wrong suffix away from being resolved by the next health check"* — escaping the sweep read as the property to protect. Read the other way it is the defect, since retention deletes an `alerts` row only when it is resolved. Both that test and its `chk_alert_agent` sibling retire with the write
  - **Fix**: the `session.add(Alert(...))` removed from all three `run_weekly_review` functions, the `Alert` import dropped from `files/review.py` and `monitor/log_review.py` (`health_review` keeps it — four alert-delta queries), and the ruling argued once in `files/review.py` with the other two citing it. `tests/test_review_announcements.py` is the guard, 9 tests over all three modules
  - **The guard is two halves that fail apart, and the separation was falsified rather than asserted.** Behaviour (the scheduler path adds nothing to the session) and provenance (no `Alert(...)` constructed in `run_weekly_review`). Neither substitutes: driven at a stand-in that **builds the row and never adds it**, only the provenance half fires — and a behavioural test alone would pass. The provenance half is an `ast` walk rather than a grep because `health_review` legitimately imports `Alert`; a name in an import list is not a caller
  - **Verified live through the real commit path, which the fixtures cannot reach.** All three `run_weekly_review` functions driven against the real database with `generate_review` stubbed and the real `get_scheduler_session`: `alerts` at **665,937 before and after, delta 0 for each of the three**, **0 rows of residue**, **0 open rows**. The standing row was resolved by hand — nothing in the code can reach it now that the writer is gone — which also makes it retention-reachable for the first time, so it purges around 2026-09-16
  - **The daemon was restarted**, because the fix is inert without it and the next firing is Monday 05:00: PID 3684053 → 3716817 at **2026-08-28 13:41:23**, `/health` 200 within 2 s. Schema checked at head 017 first, `SNAG-DB-005`'s rule
  - **Found**: 2026-08-28 by Session 109, while counting `SNAG-AGENT-009`'s population — the row that answered "how many open rows carry a message that no longer describes them" turned out not to be a member of the family being counted
  - **Check**: none, and deliberately not written. A check here would assert that the three modules *do not* write a row, which is exactly what `tests/test_review_announcements.py` asserts in the suite — a fact about this checkout, not a claim about the box that can go stale between sittings. The box-facing half is `ops_claims`' `check_alerts`, which caught the fall from 1 to 0 unresolved rows the moment it happened and is the surface `SNAG-ESTATE-008` exists for

- [P3] SNAG-PORT-003: **a per-launch unit name is not a stable identity, and `transient` recognised only the one shape it was written against** (2026-08-27, opened by Session 106, **FIXED 2026-08-28** by Session 108)
  - **Symptom**: `Listener.transient` tested for a `.scope` suffix, which catches `app-code-oss-112152.scope` — the editor dev servers Session 57 built it for. Every other unit systemd creates at runtime ends in `.service` and carries a per-launch id: a bus-unique connection name (`dbus-:1.2-org.kde.kdeconnect@0.service`) or a 32-hex instance (`app-steam@455b2e51e70244d98b817b19364641d8.service`). Both change on every launch, which is exactly the property `transient` exists to catch and is stated in its own docstring
  - **The entry's own framing was the defect's smaller half, and the population instrument said so.** It was filed as a *D-Bus* fault with a population of one and both obvious fixes rejected as guesses — *"what is missing is a second instance to tell a rule from a coincidence, and this box does not have one"*. `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/systemd/transient` holds **eight** runtime-created services: three bus-named and **five** `app-*@<32-hex>` ones carrying no colon at all. So the second instance existed on the day the entry was written, and it was a *different shape* — which is what makes a rule tuned to the first the coincidence
  - **Both fixes the entry named are refuted by that count, and by which units were listening.** A `dbus-` prefix reaches 3 of the 8; the `:N.N` pattern reaches 2, because the box carries `dbus-:1.21-org.a11y.atspi.Registry@0.service` beside `:1.2` — the entry's own spelling of the rejected pattern, refuted by the box. Both leave the two `app-*@<id>` units that were **holding ports while the entry claimed a population of one**: `app-steam@…` on 27036/34223/46847/57343 and `app-appimagekit_…@…` on 36577
  - **No name rule could have worked, which is the durable half.** `app-steam@455b2e51….service` and `syncthing@gaddi.service` are the same shape — `name@instance.service` — and separating them means deciding a 32-hex instance is special. That is `systemd-run`'s convention, not a guarantee: the reaching-into-a-format-someone-else-owns objection the entry raised against `:N.N`, met from the other side. The name does not carry the fact
  - **Fix**: systemd's own answer, which the entry did not consider. `sysadmin/units/ports.py:runtime_unit_names` lists `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/systemd/transient` and `/run/systemd/transient` — a directory listing, the same class of signal `discover_units` already reads for enablement, so no second subprocess beside `ss` and no guess. `observe_listeners` stamps `Listener.runtime_created` from it and `transient` is the union of that and the old suffix test
  - **Added to the suffix rule and never substituted for it, on one measured witness.** The obvious reading of *"ask systemd instead of guessing"* is to replace the suffix test with the listing. `init.scope` refutes it: `systemctl show init.scope -p Transient` reads **yes** from both managers and it appears in **neither** directory, so the listing is a proxy that under-reports. Additive cannot subtract — `ops_claims` rule 1's posture — and the union is exactly complete here: the suffix catches `init.scope` and the two app scopes, the listing catches the eight services, and together they are every `Transient=yes` unit observed
  - **Stamped at observation, not derived in the property**, so `judge_ports` stays pure below `observe_listeners` — the promise this module makes in its first paragraph. Deriving it in the property would make an in-memory value's attribute depend on the filesystem at access time and let one listener answer two ways at two moments. Nothing in production rebuilds a `Listener` from storage (`observe_listeners` is the only constructor), so the stamp cannot go stale in a consumer
  - **Scope routes the directory, and that is not hypothetical**: `/run/systemd/transient` held `dbus-:1.2-org.kde.kameleon.qmk.helper@0.service` on 2026-08-28, so a reader merging the two would call a user unit transient on the system manager's say-so
  - **The names need no unescaping**, which was the obvious worry and is measured false: systemd writes the escaped name as the filename and the same escaped name into the cgroup path, `\x2d` and all. Of 14 attributed user listeners exactly 4 match a transient file and they are exactly the four per-launch holders; the ten hand-written services match nothing
  - **An unreadable directory degrades to the suffix rule** — the *noisy* direction, chosen deliberately. Claiming transience on a failed read would suppress a genuine collision on the strength of not having looked, which is the estate judge's rule 2
  - **The entry's predicted failure mode was wrong, and the smaller fix it implies was refused.** It predicted *"a new title every login"* — `SNAG-AGENT-005` in miniature. `port_alert_title` is `Port collision on 1716`, keyed on the **port**, so the row deduplicates and what churns is the message. The real fault was therefore staleness, not a pile-up: a standing row naming a unit nobody can find. That reading makes the entry *less* urgent and does **not** make the fix smaller — a message-refresh addresses one of the eight units by accident and none of them correctly, and the right answer for a per-launch holder is Session 57's, to route it to `transient_ports` and quieten it rather than to describe it better. Fixing `transient` closes the staleness for this family outright, because no such finding is raised at all. What the reading *did* leave behind is `SNAG-PORT-004`, which is the general form and is not about ports
  - **Live either side, driven through the production path.** `unit_ports(audited_only=True)` goes **13 keys → 12**: `user:dbus-:1.2-org.kde.kdeconnect@0.service` leaves it and joins `transient_ports` with 1716, alongside the steam, appimagekit and code-oss holders. All 14 hand-written services are unmoved and findings are **0 → 0**, so nothing raised or resolved — the fix buys that the estate judge now reads 1716's holder as `transient: True` and would judge a breach there at `TRANSIENT_HOLDER_SEVERITY` rather than `warning`, which is Session 57's rule re-arriving through a name shape
  - **The check retired with the entry** (`transient_misses_bus_name`, Session 107's twenty-sixth), per the registry's rule that a check outliving its entry re-measures a claim nobody reads. **It reported `match` against the landed fix, and that is a finding about the instrument.** It asked `Listener(port=0, unit=name).transient` — a *synthetic* listener — so it assumed transience is a function of the name. It was built to be neutral between the two fixes the entry named, and both were name-based, so it inherited their shared assumption; the fix that refutes both is invisible to it. Read off the *observed* listener the same property returns `True`. `a-control-a-fix-breaks-is-not-a-control`, found a third time in this class and the first time where the coupling came from the entry's own framing rather than from the check's construction
  - **Found**: 2026-08-27 by Session 106; judged and fixed 2026-08-28 by Session 108. 13 tests in `tests/test_unit_ports.py`, seven falsifications each firing on the test that names it — the old suffix-only rule breaks five, dropping the suffix breaks `init.scope`'s witness alone, merging the directories breaks the scope-routing one, `return True` breaks the negative controls, and re-reading per listener breaks the once-per-sweep guard

- [P2] SNAG-UNITS-004: **`GET /api/units/actions` emitted snippets that wire permanently-failing checks, and a removal command that leaves a timer behind** (2026-08-15, **fixed 2026-08-15**)
  - **Found by executing the advice, not by reading it.** Sessions 46, 47 and 26c made the diagnosis speak; Session 48 was the first to carry out what it says, and both defects surfaced inside an hour. One root cause: `recommendations.py` under-read a `UnitFinding` the sweep had already filled in
  - **Half 1 — the snippet never asked whether the unit is meant to run.** `grep -n "enabled" recommendations.py` returned *nothing*, while every finding carries a measured `enabled` that the orphan family has trusted since Session 46 for `armed`. Two of five `host` snippets named units that are **disabled and inactive** (`deadlock-api-ingest.service` system, and its updater timer). Pasting them declares `kind: systemd`/`kind: timer` checks — which assert *active* and *armed* — that return `critical` **every 300 s for ever**. Verified by running `_check_systemd`'s logic against both: `ActiveState=inactive → CRITICAL`. With `SNAG-AGENT-006` still open that is one row per run, the 12/hour that entry already measures as "60 for one dead timer in five hours". The pile-up Sessions 41–45 spent themselves deleting, arriving through this module's own remediation text
  - **The gate is "nothing enables it", not "it is not running"**, deliberately. `enabled` is an enablement symlink `scan.py` already walks and `classify_units` folds a oneshot's timer enablement into it, so a live schedule is never read as dormant — the sweep's no-subprocess promise survives, which it would not if the test were `ActiveState`. Measured 2026-08-15: the two `enabled=False` findings were both `inactive`, the three `enabled=True` all `active`, so the cheap signal and the sharp one agree on this box
  - **Half 2 — `removal_command` left a folded oneshot's timer installed.** `ticktick-sync.timer` declares `Requires=ticktick-sync.service`; the emitted command removed only the service. `monitor_unit` already named the timer, and the timer is the half carrying `[Install]` — so it holds the enablement symlink the command's own docstring exists to avoid orphaning. The next sweep cannot catch it either: a timer with no service is not a finding shape this module has. Now removed **timer first**, disarming the schedule before its service goes away
  - **The rule both halves imply**: an advice row offering no snippet must never say *"paste the snippet below"*. `sysadmin-failed.service` shipped exactly that — `snippet: ""` under paste instructions — an item an execution sitting **cannot close**, so it returns on every sweep for ever. `SNAG-ESTATE-001`'s roll-up defect wearing a single unit's name. Every no-snippet row now names its real next step; for a disabled unit that step is a fork (enable → next sweep emits a snippet, or remove)
  - **What the 20 failing tests were actually saying.** `UnitFinding.enabled` defaults to `False`, which is correct for `armed` — absent evidence must read as "not armed", the quiet direction — and the **opposite polarity** from this gate, where absent evidence suppresses advice, the loud direction. One field, two consumers, opposite safe defaults. Fixed in the fixtures, not the default: flipping the default would quietly arm every orphan
  - **Fix**: `sysadmin/units/recommendations.py` (`_snippet_for` gate, `_no_snippet_action`, `_enable_command`, `removal_command`), 7 tests in `tests/test_unit_recommendations.py`. Suite 1708 green

- [P2] SNAG-UNITS-005: **five system-scope orphans and a pending restart are blocked on `sudo`, which no agent session can supply** (2026-08-15, **durable half fixed 2026-08-15; ops half done by the owner and verified 2026-08-17 — closed**)
  - `offline-agents-dashboard`, `personalassistant-backend`, `personalassistant-frontend`, `ticktick-sync` (+ its timer) and `ticktick-sync-db` are all confirmed dead — the first, fourth and fifth point at directories that no longer exist; the two PersonalAssistant units were confirmed retired by the owner this sitting, `/opt/personalassistant` being leftover payload rather than a live project
  - The user-scope orphan (`garmin-sync.service`) **was** removed, so the emitted command is proven correct as written; only the privilege is missing. All six unit files are backed up
  - **The same wall blocks the deploy**: `services.yaml` is read at start-up, so Session 48's three new host entries are inert until `sudo systemctl restart sysadmin.service`. This is the second sitting in a row to hand a restart forward — worth a decision about whether the daemon should reload `services.yaml` on `SIGHUP`, which would remove the class of blocker rather than the instance
  - **Not a defect in the advice**: the emitted commands correctly say `sudo`, and refusing to run them unprivileged is right. It is filed because a recommendation nobody on the box can execute is indistinguishable, on the endpoint, from one nobody has got to
  - **The durable half is fixed (Session 49): `sysadmin/reload.py`**, reached by `SIGHUP` or `POST /api/sysadmin/reload`. The entry called this "worth a decision about whether the daemon should reload `services.yaml` on `SIGHUP`"; it was taken, and widened on measurement — every agent already calls `get_config()` inside `_execute`, so config.yaml's thresholds were re-read per run all along and only the scheduler's triggers, the engine, the socket and the logging setup are genuinely read once
  - **The privilege claim the entry rests on was checked rather than assumed.** `sysadmin.service` is a *system* unit but runs `User=gaddi`, so the owner may signal it without `sudo` — probed with `kill -0`, which tests permission without delivering. `systemctl reload` would need an `ExecReload=` line and *that* edit does need `sudo`, so the raw signal is the half that removes the blocker. Python's default `SIGHUP` action **terminates**, so a HUP sent to a daemon predating the handler is a restart wearing a reload's name, and the handler is asserted by a test rather than assumed
  - **Verified against the exact blocked instance**: with Session 48's three new entries removed to stand in for the running daemon, a reload of the real `services.yaml` reports them `added`, installs all three, and reports `requires_restart: []`. Had this existed on 2026-08-15 the restart would not have been owed
  - **The five orphan removals are untouched and still owed** — those are `systemctl disable` and `rm` under `/etc/systemd/system`, which no reload path can reach. The entry conflated a *deploy* blocker with an *ops* blocker; only the first is removed, and the second is not a defect in anything this repository ships
  - **Both halves are now done, and neither closure was recorded when it happened.** Verified 2026-08-17: `offline-agents-dashboard`, `personalassistant-backend`, `personalassistant-frontend`, `ticktick-sync` (+ timer) and `ticktick-sync-db` are **absent** from `/etc/systemd/system` and all report `not-found`; the restart landed **2026-08-16 12:06:09** (PID 1410826 → 1914354, `POST /api/sysadmin/reload` now 200). The bullet above went on saying "untouched and still owed" throughout, which is `SNAG-ESTATE-008` — this entry is one of its three instances, and the only one whose evidence was a single `systemctl is-enabled` call away

- [P3] SNAG-UNITS-001: **the unit sweep's snippet always says `kind: systemd`, so following its advice under-monitors every HTTP service** (2026-08-14, **fixed 2026-08-15**)
  - **Symptom**: `_services_yaml_snippet` emits `kind: systemd` for every non-timer unit it advises on, in both the `unmonitored` and `host` categories. A `kind: systemd` check asserts only that the unit is *active*. A backend that is running while every request 500s is active, healthy by this check, and broken — which is the failure an HTTP service is most likely to have and the only one a unit check structurally cannot see
  - **It is documented rather than accidental**: the docstring says *"`kind: systemd` rather than `http`: this scan does not know the unit's port"*, which is true and is the right floor for a **generic** generator. The scan reads unit files; a port lives in an `ExecStart` argument, a config file, an environment file or nowhere at all. Inventing one would produce advice that fails at load
  - **Why it is still a defect**: the advice is presented as a paste-ready snippet with no marker saying it is the weaker of two shapes. Nobody pasting it is told that a port would buy them a real check, so the sweep quietly converts every future HTTP service on this box into a liveness-only one. That is the `SNAG-SYSD-002` shape — a check that records less than the reader believes
  - **Found**: 2026-08-14 while pre-staging the SearXNG entry, which is the case that makes it concrete: SearXNG's port *will* be knowable (estate-manager's registry claims it before anything listens), so the generated snippet would be strictly worse than the hand-written one for a service whose port is a matter of record
  - **The fix is a comment, not a parser**: emit the `kind: systemd` line with `# no port known — use kind: http with a url if this serves one`, rather than attempting port extraction. Left unfixed here because the SearXNG session pre-staged its own entry and `tests/test_searxng_wiring.py` pins the shape for that one service; the general case needs its own sitting and a decision about whether `LISTEN`/`ExecStart` sniffing is worth the false positives
  - **Fixed 2026-08-15 (Session 26c), and the entry's own premise is what changed.** It reasoned that the comment was the only honest fix *because* "this scan does not know the unit's port" — true of the sweep, and no longer true of the sweep's siblings. `sysadmin/units/ports.py` reads `/proc/<pid>/cgroup` for every listener, so the snippet now emits `kind: http` with `url:` and `port:` whenever the unit holds **exactly one** port in the registry's audited range. Neither `LISTEN` nor `ExecStart` sniffing was needed, which is the alternative the entry was right to hesitate over — both would have guessed where the kernel can be asked
  - **The comment survived the fix rather than being replaced by it.** Where no port is attributed — nothing listening, a root-owned or containerised socket `ss` will not name, or two ports where picking one is a guess — the entry still says `kind: systemd` and still carries the marker, because the person pasting it is the one who knows what the unit serves
  - **What the fix does *not* buy, measured rather than assumed: it has an empty population today.** All 12 units holding an audited port on this box are `monitored`, and `classify_units` drops those before they become findings — the same two-thirds-invisible shape `SNAG-UNITS-002` hit. Driven as a counterfactual (pretending `alfred-backend.service` were unwired), the generated entry reproduces the hand-written one exactly: `url: http://localhost:8100/api/health`, `port: 8100`. So this is advice for the *next* service someone wires up, which is what the tier is for, and SearXNG was exactly that case
  - **The health path is still a guess, and it is now a snag of its own** (`SNAG-UNITS-003`). The sweep observes a port and never fetches

- [P2] SNAG-UNITS-002: **15 of the 18 units on this box that restart cannot ever reach `failed`, so no `OnFailure=` can fire for any of them** (2026-08-14, **fixed 2026-08-15**)
  - **Symptom, measured**: of 18 hand-written units declaring a `Restart=` policy, only `sysadmin.service`, `alfred-backend.service` and `alfred-frontend.service` have a start limit their restart cadence can actually reach. The other 15 include every live service on the box — `venture-assistant-backend`, `alfred-inference`, `estate-manager-api`, `venture-chat`, `venture-chat-large`, `venture-embed`, `sysadmin-tray`, both `deadlock-api-ingest` units, both `sportsanalyser` units
  - **Cause**: the failure is arithmetic, not an omission. Systemd's defaults are `StartLimitIntervalSec=10s` / `StartLimitBurst=5`, and every one of these units sets `RestartSec` to 5, 10 or 60 seconds. Five starts ten seconds apart span 40 seconds and the window is 10, so the limiter never fires, the unit stays in `activating (auto-restart)` for ever, `systemctl is-failed` reports nothing wrong, and any `OnFailure=` hook is unreachable
  - **This is the estate-wide form of `SNAG-ESTATE-001`.** That snag's two PersonalAssistant units restart-looped 52,178 times and stalled the kernel in exactly this state. Session 39 fixed the shape on `sysadmin.service` and the estate has no rule requiring the pairing on anything else — this is the first time the population has been counted rather than asserted
  - **Re-measured 2026-08-14 while wiring SearXNG: the population is now 17 of 20, not 15 of 18, and it grew the same day it was filed.** The two additions are `estate-manager-searxng-shim.service` and `estate-manager-searxng.service`, both `Restart=on-failure` with `RestartSec=5`/`10` against systemd's default 10-second window — the deploy did not do anything unusual, which is the point: **the defect is what a correctly-written unit gets by default on this box**, so the count rises with every service the estate adds. Filing it as a fixed list of 15 understates it as a standing rule. One mitigation worth recording rather than assuming: the shim is now checked `kind: http`, so a shim wedged in `activating (auto-restart)` is caught as `unreachable` by this service even though it can never reach `failed` — monitoring is the safety net for the units the ladder cannot see, which is an argument for the sweep's advice, not against this snag
  - **Found**: 2026-08-14, by `sysadmin/units/scan.py:restart_is_bounded` being written for SNAG-ESTATE-001's armed-orphan family and then pointed at the whole sweep
  - **Deliberately not alerted on**: the armed-orphan family raises one row per unit, and 15 rows on the first run is the pile-up shape wearing a new hat — it would train the reader to dismiss the family before it had said anything true. Two candidate fixes, neither costed: report it as a single rolled-up finding beside the existing one (cheap, and the roll-up is the surface SNAG-ESTATE-001 says nobody reads), or emit it as ranked advice under `GET /api/units/actions` with the `StartLimitIntervalSec=` line to paste (matches the tier's advice-only design, and the fix is genuinely one line per unit). The second needs a decision about whether a unit **this repository does not own** should be advised on at all
  - **What it does not claim**: the arithmetic assumes systemd's documented manager defaults, hard-coded in `scan.py` rather than read from `systemctl show` to keep that module pure. Confirmed against this box on 2026-08-14; a host overriding them in `system.conf` would make the check optimistic, which is the safe direction
  - **Fixed 2026-08-15 (Session 47) as advice, not alarm**, taking the entry's second candidate — a `restart` kind under `GET /api/units/actions` ranked second, above `unmonitored`, with the `StartLimitIntervalSec=` lines to paste. The open decision ("should a unit this repository does not own be advised on at all") was put to the owner and answered **yes, all of them**: the tier already advises on units it does not own — every `unmonitored`/`host` recommendation tells you to wire another project's unit — and the estate rule that bites is about *writing* into another repository, not about serving a pointer over a GET. The recommendation names the owning project so the reader knows whose edit it is
  - **The entry costed the fix as "one line per unit" and that was the part that was wrong.** Measured before writing anything: **11 of the 17 are `monitored`**, and `classify_units` drops monitored units before they become findings, so two thirds of the population had nothing to hang a line on. The fix needed a second pass over the same units (`restart_risk_findings`) — deliberately **not** a fifth category, because `units_scanned` is the sum of the four buckets and a monitored unit appearing twice would break the one arithmetic property `UnitScanSummary` exists to make auditable. It takes the shape `armed` already takes: a subset reported beside the sum, never inside it
  - **Orphans are excluded, which is the opposite of the obvious rule and shrinks the family to 13.** A broken unit that also loops reads like the worst case and belongs here twice over. It cannot: the orphan recommendation is *remove it*, and a start limit on a file you should delete is two contradictory instructions for one unit. Nothing is lost — an armed orphan that loops is exactly what `armed_alert_severity` already promotes to `critical`. So the live family is 11 monitored + 2 host, not 17
  - **No alert family, and the count is deliberately outside `actionable`.** `scan.actionable` is the roll-up alert's title *and* the number `alert_threshold` is compared against, so 13 latent risks added there would trip the threshold on their own and would read as 13 new units to wire up — two unrelated things behind one number. It rides in `details['restart_unbounded']` as evidence. The entry's first candidate (a rolled-up finding beside the existing one) was rejected for the reason it half-states itself: the roll-up is the surface `SNAG-ESTATE-001` proved nobody reads
  - **The remedy is checked against the detection, which is the trap this family was closest to.** `suggested_start_limit_interval` picks the smallest round window clearing `RestartSec x (burst - 1)` by 1.5x, and a test feeds every live shape back through `restart_is_bounded` requiring `True`; a second test appends the emitted snippet to a real unit file and re-scans, which is the only thing proving the two lines land in a section systemd reads them from. Advice that clears a symptom without fixing the fault is exactly what `ALTER DATABASE … REFRESH COLLATION VERSION` does in `SNAG-DB-002`, and it is cheap to build here by accident. Where **no** window would help — `RestartSec` beyond an hour, or `infinity` — it emits no snippet at all and says to lower `RestartSec` instead, because a number that does not work costs the family its reader
  - **The margin cuts the opposite way from the intuition, and the code says so.** A *longer* `StartLimitIntervalSec` is a *stricter* limiter: widening the window lets more starts fall inside it. Anyone "tightening" it to be safe would make the loop more immortal, which is why the direction has its own test
  - **`UnitFinding` now carries `restart_sec`, `start_limit_interval` and `start_limit_burst`.** Without them `restart_bounded` asks the reader to trust arithmetic they cannot see, and the naive version of that arithmetic is wrong in **both** directions — which is the entry's own best point, previously true only inside `restart_is_bounded`'s docstring
  - **What is still not fixed**: nothing re-checks a unit after the snippet is pasted, so the family clears on the next 6-hourly sweep and there is no "you fixed 3 of 13" signal; and the count will keep rising with every service the estate adds, because the defect remains what a correctly-written unit gets by default on this box. Neither is a defect in the fix — the first is the tier's advice-only design and the second is the standing rule this entry identified

- [P3] SNAG-UNITS-003: **the generated `kind: http` url guesses the health path, and on this box that guess is wrong more often than right** (2026-08-15)
  - **Symptom**: `_services_yaml_snippet` emits `url: http://localhost:<port>/api/health` from an observed port. The path is the contract's (`monitorable-project.md` §2.2 requires `GET /api/health`), not an observation — the sweep reads sockets and never sends a request
  - **Measured against every hand-written entry rather than sampled**: of the 11 `services.yaml` entries declaring a port and a unit, `/api/health` is correct for **4** (`alfred`, `venture-assistant`, `estate-manager-api`, `searxng`) and wrong for **7** — `llama-server`, `venture-chat` and `venture-embed` on `/health`, `sports_analyser` on `/api/v1/health`, `sysadmin-service` itself on `/health`, and both frontends with no path at all. The first draft of this entry said "two of twelve", from the two cases that happened to be in front of me; counting the file gave the opposite conclusion
  - **Kept anyway, and the reasoning is the entry's real content.** A wrong url fails **loudly** — the service reports `unreachable` within one 300 s poll of the reader pasting and restarting, and the fix is one line. `kind: systemd` under-monitors **silently and for ever**, which is `SNAG-UNITS-001` itself. Trading a silent wrong answer for a loud one is the same trade `schema_guard` makes by refusing to boot, and the same one `SNAG-DB-002` records in reverse (`REFRESH COLLATION VERSION` turning a loud known risk into a silent one)
  - **Mitigated rather than hidden**: the snippet carries two comment lines — that the port is observed and the path is the contract's default, and that `/health` and `/api/v1/health` are both in use here — so the fix is an edit rather than an investigation. A test asserts the marker is present
  - **Why it was not fixed here**: knowing the path needs a request, and a request belongs to the sysadmin agent's `http` check — which already makes one every 300 s — not to a six-hourly sweep whose no-network character is what lets it be tested against a directory under `tmp_path`. The candidate fix is to probe `/api/health`, `/health` and `/api/v1/health` once when a snippet is generated, and emit the one that answers; it needs a decision about whether the sweep may make outbound requests at all
  - **Found**: 2026-08-15, by running the new snippet generator against the live box as a counterfactual rather than only against fixtures
  - **Check**: <!--check:health_path_guess--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — the fourth instrument, and the first that sends a request. Two halves. The **pure** half drives `recommendations_for_scan` at every port `services.yaml` declares with a unit and reads the path back out of the emitted snippet; different paths for different ports means the generator is deciding rather than defaulting, and that is this entry's fix. The **live** half asks whether that emitted url actually answers, through `SysAdminAgent._check_http` and `is_fault` rather than a status code of its own — paired every time with a probe of the service's own declared url, because a guess that fails on a stopped service is evidence about the box and not about the path. Refuted when the emitted path stops being one constant, or when the recount stops coming out wrong more often than right; the figures above are re-measured on every run and never restated in code, and a drift that keeps the direction is reported rather than treated as a refutation. `unknown` when nothing answers its own url, and when every service the guess fails on declares a bare path — an implementation that probed and fell back would emit the same constant, so without a witness the constant proves nothing

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-005: **the estate's port registry gives 8500 to `sysadmin-service`, which is not a project id on this box** (2026-08-15)
  - **Symptom**: `monitorable-project.md` §2.1 row for 8500 names `sysadmin-service`. The manifest id is `sysadmin-assistant` and the directory is `sysadmin_assistant`, so neither matches. Session 26c's `wrong_project` check finds the unit holding 8500 (`sysadmin.service`) belongs to `sysadmin_assistant` and the row claims something else
  - **Reported as evidence, not as a finding**, and deliberately: a registry row may legitimately name a third-party daemon — `_syncthing_` claims 8384 and is not a repository — and telling a typo from a daemon needs judgement this check does not have. "Wrong project" and "not a project" are different faults and only the first is ours. It lands in `findings['ports']['unknown_registry_projects']`
  - **This is the estate's document and the estate's fix**, per rule 2: the audit files findings about a repository, the repository fixes them under its own process, and neither writes into the other. Recorded here so it is not lost, to be raised with estate-manager as a recommendation rather than a ruling
  - **Found**: 2026-08-15, on the first live run of `judge_ports` — the only thing it had to say about a box where nothing collides
  - **Check**: <!--check:estate_port_8500--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when the estate's `8500` row stops naming `sysadmin-service`. Read-only, and it answers *does the row still say this* rather than *should it* — the fix is the estate's

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-006: **the audit publishes a finding's `code` nowhere, so the judge's `details['code']` is `None` on every payload the estate can serve** (2026-08-16, **delegated**)
  - **Symptom**: `judge_audit_findings` writes `details['code'] = finding.get('code')` and its rule 4 says "the code lives in `details`". It does not. `GET :8400/api/audit/findings` publishes eleven keys per finding and `code` is not among them — verified against the live route and against a payload the producer built with a real `unclaimed_listener` breach in it
  - **Cause**: `estate_service.audit.finding.Finding` has a real `code` field, uses it as the last segment of `fingerprint` (`<check>:<subject>:<code>`), and then loses it at storage — `AuditFinding` has no `code` column, so `_record` cannot write one and the route cannot read one. It is `SNAG-ESTATE-002`'s shape exactly: a value the producer computes and the wire discards, except that this one is dropped by a **missing column** rather than by `asdict` skipping a property
  - **Cost today: none**, stated so it is not over-read. `unclaimed_listener` is the only ports breach the check emits, so nothing is ambiguous, and `details['fingerprint']` carries the code where a human can read it. What it costs is the docstring's claim, which is now corrected
  - **Not worked around here, deliberately.** The only workaround available is splitting `fingerprint` on its last colon, which is this repository parsing an identity format the estate owns — `_port_of`'s rule 3 one field over ("a finding whose port will not parse is skipped rather than titled from the sentence"). `judge_audit_findings` goes on reading `code`, so the day the producer publishes one it starts working with no change here
  - **Pre-staged rather than parked**: `tests/test_estate_surface_payloads.py::test_the_producers_code_never_reaches_the_wire` asserts the **absence** and fails, with the next move in its message, on the day estate-manager adds the column. The `PRODUCER_DROPPED_NUDGE_KEYS` shape, which is where this repository put the same problem in Session 52
  - **The IDs will not correspond** — estate-manager numbers its own `SNAG-ESTATE-*` series, the way this repository's `SNAG-ESTATE-002` became its `SNAG-ESTATE-010`. Not yet handed over; it needs an estate-manager session to record it
  - **Found**: 2026-08-16 by Session 54, driving the producer's `ports.run_check` against real listeners on 3900–3905 and storing the findings through its own models
  - **May it declare itself *checked by another guard*? No — judged 2026-08-27 by Session 102, and the reason is not the precedent but the guard.** The bullet above says `test_the_producers_code_never_reaches_the_wire` "fails, with the next move in its message, on the day estate-manager adds the column". It cannot. It reads `tests/fixtures/estate_audit_findings.json`, a payload **this repository recorded**, so it fires the day somebody re-captures the fixture and is silent for ever otherwise. What it pins is that `judgements.py`'s docstring agrees with our own recording — worth having, and a different claim from this entry's. A declaration of coverage would therefore have been true-sounding and false on its first use, which is exactly the silent retirement `ops_claims` rule 1 exists to refuse
  - **The claim was measured live instead, and still holds**: `GET :8400/api/audit/findings` publishes **11** keys per finding — `age_truncated`, `check`, `detail`, `fingerprint`, `first_seen_at`, `observed_at`, `runs_observed`, `severity`, `standing_days`, `subject`, `summary` — across 3 live findings, and `code` is not among them (2026-08-27, one `docs` breach and two `ports`). Reading the guard says nothing about that; only reading the route does, which is `verify-ops-claims-live` arriving at a *guard's* stated scope rather than at a snag's
  - **So the honest move is a check, not an exemption**, and this entry is a live candidate for the next one. The reason it was ranked behind `SNAG-SVC-001` — that a check would be a second statement of an existing guard — is refuted by the paragraph above: the two would measure different things, the fixture and the wire. `SNAG-ESTATE-002`'s check (Session 88) and `SNAG-LOG-012`'s (the fourteenth) already did this for delegated entries, and this entry's own **not worked around here** bullet is what such a check must respect — it reads the published keys and never splits `fingerprint`, which is this repository parsing an identity format the estate owns
  - **Check**: <!--check:audit_code_unpublished--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when `GET :8400/api/audit/findings` publishes a `code` key, when it publishes one inside a finding's `detail` blob, or when `Finding` stops computing a `code` at all; `match` **carries a note** when `audit_findings` gains the column and the route still publishes no key, because a fix in flight must not read as silence. **The twenty-fifth check, the sixth across a repository boundary, and the third whose subject is what another repository *publishes*.** The bullet above ruled this entry a live candidate and this is what was built. **The entry's own *not worked around here* bullet is a constraint on the instrument, not a note beside it**: nothing splits `fingerprint`, the reading is the set of published keys, and `tests/test_snag_claims.py` walks this module's own source for such a split — the cheapest way for a later sitting to make the check "better" is the one thing the entry forbids. **Two instruments, and the second exists because the first goes blind at the finish line.** The live wire is what the entry measured and what `judge_audit_findings` reads, pulled through `estate.client.pull_all` so the path, base URL and failure handling are the consumer's rather than a second set that can disagree; across **135 audit runs it has never once been clean** (minimum one finding, measured 2026-08-27), so rule 1's weather problem does not bite here the way it did for the nudge surface. What does bite is the end state: a clean audit publishes `"findings": []` and says nothing about its own key set, so a wire-only check reports `unknown` on precisely the morning the estate becomes conformant. The specimen answers whatever the audit found. **The wire can refute and cannot confirm**, which is the verdict rule: a `code` key on a served finding kills the claim whatever the checkout says, and its absence is one deploy behind by construction — `estate_module_state`'s "committed is not deployed" read the other way, and named rather than measured for the reason that function was written. **No database is opened in either repository**, which is the estate rules' first rule met by a check that runs at both ends of every sitting: the key set is decided by a literal dict in their serialiser, so the session is a stand-in dispatching on SQLAlchemy's public `column_descriptions` (a reordered query raises rather than being answered wrongly) and `get_db_session` is **poisoned before their router is imported**, which is `QUEUE_TIMEZONE_PROBE`'s `UserSystemd(runner=refuse)` one surface over. **The live wire is a control on the specimen rather than only evidence**: the two produced the identical eleven-key set on the first drive, which is what a stand-in drifted from their query shape could not do. **And the entry understates its own scope in one direction and overstates it in another.** `Finding.as_payload` — the form published on `estate/audit/findings/{check}` — **does** carry `code`, so the producer already has a published shape holding the field and "publishes a finding's `code` nowhere" is true of the surface this repository reads and false of the bus. The four remedies are four different notes rather than one verdict, and the `detail`-blob one is the only `mismatch` here that owes *this* repository a line, since `judge_audit_findings` reads `code` at the top level
  - **The general rule the judgement settles, so a later sitting does not re-derive it**: a declaration may move an entry **between published buckets** and may never remove it from the report. `ops_claims` rule 1 is that a marker is additive and cannot subtract — the same sentence at the level of the register. A `covered-by` bullet is legitimate only when the machinery *resolves the named guard* (a name nothing implements is a finding, exactly as `check_markers` treats a marker naming no check) **and** the entry stays named every sitting in a category of its own, so `unchecked` falling by one shows up as `covered` rising by one. Unbuilt, deliberately: this sitting was asked for the judgement rather than the mechanism, and the mechanism has one live candidate, which is not enough population to design against

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-007: **the queue surface stamps its timestamps in local time while every other estate surface stamps UTC** (2026-08-16, **delegated**)
  - **Symptom**: a granted lease comes back as `"granted_at": "2026-08-16T09:31:01+01:00"`, where `/api/projects/invariants`, `/api/audit/invariants` and `/api/audit/findings` all serve `+00:00`. Same process, same database, two renderings
  - **Cause**: `estate_service/projects/db.py` builds its SQLAlchemy engine with `connect_args={"options": "-c timezone=utc"}` and says why — the Session 4 cutover diff caught asyncpg and psycopg disagreeing about the wire format. `estate_service/db.py`'s `AsyncConnectionPool`, which the arbiter uses, passes only `row_factory` — so `psycopg` renders `timestamptz` in the connection's timezone, which is the box's `Europe/London`
  - **Costs nothing here and is filed anyway.** `judge_queue_invariants` carries `active_lease` into `details` verbatim and parses none of it, so no judgement is wrong. The cost is to whoever parses it next and assumes the estate speaks UTC, which three of its four surfaces do and which the surviving comment in the *other* engine explicitly promises
  - **Observed rather than reasoned about**: the live queue has never had a granted lease at the moment anyone looked (`"active_lease": null` on every reading), so this came out of driving `Arbiter.invariants()` against synthetic leases on a connection built with the producer's own pool kwargs
  - **The estate's fix, not this repository's** — one `kwargs` entry in their pool, and rule 3 forbids writing code into another repository whatever the size
  - <!--check:queue_stamps_local--> **Checked 2026-08-27 (Session 101) — the twenty-third check, and the fifth driven across a repository boundary.** The second whose subject is what another repository *publishes* rather than what its code computes, and the first where the fix could land in **three** places — the pool's kwargs (which this entry's cause bullet names), `invariants()`, or the route's serialiser — so an `ast` walk for the `connect_args` entry would report *still holds* for two of the three. All three are driven as real stand-ins. What is read instead is the **string on the wire**, taken by calling the route object mounted at the queue's published path, which is how `_public` is reached without ever naming a private symbol of theirs. **Its population is empty by construction**, which is rule 1's shape for the fourth time: `active_lease` is re-read live every run and reported as *evidence*, never as the verdict, because a check that waited for a granted lease would be measuring whether somebody happens to be holding the GPU this afternoon — so the lease is built, exactly as this entry's own *observed rather than reasoned about* bullet says it was when the defect was found. **The obvious instrument agrees with itself only in summer**: this entry quotes one rendered offset, and `Europe/London` renders `+00:00` from late October to late March, so a one-instant check reports the entry refuted every winter and true again every spring, having measured the calendar. Two instants six months apart are driven through every surface and *stamps UTC* means **both** came back at zero — which a session pinned to UTC satisfies by construction and a local one cannot. **The cause is driven rather than read, and the same reading validates the stand-in.** `pg_settings.source` says where the connection's `TimeZone` came from — `client` when it asked, which is exactly what the fix this entry proposes produces, and `configuration file` when it inherited the cluster's — measured against both pool shapes before it was written down. That is what separates *their fix landed* from *the box's default moved to UTC*, which renders identically and leaves the mechanism intact, and the second is `unknown` because `match` would assert a local rendering nobody saw. It is also what makes pointing their pool at **this** repository's database legitimate rather than merely convenient: the estate rules forbid one application reading another's database, and a check running at both ends of every sitting would be the most regular breach of that rule on the box — so nothing of theirs is opened, the lease lives in a `TEMPORARY` table, and a `database` or `user` source would say the substitution is unsound and stop the check answering. **The complaint and the premise refute this entry for opposite reasons** and are reported apart: the sibling engine ceasing to stamp UTC is the premise dying with the complaint intact, and a single boolean would file a deleted guarantee as a job well done. Live: the surface publishes `+00:00` and `+01:00` off a connection in `Europe/London` sourced from the configuration file, the project engine publishes `+00:00` twice off one sourced from `client`, and `active_lease` is still null. **Two stand-ins corrected the check before it shipped.** A route that normalises while the connection stays local was read as *the box's default moved* — the draft asked whether `invariants()` had handed UTC up, where the question is whether the **connection** is in UTC, and `zone_stamps_utc` resolves the reported name rather than comparing it to the string `UTC`, which `Etc/UTC` refutes. And a surface publishing only `granted_at` satisfied *every offset is zero* through `Europe/London` — the seasonal defect arriving by a dropped field instead of by the calendar, which is why the pair is required rather than merely preferred. **A falsification passed against broken code for the fifth time here**: removing the completeness half of `stamps_utc` broke nothing, because the verdict body refuses an incomplete reading one gate earlier. It is not two statements of one fact — `complete` is defined once and consulted twice — so the gate stays and the observation moved to the property, where it is reachable

- [P2] SNAG-ESTATE-008: **nothing checks a documented ops action against the box, and all three in the block that opens every sitting were already done** (2026-08-16, **scope corrected the same sitting** — filed for one stale action, measured at three of three, **machine-checkable half FIXED 2026-08-24 by Session 73**; the general case is `SNAG-ESTATE-011`, **closed 2026-08-25** by Session 83)
  - **Symptom**: `SNAG-DB-002`'s `REINDEX` half was carried out by estate-manager on 2026-08-13 between 18:03 and 20:29. `snag_list.md`, `tasks.md`, `STATUS.md` and `HANDOFF.md` each independently restated it as pending until 2026-08-16 — **three days and five sittings** — with `STATUS.md` carrying it as a standing **sub-session action**, which is the block the global rules require to be read first at the top of every sitting
  - **This application observed the remedy land and said so.** `monitor/collation.py` saw the estate's first pass on the next five-minute poll and resolved all eight rows at `18:01:48`; `details->'collation'` has read `mismatched: 0` on every run since. The observation was correct, timely and machine-readable, and nothing read it back — `SNAG-ESTATE-001`'s roll-up defect with the polarity reversed, which is the worse direction. An unread fault is a missed alarm; an **unread recovery** is an instruction to redo finished work, and the work in question was a `REINDEX` of a live 16 GB database
  - **Cause is structural rather than an oversight.** Two repositories own a collation check deliberately — this one alerts, the estate files findings — and nothing reconciles the two. `EstateJudgeAgent` reads `/api/audit/findings` and is narrowed to `check == "ports"` (Session 26b-A), on the stated grounds that every other check is either this service's own alerts arriving through a second producer or another repository's conformance. **Collation is the case that narrowing did not anticipate**: shared infrastructure, owned by neither application, remedied by the estate — so the estate's record is the only record that the work happened, and it sits in the one finding class this repository declines to read
  - **Three candidate fixes, none costed, and the cheap one is not the obvious one.** (1) Widen the judge to `check == "collation"` — re-imports this service's own alerts through a second producer, which is precisely what rule 3 exists to prevent, and gives one fault two owners. (2) Have a document check compare a closed ops action against the estate's audit — a new consumer of a surface that may 500, and a new way for a document to be wrong. (3) **Give this repository's own resolve a reader**: nothing anywhere treats "an ops action in a document with no open alert row behind it" as suspect, and that is the signal this case already generated correctly. The third adds no cross-repo dependency and is the one to price first
  - **The narrow half is cheap and separable, and would have caught this instance**: `scripts/claude-preflight.sh` prints the handoff and the priorities without checking either against `sysadmin.alerts`. A single query — ops actions named in `STATUS.md`'s sub-session block against open rows — is not a fix for the general case and is one line of `psql` in a script that already runs every sitting
  - **Filed for one item and immediately measured at three, which is the entry's real finding.** `STATUS.md`'s sub-session block and `HANDOFF.md`'s "Blocked / owed" list carried three actions at the close of Session 55. **Every one had been carried out**, and none of the three by the party that recorded it:
    1. **`sudo systemctl restart sysadmin.service`** — done **2026-08-16 12:06:09**, seven minutes after Session 55's own commit (`a330e31`, 11:59). PID 1410826 → **1914354**; `POST /api/sysadmin/reload` returns **200** where the block says it 404s; and `desktop_reminder_sweep` is scheduled in the running process at `interval[0:03:00]`, which is the one thing the block said was *entirely* invisible until the restart landed. The block was written after the restart it asks for
    2. **The five system-scope orphan removals** (`SNAG-UNITS-005`) — `offline-agents-dashboard`, `personalassistant-backend`, `personalassistant-frontend`, `ticktick-sync` (+ timer) and `ticktick-sync-db` are all **absent** from `/etc/systemd/system` and all report `not-found`. Done by the owner
    3. **`SNAG-DB-002`'s `REINDEX`-before-`REFRESH` remedy** — done 2026-08-13 by estate-manager, above
  - **That reframes the fault from a cross-repo seam to a missing check**, and widens it in the direction that matters. One stale action is an oversight; **three of three** is the block having no verification at all, and two of the three are plain `systemctl` state this repository already reads every 300 seconds. The original framing — "estate-manager's remedy is invisible here" — was true and was the *narrowest* reading available, arrived at because the first item measured was the cross-repo one
  - **Each of the three had its evidence somewhere on the box, in a different place**: an `alerts` row this application resolved itself (collation), `systemctl is-enabled` reporting `not-found` (the orphans), and the daemon's own `MainPID` plus a route that had stopped 404ing (the restart). No single query covers them, which is why candidate 3 above is better read as a **convention** — every ops action names the check that closes it — than as one clever query
  - **`STATUS.md` is now the first version of that block to be empty**, and an empty block is itself a claim needing the same treatment: "nothing owed" and "nothing checked" must not render identically. `UnitScanResponse.ports_checked`'s rule, arriving in a document rather than a payload
  - **P2 stands, and the reasoning changed.** Filed at P2 because re-running a completed remedy would have been a 30-second idempotent no-op — the estate's script skips a database already at the current version; kept at P2 for a different reason — the cost is not a wrong action but five sittings of ranking attention spent on settled items at the top of the document that sets the agenda, plus a **restart the owner may have performed because the document asked for it after it was already done**
  - **Found**: 2026-08-16, by taking `SNAG-DB-002` as a session and measuring the box before reading the entry — the global rule "read the live state, not what the documents claim about themselves", failing on the one snag whose whole subject is a live-state query, then widened within the hour by checking the other two items in the same block rather than stopping at the one the session came in for
  - **FIXED 2026-08-24 (Session 73) — the narrow half this entry named as cheap and separable.** `sysadmin/ops_claims.py`, the console script `sysadmin-check-claims` and `scripts/check-ops-claims.sh`, run by `claude-preflight.sh` at the start of a sitting and `claude-postflight.sh` at the close. Seven checks: five claims parsed out of the block itself (routes, tables, the documented Alembic head, unresolved alerts, the daemon's start time) and two that compare the **box** against this checkout (the live schema against the packaged head; whether the running daemon is serving the code on disk). Advisory at both ends — nothing blocks and nothing edits a document
  - **The entry understated the defect it filed, and by one whole surface.** It says preflight "prints the handoff and the priorities without checking either against `sysadmin.alerts`". Measured: preflight printed `sed -n '/## Quick Status/,/^## /p'`, and the sub-session block is a blockquote **above** that heading — so the banner that opens every sitting was not failing to *check* the block, it never *printed* it. The one surface the global rules require to be read first was the one it omitted. It prints now, bounded at 30 lines and stopped at the ranked recommendation, which is prose for the reader rather than a claim about the box
  - **The obvious "is a restart owed" check is wrong on this box, and it was wrong *today*.** Comparing the daemon's `ActiveEnterTimestamp` (09:58:28) against the newest commit touching `sysadmin/` (10:05:22) reports a restart owed, and the content is identical — this repository restarts to verify and commits afterwards, so commit time trails the deploy by minutes. The newest `.py` on disk was written **09:57:46**, 42 seconds *before* the start, which is the question actually being asked: is the running process serving what is on disk. Measured both ways before either was written down
  - **`systemctl show` answers for a unit that does not exist**, exits `0` and prints `ActiveState=inactive` — "nobody looked" rendered as a measurement, which is this entry's own shape found inside its own fix. `LoadState` is the gate, and the test that pins it drives the real binary rather than a stub, because a stub written by the same hand as the gate agrees with it by construction
  - **A *fall* in the unresolved-alert count is the founding case, and it is the direction nobody writes a rule for.** The obvious comparison is equality, or a rise meaning "something new opened". This entry exists because eight collation rows **resolved themselves** at 18:01:48 and four documents went on asking for the `REINDEX` for three days, so the fall is the signal that already existed and had no reader. Both directions are reported and the wording differs; the open titles are named rather than counted
  - **The route count has a definition, and the obvious one is off by four.** `len(app.routes)` is **50**; the figure this file has always carried is **46**, because FastAPI adds `/openapi.json`, `/docs`, `/docs/oauth2-redirect` and `/redoc` as plain Starlette routes of its own. A sitting re-counting "live off `create_app()`" the obvious way would have reported the Quick Status table stale on its first run
  - **Every way of not-knowing is `unknown`, never `match`** — `ports_checked`'s rule, and the verdicts and exit statuses are `schema_guard`'s three rather than a boolean, imported rather than restated. Four distinguishable faults: a pattern that finds nothing, a block stating one figure two ways (drift with both halves inside one file), a database that will not answer, and a unit systemd has never heard of. The state checks still run when STATUS.md cannot be read at all, because a missing document is never a reason to stop asking whether the box is behind its checkout
  - **It ships untriggered on the claims half and was driven against a document made false on purpose.** All five claims in today's real block hold. A copy with the route count set to 44, the alert count to 9 and the restart backdated to 2026-08-22 produces three `no` lines, and the alert line reads *"7 fewer unresolved rows than the block accounts for — a row it treats as open has resolved, so an action it asks for may already be done"*, which is this entry's founding case reproduced from the outside. 35 tests, six of them falsified against the behaviour they replace (first-match parsing, `len(app.routes)`, no `LoadState` gate, `max()` over the exit map, an unbounded region, and the missing `flatten()`) — all six fired
  - **The check refuted its author within a minute of being wired up**, which is the one finding no fixture could have produced. The first rewrite of the block under it wrapped `holds **2**` and `unresolved` across two lines with a blockquote marker between them, and the claim came back `unknown` — correct by rule 2, and useless: `unknown` for a reason no reader would guess is how a check goes quiet. `flatten()` strips the decoration and matches against prose, so a paragraph reflow cannot retire a claim
  - **What is left is `SNAG-ESTATE-011`**, and it is the half this entry called a *convention* rather than a query: five figures are machine-checkable and the rest of the block is prose no pattern can reach
  - **CLOSED 2026-08-25 (Session 83) — decided rather than measured, and what the decision settles is that an empty *residue* is a closure while an empty *population* is not.** Session 82 measured thirty entries against the box and deliberately left this one, on the grounds that closing it is a judgement about a chain rather than a measurement. The judgement is taken here, and the rule it turns on is that **closing a chain is not a closure**: what closes an entry is having nothing left that it *uniquely* names and nothing owns
  - **The test is this entry's own founding instances, and it was driven rather than read off the docstrings.** Of the three ops actions measured as already-done, two are now caught. Backdating the block's restart to `2026-08-22 09:00:00` makes `check_daemon_start` report *"it has restarted since the block was written, and nothing recorded why"*; raising `holds **2**` to `**9**` makes `check_alerts` report *"7 fewer unresolved row(s) than the block accounts for — a row it treats as open has resolved, so an action it asks for may already be done"*, a note that **ends `(SNAG-ESTATE-008's founding case)`** — the running code cites the entry it closes
  - **The third instance is genuinely uncovered, and it is `SNAG-ESTATE-012`'s class exactly — which is what makes the residue empty rather than merely small.** The five system-scope orphan removals were evidenced by `systemctl is-enabled` reporting `not-found`, and nothing here resolves an arbitrary unit: `measure_unit(unit: str = OWN_UNIT)` has **one** caller and it passes the default, so the only unit this module ever asks about is its own. But the sentence that would carry that action has no figure and no marker, which is ESTATE-012's symptom verbatim. The residue has an owner, and a **narrower** statement than this entry's
  - **Leaving it open had become an instance of itself, which is what decides a judgement when no measurement will.** The `P2` was kept for a stated cost — not a wrong action, but *"five sittings of ranking attention spent on settled items at the top of the document that sets the agenda"*. It has since been named by eight consecutive rankings with nothing of its own to do. An open `P2` whose work is done, at the top of the document that sets the agenda, is that cost being paid by the entry itself
  - **Two entries describing one open defect is a shape refused everywhere else here**, and these two already disagree about severity: this one is `P2`, and `SNAG-ESTATE-012` is `P3` by its own reasoning (*"the classes that drift silently are covered. This one drifts in front of a reader"*). Left open, this entry ranks the residue two rungs too loud — `SNAG-DB-003`'s two-statements-of-one-fact arriving in a roadmap rather than in a config
