  - **Two of the three candidate fixes were refused on the record and are not residue.** Widening the judge to `check == "collation"` re-imports this service's own alerts through a second producer (`judgements.py` rule 3); a document check against the estate's audit adds a consumer of a surface that may 500. Candidate 3 — *"give this repository's own resolve a reader"* — is what shipped, as `check_alerts`'s fall note and `check_open_titles`. A rejected alternative is not an open half
  - **The counter-argument, stated and rejected.** Session 82 deferred on the parser author's trade: *an entry left visible costs a reader a glance, an entry closed by a misread manufactures a clean row.* That trade governs a **misread**, and this is not one — the closure is not taken on `SNAG-ESTATE-011` being fixed, it is taken on this entry's residue being empty, established by driving the three founding instances through the live machinery above. Session 82's own rule survives untouched: `SNAG-LOG-013` stays open on an empty *population*, because its claim holds again the moment the population refills, and a residue cannot refill

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-011: **the block's other claims are still unverified prose, and the convention that would fix them is not built** (2026-08-24, opened by Session 73, **FIXED 2026-08-24 by Session 76**)
  - **Symptom**: `sysadmin-check-claims` re-measures five figures. Today's block also asserts that `/health` answers, that `GET /api/logs/actions` "serves 9 rows with 0 colliding titles", that an estate row "clears at 03:32 tomorrow with nothing done", and that a set of historic log rows "leave that window at about 14:11 today". Every one of those is checkable by hand and none by pattern, and the last of them is the instance that opened this pair — a boundary asserted three hours before it happened
  - **Cause**: `SNAG-ESTATE-008` proposed candidate 3 as *"a **convention** — every ops action names the check that closes it — rather than one clever query"*, and what shipped is the query half, because that is the half with an enforcement point. The convention has no mechanism: nothing requires a claim to carry the command that would refute it, and a claim that names no check cannot be re-measured by anything
  - **The cheap next move is a shape, not a parser.** A block line that carries its own check — a fenced command, or a `<!-- check: … -->` — is re-runnable without anyone guessing the pattern. It was **not** done here on purpose: a marker beside the prose is a second statement of one fact that can disagree with the first, which is `SNAG-DB-003`'s shape arriving in a document, and the trade is only worth taking once somebody has written the block twice under the rule
  - **Related and deliberately not folded in**: the deploy check keys on the newest `.py` under `sysadmin/`, so a file the daemon never imports — `ops_claims.py` itself, the moment it is written — reports a restart owed, as does a rebase or an edit reverted back. It fails in the direction that costs a needless `kill -TERM`, which on this box is not privileged and takes a second
  - **P3**: the five figures that drift silently are covered, and what is left needs a human to have read the sentence either way
  - **FIXED 2026-08-24 (Session 76) — the convention, and the resolution of the contradiction this entry carried.** The entry names `<!-- check: … -->` as the cheap next move and argues against a marker in the next sentence, which is the same objection `ops_claims.py` rule 1 makes: `<!-- routes=46 -->` beside a sentence is a second statement of one fact that can agree with the box while the prose disagrees, and nothing notices. **`<!--check:routes-->` is not that.** It names a *check*, never a value, so there is still exactly one figure in the document — the one in the prose — and the named check reads it from there. Two statements that cannot disagree about a fact, because one of them states none
  - **The marker is additive and cannot subtract**, which is what keeps rule 2 true. Every pattern-bearing claim runs whether or not a line names it, so deleting a marker can never be a way to retire a check — only a way to be told about it. A marker that *gated* a check would make "edit the document" a way to switch one off, which is the silent retirement rule 2 exists to prevent, arriving inside the fix for it
  - **What the marker buys is the enforcement point `SNAG-ESTATE-008` asked for and had no way to have.** That entry proposed *"every ops action names the check that closes it"* and shipped only the query half, because the convention half had nothing behind it. `check_markers` is that: a figure this module can test that no line claims is reported, and a marker naming a check nobody implements is reported. On its first run against the real block **five figures came back unclaimed** — routes, tables, migration_head, alerts, daemon_start — every one of them a sentence that had been checked for a sitting and never claimed
  - **A typo fires from both sides, which was not designed and is the better property.** Driven against a document broken on purpose, `<!--check:helth-->` produced *two* findings: the unknown name, and the now-unclaimed `health` figure beside it. A rename that misses one end is caught at the other
  - **The founding instance is a different class and needed a different mechanism.** This entry was opened by *"the row clears at 03:32 with nothing done"*, written at 00:30 — a sentence that was not wrong when written and not measurable when written either, so no pattern reaches it. `<!--check:expires 2026-08-25T03:32 …-->` is the one family whose **members the document declares** rather than this module. Before its moment the claim stands and the report says how long it has to run; after its moment it is `unknown` — **never `mismatch`**, because the prediction may well have come true and "nobody went back" is exactly what rule 2 reserves `unknown` for
  - **The instant is the single fact stated twice, so it is pinned rather than trusted.** The marker must carry a date the prose has no room for — "at 03:32" names a wall clock and no day, and a pattern guessing the day is wrong once per prediction. So the wall clock the marker renders must appear in the block, or the claim is `unknown` and names both moments: `syslog_priority` against `PRIORITY_MAP`'s treatment, not asserted on each side
  - **The pin was broken and only a live run said so.** It searched the flattened region, **which contains the marker**, so `03:32` matched the marker's own copy and the pin passed whatever the sentence said — a check agreeing with itself by construction, which is this file's recurring shape at the size of a substring test. Three fixture tests of the pin were green throughout and stayed green after the fix, because their fixtures happen not to carry a marker; the one test that fails without `prose_without_markers` is the one written *after* driving a reworded block through the real script
  - **`check_open_titles` is the finer half of the alert count.** The count moves when a row opens or closes and holds still through a **swap** — one row resolving as another opens — while the block's sentence about *which* rows are open goes wrong. One direction only, and the other has an owner: a row the block names that has resolved is already `check_alerts`'s *fall* note, this entry's founding case; what that note cannot say is that a row nobody wrote about is open
  - **`/health` is checked and `8400` deliberately is not.** The block asserts both. The first is this daemon's own route and is a different fact from the deploy check's — `systemctl` reporting `active` says the process is up, `/health` says the application is serving, and `SNAG-DB-005` is the 23 hours where those parted company. The second is estate-manager's availability, which `estate/judgements.py` rule 3 declines to judge here on the grounds that a second owner of one lifecycle closes rows the first still holds true; a claims-checker that alerted on it would re-import exactly that. The block now says so in the sentence rather than leaving the silence to be read as an oversight
  - **`CHECK_KEYS` is derived from `CLAIM_PATTERNS | KEYLESS_CHECKS`**, never written beside them — `max_priority_for` against `PRIORITY_MAP`'s rule, and the falsification showed why: a hand-written set forgot `health`, and **two** tests fired, the derived guard and the live-document one
  - **The cost, stated rather than implied.** The markers are HTML comments, so they do not render — but `claude-preflight.sh` prints the block as raw text, so a reader of the session-opening banner now sees them. That is the trade: the banner is slightly noisier, and every figure in it carries the name of the thing that would refute it. `SNAG-ESTATE-012` is what remains — a sentence with no pattern *and* no marker is still invisible, because deciding that a sentence is a claim is a human's job and always was
  - **19 new tests, five falsified against the behaviour they replace** (the unclaimed half removed, `mismatch` for a passed prediction, a hand-written `CHECK_KEYS`, the pin without `prose_without_markers`, and the marker read from the unflattened region) — all five fired, and the `CHECK_KEYS` one fired twice. The whole check was then driven against a copy of the real document broken four ways at once: a dropped marker, a `helth` typo, a backdated prediction and an unnamed alert row produced **four faults and exit 1**, and the real document produces **eleven `ok` and exit 0**

- [P3] SNAG-ESTATE-012: **a block sentence that is neither a figure nor a marked prediction is still invisible** (2026-08-24, opened by Session 76)
  - **Symptom**: `check_markers` reports a figure this module can test that no line claims, and `check_expiry` times a prediction the document declares. Neither can see a sentence that carries **no pattern and no marker** — "ask estate-manager the Session 33 question", "8400 answers `200` now", "`SNAG-ROADMAP-002` has published wrong board movement for seven consecutive sittings". Today's block carries three such
  - **Cause is not an oversight and may not have a fix.** Deciding that an English sentence is a *claim* is the human's job; a checker that guessed would either miss most of them or flag prose. The convention makes the decision visible — a sitting writes a marker or it does not — but it cannot make the decision
  - **What could be built, and why neither was**: (1) require every blockquote paragraph in the region to carry at least one marker, which turns the ranked recommendation and the "blocked" list into claims they are not; (2) a `<!--check:none reason-->` marker meaning "deliberately unchecked", which is a marker whose absence is indistinguishable from forgetting it — the thing it is meant to detect
  - **The one instance with a name**: "8400 answers `200`" is deliberately unchecked for a stated reason (`estate/judgements.py` rule 3), and that reason now lives **in the block's own prose** rather than in a marker. That is the cheapest form of the convention for this class and it is not mechanical
  - **P3**: the classes that drift *silently* are covered. This one drifts in front of a reader
  - <!--check:unmarked_sentence_invisible--> **Checked 2026-08-27 (Session 98) — the twentieth check, and the second aimed at this repository's own claims machinery.** `check_unmarked_sentence_invisible` builds a printed region carrying one sentence a pattern can reach, marked, and one of this entry's own three unmarked instances, runs the real `ops_claims.check_all` over both, and asserts the second is absent from every family. **The marked half is the witness**: a reader that had stopped parsing the region reports the unmarked sentence exactly as a working reader does, so silence alone would report this entry holding hardest on the morning `printed_region` broke. The check measures that the invisibility holds and is **not** the marker convention this entry rules out — both remedies it names by name are still refused


- [P2] SNAG-DB-002: **every PostgreSQL database on this box has a stale collation version**, and all three warn on every connection (2026-08-11, check half **fixed 2026-08-13**, remedy half **carried out by estate-manager the same evening** and **verified here 2026-08-16**, **closed 2026-08-25** by Session 82)
  - **Symptom**: `psql -d projects` prints `WARNING: database "projects" has a collation version mismatch — created using collation version 2.43, but the operating system provides version 2.44` before every command. Confirmed across the estate, not just this app's database: `pg_database.datcollversion` is **2.43** for `projects`, `alfred` **and** `postgres`, while `ldd --version` reports glibc **2.44**
  - **Cause**: a routine glibc upgrade on a rolling-release distro. `libc`'s locale data changed the sort order of text, and PostgreSQL records the version a database was created with precisely so it can tell you it no longer matches
  - **Impact**: this is not cosmetic and it is not urgent, which is an awkward combination to hold. Any B-tree index on a text column was built against 2.43's ordering, so a lookup can miss a row that is present — an index scan walks to where the string *used to* sort. There are **25 indexes in the `sysadmin` schema** alone, several on text (`alerts.title` is matched with `ilike` in `resolve_alerts`, `service_health.service_name`). No wrong result has been observed here and glibc's changes between minor versions are usually confined to unusual scripts, so the realistic risk is low — but "low" is a probability, not a guarantee, and a silently missed row in `_active_alerts` would present as **an alert that never gets resolved or never gets deduplicated**, which is indistinguishable from the bugs this repository keeps finding by hand
  - **Found**: incidentally, by Session 39 running `psql` to check a column type. Nothing monitors it — and this application is the thing on this box whose job would be to
  - **Fix**: `REINDEX DATABASE projects` then `ALTER DATABASE projects REFRESH COLLATION VERSION`, per database, during a quiet window. **The `REFRESH` on its own is the trap**: it clears the warning by asserting the versions now match without rebuilding anything, so it converts a loud known-risk into a silent one. Deliberately not run in this session — it touches two other apps' data, and a reindex of a 16 GB database is not something to start while its owner is mid-session
  - **Worth its own follow-up**: a check for this belongs in the sysadmin agent. It is exactly the class of fault this service exists to catch — measurable in one query, invisible until something goes wrong, and currently discovered only when a human happens to open `psql`
  - **The check half landed 2026-08-13 (Session 44); the `REINDEX` half was carried out the same evening — by estate-manager, not here.** *(This bullet read "is still open and stays open" until 2026-08-16; the closure bullets are at the end of the entry, and the three days between are `SNAG-ESTATE-008`.)* `sysadmin/monitor/collation.py` reads both sides from `pg_database` on every sysadmin run and raises `Stale collation version on <db>` at `warning`, with `REINDEX` named before `REFRESH` in the message because REFRESH alone converts a loud known risk into a silent one. The remedy is deliberately not automated: a reindex of a 16 GB database touching two other apps' data wants a quiet window and a human, which is the same judgement the original entry took
  - **The count was wrong by more than half, and the shape of the error is worth keeping.** This entry says three databases (`projects`, `alfred`, `postgres`) because those are the three someone had opened a `psql` session against. `pg_database` is a **cluster-wide** catalog, so one query from the existing connection sees all eleven: **eight are stale** — the five above plus `alfred_dev`, `alfred_e2e`, `alfred_test`, `alfred_test_cc` and `template1`. Reading the catalog rather than the warnings is the difference, and it is the same lesson as SNAG-BRIEF-001: a symptom seen by hand samples wherever the hand happened to be
  - **`estate`, `estate_test` and `venture` are clean at 2.44**, having been created after the upgrade. That is the evidence that `CREATE DATABASE` stamps the *current* OS version rather than inheriting the template's — so `template1` sitting at 2.43 is not a trap for future databases, only for its own indexes. `template0` records no version at all, which is why the check requires **both** sides non-NULL and fails *open*: `recorded != actual` in Python reads NULL as a fault and invents an alert whose remedy does not exist. Deliberately the opposite of `schema_guard`, where not-knowing must fail closed
  - **In production since 2026-08-13 14:22**, and the numbers are the live ones: run 1 at 14:27:11 wrote `details->'collation' = {raised: 8, resolved: 0, mismatched: 8}` and opened eight `warning` rows; run 2 at 14:32:11 wrote `{raised: 0, mismatched: 8}` and the table still holds **8 collation rows in total**. Under the `_check_thresholds` pattern run 2 would have written eight more
  - **Verified live 2026-08-13** in a rolled-back transaction, both halves: run 1 raised exactly 8 rows for exactly the 8 stale databases; run 2 raised 0 with all 8 still open; narrowing the query as though two had been reindexed and one dropped resolved exactly those 3 and left 5 open. Residue after rollback: 0 rows
  - **Closed 2026-08-16, and not by this repository.** estate-manager carried out the remedy on 2026-08-13 in two passes, both committed there: `eb51ff6` at 18:03 took the safe set (`template1`, `postgres`, `alfred_dev`, `alfred_e2e`, `alfred_test`, `alfred_test_cc`) and `52b312c` at 20:29 took `alfred` then `projects`, rebuilt 20:23–20:24. The remedy is their `scripts/refresh-collations.sh`, which **never issues the `REFRESH` unless that database's `REINDEX` has just succeeded** and carries a comment telling the next reader not to simplify it by splitting the two — this entry's trap, encoded by the party that owns it. The estate owns it because it spans three applications' databases, which is the reason no single application had ever fixed it
  - **Verified here independently 2026-08-16, and the obvious method was not sufficient.** Index file mtimes show the two bursts (83 indexes in `alfred_test_cc` at 17:59, 206 in `projects` at 20:24) and prove nothing about an actively-written index, whose file carries a recent mtime whether or not its contents were rebuilt — a hole worth naming, because mtime is what a first pass reaches for. The exact test is `pg_class.relfilenode` against `pg_class.oid`: a rebuild draws a fresh relfilenode from the cluster-wide counter, so an index never rewritten since creation still has `relfilenode = oid`. Across all eight databases **0 of 125 collation-sensitive user indexes** retains its original relfilenode; `projects`' 58 sit in one band, 3,882,764–3,886,294, against creation OIDs from 46,010
  - **"Collation-sensitive" is the filter this entry lacked**, and it cuts the stated exposure down. The entry counts "25 indexes in the `sysadmin` schema, several on text". An index is only at risk where `pg_index.indcollation` holds something other than `0` (not collatable), `950` (`C`) or `951` (`POSIX`) — the last two being byte-order and therefore immune to a glibc change. Measured: **58** in `projects`, **125** across the cluster, **0** in `pg_catalog`
  - **The eight alerts resolved themselves at 18:01:48**, all together, on the sysadmin agent's next five-minute poll after the estate's first pass. This application observed the remedy land, correctly and within five minutes, and recorded it in the one place nothing reads back; `details->'collation'` has read `{"raised": 0, "resolved": 0, "mismatched": 0}` on every run since. That gap is filed as `SNAG-ESTATE-008`
  - **Two of this entry's numbers were true when written and are now wrong, in opposite directions.** "One of them 16 GB" was right: `projects` *was* 16 GB, the reindex reclaimed 2.8 GB of index bloat, and estate-manager's `09d231c` then dropped the retired `personal_assistant` schema on 2026-08-14, taking the database to 1,094 MB — so today's 1098 MB is PersonalAssistant's data being deleted, not this entry having been wrong. "Eight stale databases" was already this entry's own correction of "three", and the estate audits all eleven
  - **The quiet window this entry was nervous about was 30 seconds.** `REINDEX` rebuilds indexes, and `projects` held 3,123 MB of them inside its 16 GB — the size the entry cited was never the size that governed the work, which is the same shape as its own correction from three databases to eight: a figure taken from where the hand happened to be. estate-manager checked the preconditions this entry only implied — no session idle in transaction, 342 GB free, next timer 5.5 h away, superuser role
  - **`REINDEX DATABASE` skips system catalogues on PostgreSQL 14+**, so the paired `REFRESH` asserts conformance over indexes it never rebuilt — this entry's own trap one level down, and neither half of the estate's script covers it. Measured from both ends rather than reasoned about: estate-manager found 35 of `projects`' 4,162 indexes unrewritten, all catalogue TOAST indexes on `(chunk_id, chunk_seq)` with no collatable column, and **0 of the 1,531 carrying collation 100 skipped**; this repository arrived at the same answer from the other side, `pg_catalog` holding **0** collation-sensitive indexes at all because its text columns are `name`. No gap — but "no gap" is now a measurement, taken twice, independently
  - **CLOSED 2026-08-25 (Session 82), measured against `pg_database` rather than against this entry's own history.** **11 of 12** databases report `datcollversion` and `pg_database_collation_actual_version()` both at **2.44**; the twelfth is `template0`, which records no version and is excluded by the check's own non-NULL-on-both-sides requirement — not-knowing is not a mismatch, `monitor/collation.py`'s rule 1, and it is why the honest count is **0 stale** rather than one. Note what kept this open with three closures already in its title: `read_snags` needs a *done word at the start of a clause*, and `check half **fixed 2026-08-13**` starts with "check" — this entry is the named example in the parser's own docstring

- [P3] SNAG-SYSD-003: `sysadmin.service` still orders itself after a service retired three weeks ago (2026-08-11)
  - **Symptom**: `After=network.target postgresql.service ollama.service`. Ollama was retired on 2026-07-24 when the LLM runtime migrated to llama.cpp (`alfred-inference.service`); there is no `ollama.service` on this box
  - **Impact**: none functionally — systemd ignores an `After=` on a unit that does not exist, and ordering never *requires*. The cost is documentary: the unit file is read as the record of what this service depends on, and it names the wrong inference runtime. Anyone deriving a new project's unit from it (which [monitorable-project.md](../guides/monitorable-project.md) invites) copies the stale dependency forward
  - **Found**: while editing the same file to add `StartLimitBurst` in Session 39
  - **Deliberately left**: the Session 39 edit needed to stay a single-purpose, reversible change with one rollback path. Removing a dependency line in the same commit as a restart-limit change means a rollback either reverts both or reverts neither. The correct successor is `alfred-inference.service`, and the honest question to settle first is whether this service should order against it at all — it does not need an LLM to start, and both weekly reviews already fall back to a deterministic digest when inference is unavailable, so the answer is probably to drop the line rather than replace it
  - **Check**: <!--check:sysd_ollama_ordering--> `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when the `After=` line drops `ollama.service`, or when `ollama.service` resolves again — opposite refutations, so the check reports them apart

- [P2] SNAG-TRAY-009: **the tray watched a dead backend for 22 hours and never said a word, and it is silent for two independent reasons** (2026-08-29, opened by Session 126 as the measured residue of `SNAG-SYSD-005`, **FIXED 2026-08-29** by Session 127)
  - **Fixed** by `NotificationPolicy.evaluate_backend_unreachable` behind a **300-second** grace, fed by a new `ApiWorker.backend_unreachable(float)` tick and a tri-state `_was_connected`. Both faces move together, because the entry is right that a fix for one is not half the benefit
  - **The grace is `max(3 × status_poll_seconds, 300 s)` and both halves are borrowed.** The 3 is `self_monitor.stall_grace_multiplier` — one missed observation is merely late — which `notifications.desktop.tray_grace_seconds` already applies to a tray poll ("3× the tray's alert_poll_seconds"). The 300 is `min_stall_grace_seconds`, whose stated reason in `config.yaml` is literally *"so a restart doesn't flag the 60s log aggregator"*: this family's noise population, one domain over
  - **The floor is what does the work, and the multiple alone would have derived from a leaf with two producers.** `status_poll_seconds` is **10** in `sysadmin_tray/config.py`'s model and **30** in the shipped `config.yaml`, so "3× the poll interval" spans 30–90 s while the daemon startup it must clear does not move with the poll interval at all. The noise is bounded in *seconds* and the observation counted in *polls*, so the threshold is stored in seconds and the polls only wake it — `failures.py`'s runs-not-hours argument running the other way
  - **The two populations are 1,400× apart, measured from two instruments.** The daemon's journal holds **104 deploy restarts of 2, 3, 12 or 13 s — max 13 s** over 30 days, with nothing between that and a reboot (the one 276 s window has a `-- Boot --` marker inside it, so the tray was down too, and the six 8.9–12.5 h windows are the box off overnight). The tray's own journal — its `httpx` line is logged only on a *successful* `/health`, so a gap inside one tray life is a window in which it polled and got nothing — holds **27** such windows across 36,240 polls, **every one exactly 60.0 s**, one missed poll. Against a nearest real fault of **18,235 s**. Every value between produces identical output; 300 s sits **below** the geometric midpoint (487 s) on purpose, because the cost curve is asymmetric — firing early costs a toast on each of 104 deploys a month and firing late costs minutes of an outage that ran hours
  - **The entry's "multiplicative" is understated: the two populations are *disjoint*.** Both real outages were **arrivals** — tray started 18:34:46 and 14:44:57 against an already-dead daemon, first successful poll 17h 09m later — so `_was_connected` was never `True` and `connection_lost` never fired. Every window where the signal *did* fire was a 60 s deploy restart. So the transition signal fired **only on noise and never once on a fault**, which is sharper than either half being useless alone
  - **`escalation_polls` is deliberately not applied**, the one family here that opens loud. A new `critical` normally opens at `info` and escalates once still failing several polls later; waiting out 300 s **is** that test, already passed, so applying both would speak quietly at 300 s and loudly at 390 s about one fault. `critical` itself is derived — Session 39's rung, the only one the tray leaves on screen, chosen because the reported failure was "I never saw the toast", and this fault ran 17 hours with the room *occupied*
  - **The episode is closed by the connection, never by an alerts poll.** `_close_inactive` resets any fingerprint absent from a poll and this one is absent from every poll, so it is excluded there and `backend_reachable()` owns it — letting an alerts payload close it would make a question about reachability answerable by the surface that cannot be read while it is open. `last_notified_at` deliberately survives the close, so a backend failing the grace twice inside the flap cooldown is one story
  - **The retired check answered `match` against the fixed code, and that is worth carrying.** Its Face 1 predicate asked whether the emit sits inside an `if` reading `_was_connected` — **`True` before and after**, since the fix keeps the guard and corrects its polarity; the defect was the guard's *reachability*, never its existence. Its "initialised `False`" predicate walked the whole module for an `Assign` of `False` and at HEAD matched **two** (line 91, the initialiser, and line 411, inside `_on_disconnected`), so it was right for the wrong reason; after the fix it went on matching the one inside the method. And its Face 2 predicate was aimed at `on_connection_lost` by name while the speaking legitimately moved to a new method. A shape check about a defect whose essence is a *value*
  - **Check**: retired with the entry, and the detector re-homed as `tests/test_tray/test_backend_unreachable.py` (`FROZEN_TABLES`' rule) — **stronger than the check**, because it drives the real `ApiWorker`, the real policy and the real wiring rather than the AST, and what it re-homes is the *corrected* predicate: the **initialiser**, which is what the retired one could not name
  - **Driven live, and Face 1 landed at 0.0 s.** The real `ApiClient`, `TrayIcon`, `NotificationPolicy` at the shipped 300 s and the real `DbusNotifier` on the real session bus, pointed at a dead `127.0.0.1:8599` — an *arrival*, which is the population. `connection_lost` emitted on the first failed poll, silence held through 29.6 s / 59.6 s / 89.6 s (the measured deploy shape), and one non-transient `critical` arrived after the grace
  - **17 mutations were driven against the 34 tests and each lands red on the right one — one of them wrong on the first attempt**, which is the part worth carrying: `test_the_shipped_file_reaches_the_policy` asserted `load_tray_config(config.yaml) == the value in config.yaml`, and the shipped 300 **is** the model default, so it was green whether or not the loader read the file at all. Removing the key from `config.py`'s parse loop — `SNAG-CFG-001`'s exact shape — survived it. It drives a mutated copy carrying a witness value now, and the "the file and the model agree today" claim is a second test rather than the same one
  - **The cost, filed rather than implied**: the policy state is in memory, so a tray restart re-opens the episode and re-speaks after a fresh grace (`_reminder`'s rule 1) — closer to desirable than not here, since a tray restarting into a dead daemon should say so. And recovery is **not** announced, matching the daemon's rule for `alert.resolved`: the icon goes green within one poll and a second toast per restart is the noise this derivation exists to avoid
  - **Symptom**: across the 2026-08-22 outage the `sysadmin.service failed` row stood open **37.73 hours**, and **22.2** of those had a live graphical session with `sysadmin-tray.service` running, polling 8500, receiving nothing and displaying `IconState.DISCONNECTED`. Two sessions, 5h 04m and 17h 09m. The user was at the machine and was never interrupted
  - **Face 1 — a transition signal cannot fire for a state that was true at startup.** `client.py:407` `_on_disconnected` emits `connection_lost` only `if self._was_connected`, and `_was_connected` initialises to `False` (`client.py:91`). A tray that starts against a backend that is *already* dead has never connected, so it never emits — and starting after the daemon died is precisely the population, because `graphical-session.target` is reached long after a boot-time failure
  - **Face 2 — the handler recolours an icon and tells nobody.** `tray_icon.py:151` `on_connection_lost` sets `_backend_reachable = False` and calls `_recompute_state()`. There is no path from it to `notifications.py` at all, so even when the signal *does* fire — a daemon dying while the tray watches — the response is a colour change in a corner of the screen
  - **They are multiplicative, not independent**, which is `SNAG-AGENT-008`'s shape in the tray: fixing the emit reaches a handler that says nothing, and fixing the handler reaches a signal that never fires for the case that matters. A fix for one half is not half the benefit, it is none
  - **The tray is the natural owner and this is wider than the replay that prompted it.** `SNAG-SYSD-005` now speaks at login for a failure that reached `OnFailure=`. This family covers *any* backend the tray cannot reach — a daemon killed by hand and not restarted, a port conflict, a hung process that never enters `failed` — none of which writes an alert row, and all of which the tray can see within one 30-second poll
  - **The real work is the noise question, not the wiring**, which is why this is filed rather than folded into the replay. The journal's 30 days hold **122 daemon starts**; this repository restarts to verify and then commits, so a naive "notify on disconnected" fires on every deploy. It needs a grace period — the obvious derivation is a small multiple of the status poll interval — and it belongs beside the policy `notifications.py` already owns (dedup, flap cooldown, `reminder_hours`), never as a second speaker beside it
  - **The trap a fix must not walk into**: while the daemon is *alive*, `monitor/desktop.py` and the tray already share one notification policy with a grace window between them. A "backend unreachable" toast is safe only because the daemon being unreachable is exactly the case where no other speaker exists — so it must be scoped to unreachability and never widened into a general health notification, which would re-import the second-owner defect this repository has now found at seven scales
  - **What it cannot do, and what keeps `SNAG-SYSD-005`'s fix load-bearing**: with the backend dead the tray cannot read the alert row, so it cannot name `_schema_diagnosis`'s `CAUSE:` line or the one command that fixes it. It can say *that* monitoring is down; only the replay can say *why*. The two are complementary rather than alternatives
  - **Check (retired 2026-08-29 with the entry)**: `sysadmin-check-snags` → `tray_silent_on_arrival`. A conjunction over both faces, because either alone leaves the tray mute in the case that matters: `match` means both still hold. Driven at three mutations — emitting unconditionally, giving the handler a notifier, and renaming the method away — which give `mismatch`, `mismatch` and `unknown` respectively, the last because a shape that no longer exists needs re-reading and is not the same as being fixed
  - **Found**: 2026-08-29 by Session 126, while measuring whether `SNAG-SYSD-005`'s login replay was worth building. It was found by reconstructing the outage minute by minute rather than by reading the tray, and the entry it came from had recorded the room as empty

- [P3] SNAG-SYSD-005: **a failure at boot is now refused honestly instead of hanging, and still reaches nobody — the wait needs an owner that survives a login** (2026-08-29, opened by Session 125, **FIXED 2026-08-29** by Session 126)
  - **Fixed** by `sysadmin-replay-failures.service`, a **user** unit wanted by `graphical-session.target`, running `sysadmin.core.failure_replay` — the third half of the lifecycle `unit_failure.py` owns: the handler writes the row while the application is dead, the lifespan closes it when the application returns, and this speaks the gap between them to the first human who arrives
  - **The entry priced its own benefit at the gap and the table prices it at the row.** It argued from the wait to the next `class=user` session — 24 min at best, 6.1 h at worst. But only **2** alert rows exist for all **5** firings, three having hit `record_unit_failure`'s dedup branch, and the one row that was not fixed at once stood open **37.73 hours** (`1 day 13:43:31`). The login gap is 24 minutes of that, so the replay recovers **37.3 hours of silence** rather than 24 minutes of lateness. Reading the entry gives the population; querying `alerts` gives the cost
  - **And the room was not empty for most of it**, which reranks the entry's own framing. `start-limit-hit` 18:11:15 → login 18:34:41 → tray started 18:34:46, polled 8500, got nothing, went to `IconState.DISCONNECTED` and stayed there. **22.2 of the 37.7 hours** carried a live graphical session with the tray running and silent; only 15.5 were an empty room. "Four of five fired into an empty room" is a true statement about *firings* and a misleading one about *silence*. The replay still works — it fires at 18:34:46, the start of the occupied window — but the reason is that nothing here interrupts about a dead daemon, occupied or not. `SNAG-TRAY-009` is the other half
  - **The deferred flag was not needed, and the entry's own premise is why.** It asked for `--unannounced` at the announcer first and refused to add a flag with no reader — correct for a *history* predicate. The replay needs a *state* one: `resolve_unit_failures` has exactly one production caller (the lifespan, `main.py:244`), `% failed` is outside `RESOLVABLE_TITLE_PATTERNS`, and retention purges resolved rows only, so nothing else in this application can close one of these rows. An unresolved `systemd_onfailure` row therefore *means* "this unit has not come back". The second-speaker trap the entry names dissolves with it: a state predicate cannot speak about a fault that is over, and re-speaking at a **new** login about a daemon that is still dead is `reminder_hours`' own argument, bounded by 12 logins in 30 days
  - **Waiting is permitted here and was refused in the announcer, and only the number changed.** `SNAG-SYSD-004` rejected it at 60.08 s against a 24-minute gap. At login the precondition arrives in seconds — `plasma-plasmashell.service` active 14:44:55, target reached 14:44:57, plasmashell still initialising 14:44:58 on 2026-08-23 — so a unit started by the target genuinely finds the name unclaimed, briefly. `WAIT_BUDGET_SECONDS` is **derived**: notify-send's own measured 60.08 s bound, so the replay spends exactly the patience one blocked call would have spent anyway, on a mechanism that can be observed and that starts no `plasma_waitforname`. A test pins it below that bound
  - **The read comes before the wait**, which is the ordering the module exists to get right: a clean login costs one query and **0.34 s**, never touching D-Bus. Waiting first would charge every login 60 s for the benefit of about one a month
  - **The question is asked by the script that already owns it.** `notification-server-present.sh` is the one statement of "can this box speak", three verdicts and three exit statuses; re-implementing `NameHasOwner` here would be a second statement of a fact one module owns. An AST test refuses a `busctl` call in this module's code
  - **Every way of not-knowing keeps waiting rather than becoming a "no"** — a missing guard, an unreadable one, an unrecognised exit status. A bus that is not up *yet* answers `2`, and login is exactly when it comes up, so giving up on the first `2` would fail the case this exists for
  - **Driven live, both ways.** Clean box: exit 0 in 0.34 s, no D-Bus call. With a real standing row inserted and removed: the installed unit found it, announced it and exited 0, with the row's own `CAUSE:` line carried verbatim to the screen and `Failed 38 hours ago` added. Against a private `dbus-daemon`: a server claiming the name at t+2 s is caught at **3.02 s** and receives the notification intact — `urgency=2`, `expire_timeout=0`. The `alerts` table is back to **0** unresolved either side
  - **The cost, filed rather than implied**: the message says *what* died and not *why the session only hears about it now*, and a user who logs in twice during one outage hears it twice. Both are deliberate — the second is `reminder_hours`' argument and the first is the row's, since `alert.message` is `_schema_diagnosis`'s to author
  - **Check**: none, and deliberately — the drive replaces it, `SNAG-SYSD-004`'s precedent. `tests/test_failure_replay_live.py` starts a real bus with nothing on it, shows the wait bounded there, shows a name claimed mid-wait being caught, and shows what arrives being what was sent. The detector outlives the finding
  - **Symptom**: `SNAG-SYSD-004` removed the hang and did not remove the silence. At a boot-time failure the announcer asks whether anything is listening, is told no in **15 ms**, writes the reason to the journal and exits 1. The journal line and the `alerts` row are both written, so nothing is *lost*; what is lost is the **interruption**, which is the entire thing Session 39 built this handler for
  - **The population is the common case, not the edge case.** Four of the five firings this handler has ever had came at a boot with nobody logged in, and the next login was **24 min** away at best and **6.1 h** at worst. `SNAG-DB-005` is the shape — an unapplied migration refusing the lifespan — and it fires at exactly the moment the room is empty by definition
  - **Why the announcer cannot hold the wait, measured rather than assumed.** A pending call *is* delivered if a server appears (driven: a server claiming the name at t+4 s got the notification intact, `rc=0`), but notify-send's own bound is **60.08 s** against a nearest real gap of 24 minutes. Covering the observed population means waiting **hours**, and the announcer is a `Type=oneshot` with `TimeoutStartSec=30` holding a systemd job open. Lengthening it does not scale to the gap and re-admits the hang on the way
  - **Why nothing that already exists can hold it either, which is the interesting part.** The tray polls `GET /api/sysadmin/alerts` and would be the natural speaker — but the premise of this entire family is that **the daemon serving that route is dead**. `monitor/desktop.py`, its `desktop_notifications` store (migration 018) and `sweep_reminders` are all *inside* the daemon, and `SNAG-TRAY-008` gave that store persistence across a daemon restart precisely so a fault would not be forgotten. None of it is reachable when the process itself is what failed. The one component able to speak is the one that has died
  - **Shape of a fix, named rather than left to be invented**: a **user** unit wanted by `graphical-session.target`, which at login reads unresolved `agent='sysadmin'` rows carrying `details['source'] = 'systemd_onfailure'` and announces any that were never spoken. A user unit is what makes the wait cheap — systemd holds it, not a blocked process — and login is the only moment at which the missing precondition becomes true
  - **It needs one thing the announcer does not yet record, and that is deliberately deferred to the entry that will consume it.** The `alerts` row is written *before* the guard runs, so it cannot distinguish a failure that was announced from one that was refused; the distinction currently exists only as the journal's `not notifying:` line. Adding an `--unannounced` flag now would be a flag nothing passes, which is `SNAG-CFG-001` at the size of a flag, so it lands with its reader or not at all
  - **The trap the fix must not walk into**: this would be a **second speaker** for one fault, which is the defect this repository has now found at six scales. It is safe only if scoped to rows the announcer *refused* — mutual exclusion by construction, `failures.py`/`stalls.py`'s rule — and never to open rows generally, which is `SNAG-TRAY-007`'s adoption sweep and its own filed cost
  - **No check, and the reason is the one `SNAG-SYSD-004` gave on opening**: writing one to this registry's standard is a sitting's own work, discharged as such twice now. **This opening therefore breaks the property Session 124 established** — that every open entry names a check — and it is said here rather than left for a later sitting to discover. The shape is a population count: correlate `sysadmin-failed` firings in the journal against `systemd-logind` `class=user` sessions, and report the fraction that fired into an empty room. Refuted when a component exists that speaks for them
  - **Found**: 2026-08-29 by Session 125, as the named residue of `SNAG-SYSD-004` rather than as a new discovery

- [P2] SNAG-SYSD-004: **lingering makes the announcer's "is anyone there" guard pass with nobody there, so it hangs at boot and is killed** (2026-08-29, opened by Session 124, **fixed 2026-08-29** by Session 125)
  - **Fixed** by asking a different question. `scripts/notification-server-present.sh` tests whether anything **owns** `org.freedesktop.Notifications` rather than whether the bus socket exists, and `notify-unit-failed.sh` asks it before it speaks. Live, both ways, against the real announcer: the served bus gives **exit 0 in 31 ms** with the toast on screen, the unserved bus **exit 1 in 15 ms** naming the reason — where that second case previously ran to the 30-second kill
  - **The choice was fail fast, and waiting was measured before it was refused rather than dismissed.** The handoff put it as an open question and it is not a matter of taste: a *pending* call really is delivered. Driven against a private bus, a notification server claiming the name at **t+4 s** received the notification intact — right summary, right body, `urgency=2`, `expire_timeout=0` — and notify-send returned **0**. So `plasma_waitforname` does what it is for. What kills the option is the size of the window against the size of the gap
  - **The window is 60.08 s and the nearest real gap is 24 minutes.** Measured with no bound imposed: notify-send blocks for **60.08 s** on an unserved bus and then fails with `StartServiceByName … Timeout was reached`. Against the four killed firings, the next `class=user` login was **24 min 20 s**, **23 min 49 s**, **6.11 h** and **6.10 h** away. **Nought of four** could ever have been delivered, and the nearest miss is **24×** the window. The control is the fifth firing, 2026-08-11, the only one that completed — a human was already logged in
  - **So the shipped behaviour was never "wait"; it was "hang, then be killed".** Three bounds, and the smallest is systemd's: `TimeoutStartSec=30` < notify-send's own **60 s** < the bus's `service_start_timeout` of **120 s**. The call therefore *cannot* resolve on an unserved bus at any point — it is always killed first. The entry's framing of a wait that might one day pay off had no reachable state in it
  - **The mechanism is one level deeper than the entry says, and it is what makes the wrong question expensive rather than merely wrong.** `/usr/share/dbus-1/services/org.kde.plasma.Notifications.service` declares `Exec=/usr/bin/plasma_waitforname org.freedesktop.Notifications`. A call to an unowned name is not refused — the bus **starts a program whose whole job is to block until the name appears**. That is why nothing errored, and why a probe on a working desktop returns instantly
  - **The new question is answered by the bus daemon itself, so it cannot be caught in the same trap.** `NameHasOwner` is addressed to `org.freedesktop.DBus`, never to the notification name. Measured under `env -i` with no `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` — what a system unit with `User=` actually gets — at **3.1 ms** served, **3.8 ms** unserved, **1 ms** for a missing socket. And directly: **five guard calls started zero `plasma_waitforname`; one notify-send started one**, which is the whole distinction stated as a count rather than as a claim about D-Bus
  - **Three verdicts, `check-migrations.sh`'s three**, since the script already consumes that trichotomy one function up: `0` a server is listening, `1` a bus with nothing on it, `2` no bus or the question could not be answered. The announcer collapses 1 and 2 into "could not speak" and reports them apart, because a box that is merely pre-login and a box whose bus is broken need different next steps — `ports_checked`'s rule at the size of an exit status. An answer in an unrecognised shape is `2` and never a "no", so a change in `busctl`'s output format cannot silence the handler by being mistaken for silence
  - **The guard bounds its own question**, at 5 s against a measured 3.8 ms. A wedged bus daemon would leave even `NameHasOwner` unanswered, and a guard against hanging that can itself hang is not a guard
  - **`TimeoutStartSec` is deliberately unchanged at 30**, which is the one thing that looks like an oversight and is not. Once the activation path is refused, the only remaining call is `Notify` against a server that exists, whose own GDBus bound is 25 s — inside the budget. Raising the unit's timeout to accommodate the 60 s activation would be re-admitting the wait this entry exists to remove
  - **It needed no deploy.** The installed unit's `ExecStart` names the repository path directly, so the fix was live the moment it was written — a fourth deploy path beside the three this repository already documents, and the only one where writing the file *is* the deployment
  - **Clearing the stale state did need privilege, and how that was got wrong is the more useful half.** `systemctl reset-failed sysadmin-failed.service` returned **exit 0**, which was read here as "unprivileged, like `systemctl status` before it" and written up as a third correction to this repository's `sudo` claims. It was not: **polkit put an authentication dialog on the owner's screen and they authorised it** — reported by the owner mid-sitting, because nothing in this session could see it. A polkit prompt is mediated by an agent inside the user's graphical session, so the calling process observes only the outcome; **exit 0 is evidence about the result and never about the privilege**. Worse, the retry that was supposed to check this *also* returned 0, because the granted authorisation is retained
  - **`pkcheck` is the instrument that answers the question the exit status cannot**, and it should have been the first thing run: `pkcheck --action-id org.freedesktop.systemd1.manage-units` reports **`auth_admin_keep`** — administrator authentication required, *and retained after the challenge*, which is precisely why the second observation agreed with the first and confirmed nothing. That is `a-differential-probe-measures-its-own-noise` in a new dress: two agreeing observations of a latched state are one observation
  - **The scope of the correction is bounded by measurement rather than by assertion.** The reads this script performs do not go through polkit at all — driven under `env -i` with no session to prompt from, `journalctl -u sysadmin.service` exits **0** and `systemctl --no-pager status` exits **1** (the inactive-unit status, not a refusal), so the Session 70 finding that the toast should not say `sudo systemctl status` still stands. What is now known to be wrong is only the claim about `reset-failed`, which is a *state change* and gated as one. An earlier 127 from the same probe was `systemctl` failing to fork a pager under `env -i`, not a permission result — noted because it would read as a refusal to anyone re-running this
  - **What the existing test was doing is the part worth carrying.** `test_the_handler_is_a_oneshot_that_cannot_hang` has asserted `0 < TimeoutStartSec <= 60` since Session 39 and was green through all four killed firings — because 30 seconds is exactly what *fired*. A test that a hang is bounded is not a test that the hang cannot happen, and this file carried the first without the second for the life of the defect
  - **One falsification passed against deliberately broken code, and it is the shape this repository keeps finding.** Mutating the guard to refuse *everything* — the silent-forever failure, which has no symptom because what it suppresses is itself a notification nobody receives — left `test_it_admits_the_live_bus` **skipping** rather than failing, since that test asked the guard whether a live server existed in order to decide whether to run. A control a broken subject can switch off is not a control. The skip predicate now asks `busctl` directly; re-driven, the mutation turns it red. Eight mutations in total, each landing on the right test
  - **A cost that is filed rather than implied**: the activation the old behaviour triggered **outlives its caller**. `plasma_waitforname` is started by the bus, not inside the handler's cgroup, so systemd killing the announcer at 30 s left it running; on this box they clear only when a login finally claims the name. Empty population now, since nothing reaches the activation any more
  - **What is lost is what the entry said would be lost, and it now has an owner rather than being absorbed**: at a boot-time failure there is still no toast, because there is still nobody to show one to. `SNAG-SYSD-005` is the wait, given to a component that can survive a login instead of to a oneshot with a 30-second budget
  - **Check**: none, and deliberately — the drive replaces it. `tests/test_notify_guard_live.py` starts a real `dbus-daemon` with nothing owning the name, shows that notify-send blocks there and returns on the live bus (the discriminating witness), and shows the guard separates the two in milliseconds without starting the waiter. That is the shape this entry itself named, and it is `FROZEN_TABLES`' rule met in advance: the detector outlives the finding
  - **Symptom**: `sysadmin-failed.service` has sat in `failed` state since **2026-08-23 08:39:35** and every firing since 2026-08-22 was killed at `TimeoutStartSec=30` with `Result=timeout`, `status=15/TERM` — **4 of 4**. The firing on 2026-08-11 completed. The unit is the `OnFailure=` handler Session 39 built so that a daemon systemd has given up on is *heard*
  - **It reaches `notify-send` and blocks there**, which the journal settles rather than infers: every firing wrote its `systemd-cat` summary and its alert row (`recorded unit failure alert` / `already open … not duplicating`), and none wrote the script's `no session bus at /run/user/1000/bus` line — so it passed the guard, reached the final `exec`, and was killed 30 s later
  - **The guard tests for the bus and the thing that must exist is a *server on* it.** `notify-unit-failed.sh` checks `[[ -S /run/user/1000/bus ]]`, and its own comment states the intent: *"If nobody is logged in there is no session bus and notify-send fails."* That is untrue on this box, because **`Linger=yes` for gaddi**: `user@1000.service` starts at boot and creates the socket with no human session behind it. Nothing owns `org.freedesktop.Notifications`, so the call waits instead of failing, and the intended fast-fail becomes a hang
  - **Measured at the boot itself, not reasoned about.** Boot **08:37:10**; `user@1000.service` active **08:38:19** (session class `manager` — lingering, not a login); the sddm **greeter** session opens **08:38:22**; the announcer fires **08:38:28** and is killed **08:38:58**. Nobody had logged in at any point in that window. Re-run today on a live session the same flags return **exit 0 immediately** (`notify-send 0.8.8`, `--urgency=critical --expire-time=0`), so the flags are not the fault and a probe on a working desktop cannot reproduce it — which is why four firings looked like a puzzle rather than a mechanism
  - **It is silent in exactly the case it exists for.** A daemon that fails *at boot* is this box's commonest failure — `SNAG-DB-005` is that shape, an unapplied migration refusing the lifespan — and boot is precisely when no one has logged in. So the handler is available for a mid-session crash and unavailable for the one Session 39's `StartLimitBurst` work was written to catch
  - **`SNAG-DB-005`'s account of this handler is wrong and this is the artefact that says so.** That entry records the handler having *"fired correctly, with a persistent critical toast"* on 2026-08-23; both firings that day were killed by timeout with no logged-in user to show a toast to. A claim written from the mechanism rather than from counting its output, which is the failure `verify-ops-claims-live` names — and it stood for six days because the surviving journal line and alert row make the *record* look complete
  - **What is not lost, which is why this is P2 and not higher**: the journal line and the `alerts` row are both written before the hang, so the failure is recoverable from `GET /api/sysadmin/alerts` once the service is back. What is lost is the *interruption* — Session 39's whole argument is that a toast nobody is in the room to see is the miss, and this is worse by construction: no toast at all, in a room that is empty by definition. Ranked rather than argued
  - **A stale `failed` unit is the second cost.** `systemctl is-failed sysadmin-failed.service` has answered yes for six days about an announcement attempt, not about anything currently wrong, and the unit sweep reads unit state
  - **No check, and the shape of one is named rather than left to be invented.** Session 119 declined a check for `SNAG-CFG-003` because writing one to this registry's standard is a sitting's own work, and that judgement was discharged by a whole sitting — the same holds here, and more so, because the mechanism needs a bus with no notification server on it and a probe run on a working desktop returns instantly. The shape is a socket the drive owns: point `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS` at a bus this repository started with nothing owning `org.freedesktop.Notifications`, run the script's final call under a bound, and assert it does not return — with the witness being the same call against the live bus, which does
  - **Found**: 2026-08-29 by Session 124, reading `systemctl list-units 'sysadmin*'` while measuring something else

- [P1] SNAG-BRIEF-001: `Project Health` publishes every project ever scanned, including retired ones (2026-08-07, **fixed 2026-08-11**)
  - **Fixed** in Session 36. Both project sections now render from **one** query and one filter (`status == "active"`), so the payload cannot give two answers; ordering is by score **ascending** because a cap over a descending sort shows exactly the rows carrying no information; the cap is `_BOARD_LIMIT` (5), shared with `Pick This Up`, and `facts.projects.omitted` reports what was cut. Live on 2026-08-11: **26 rows → 5**, and the two sections agree. The choice of `active` over `ACTIVELY_SCORED` was deliberate — the board and `/api/projects/next` both draw the line there, and the whole defect was two surfaces disagreeing
  - **Symptom**: The section carries **26 rows**, among them `PersonalAssistant`, `PersonalAssistant-auto` and `PA-worktrees` — a project retired 2026-07-24 whose repos are deliberately archived — plus four near-duplicate casings of the same work (`Portfolio` / `portfolio` / `portfolionew`, `BSL-Translator` / `bsl-translator` / `bsl-translation-app`). The same briefing's `Pick This Up` section lists 5 projects and `GET /api/projects/board` returns 6. One payload, three different answers to "what is on this box"
  - **Cause**: [`briefing._build_project_health_section`](../../sysadmin/services/briefing.py) selects the latest snapshot per project with **no status filter and no cap** — it never reads `findings["status"]`. `_build_next_actions_section`, 40 lines below it in the same file, does exactly that filter (`if findings.get("status", "active") != "active": continue`), and so does the board router. The health section predates the `status` declarations added to `projects.yaml` and was never brought forward
  - **Impact**: Directly undoes the highest-leverage line in [monitorable-project.md](../guides/monitorable-project.md) — declaring `status: dormant` is supposed to suppress board rows and staleness nagging at once, and it does everywhere *except* the one section a human actually reads each morning. A consumer cannot repair this: nothing in the section's rows carries status, so Alfred can only render 26 rows or filter by a hardcoded name list
  - **Fix**: Apply the same `status == "active"` filter, and cap the table the way `Pick This Up` is capped. Sorting by score already puts the 100s (all uninformative) first, so a cap without a re-sort would show the least useful rows — worth ranking by score *ascending* or by score-drop-since-last-scan while the filter is in
  - **Note**: 26 → 6 is a large enough drop that the section may stop justifying a table; the honest replacement is probably "3 below threshold" plus the offenders

- [P1] SNAG-BRIEF-002: `Pick This Up` truncates a next action mid-word with no marker (2026-08-07, **fixed 2026-08-11**)
  - **Fixed** in Session 36 with `truncate_at_word` in `sysadmin/core/text.py` — word boundary, and always the marker `… (truncated)`, matching what Alfred's own `sanitise_text` appends so a cut made here and a cut made there read identically. The 180 is now a **documented** cap (`NEXT_ACTION_CHARS`, written up in [alfred-briefing-integration.md](../guides/alfred-briefing-integration.md)) rather than an anonymous slice. Deliberately **not** capped at source in `roadmap.next_action_from_handoff`: the board serves the full field on purpose and the guide now says so, so moving the cap upstream would shorten a surface that had no defect to fix. The marker is the assertion the test makes, not the length — 180 characters of prose is indistinguishable from prose that happened to be 180 characters
  - **Symptom**: venture-assistant's row reads `"Next up → Review the first scoring sample from the 2026-08-06 nightly run: check \`SELECT lens, count(*), … FROM idea_score GROUP BY 1\` for"` — it stops mid-sentence and nothing says so. The board serves the same field at its full 293 characters, so **the same `next_action` has two lengths on two endpoints and the shorter one looks complete**
  - **Cause**: [`briefing._build_next_actions_section`](../../sysadmin/services/briefing.py) does `"next": action[:180]` — a bare slice, no ellipsis, no marker, no word boundary. The board applies no cap at all
  - **Impact**: A truncated action reads as a finished instruction, which is worse than a long one. Alfred's own contract (ADR-0063 §2) requires a visible `… (truncated)` marker and its `sanitise_text` adds one — but only to `text` sections; `table` cells pass through per-key untouched, so this producer-side cut is what reaches the page. A consumer cannot detect it: 180 characters of prose is indistinguishable from prose that happened to be 180 characters
  - **Fix**: Truncate at a word boundary and append a marker, or (better) don't truncate here — cap once at the source in [`roadmap.next_action_from_handoff`](../../sysadmin/services/roadmap.py) so board and briefing agree. The 180 that stays should be a *documented* cap, since [alfred-briefing-integration.md](../guides/alfred-briefing-integration.md) currently promises "one action per project" without saying how long one is

- [P2] SNAG-ROADMAP-001: An unfilled handoff placeholder is published as a real next action (2026-08-07, **fixed by estate-manager 2026-08-25**, closed here 2026-08-26 by Session 87)
  - **Symptom**: Alfred's `Pick This Up` row reads `"No unchecked task found — set one before the next session."` with `source: handoff` — i.e. a template's apology for having no next action is rendered as the next action, at the highest confidence level the honesty field has
  - **Cause**: Two compounding faults in [`roadmap.py`](../../sysadmin/services/roadmap.py). (1) `next_action_from_handoff`'s **first branch — the "next" heading path — never calls `is_placeholder` at all**; the detector is only wired into `first_unchecked_task` (lines 162, 166), which is the *fallback*. The deliberately-signalled path is the unguarded one. (2) Even adding the call there would not catch this line, because `_first_meaningful` strips leading/trailing underscores (`re.sub(r"^_+|_+$", "", line)`) **before** returning, and `_PLACEHOLDER_RES[0]` is `^_.*_$` — the strip removes precisely the marks the rule matches on. Verified: `is_placeholder("_No unchecked task found…_")` → `True`; `is_placeholder(_first_meaningful([same]))` → `False`
  - **Impact**: Narrower than it looks — `~/.claude/hooks/generate-handoff.sh` emits this italic line, so it will recur on every repo whose session ended without an unchecked task, and always with `source: handoff`. That is the value the guide tells consumers to "show plainly — this is the real thing", which is exactly backwards here. The right outcome is the row being **omitted**, which the builder already does for projects with no action
  - **Fix**: Run `is_placeholder` on the raw line before `_first_meaningful` normalises it (or have `_first_meaningful` return both raw and cleaned), and call it on the "next" heading path. Worth adding the generated line's own wording to `_PLACEHOLDER_RES` as a belt-and-braces rule, since it is emitted by a hook this box controls
  - **Re-measured 2026-08-25 (Session 82) by driving the producer, and cause (1) above is now wrong while cause (2) is the whole of it.** The code moved to estate-manager on 2026-08-13 (ADR-0005) and the "next" heading path **does** reach the detector there — `next_action_from_handoff` calls `_first_meaningful`, which calls `is_placeholder`. It still cannot fire: `_first_meaningful` **strips the italic markers before testing**, and the detector keys on them. Driven against the exact line `~/.claude/hooks/generate-handoff.sh` emits: `is_placeholder(raw)` is **True**, `is_placeholder(naked)` is **False**, and `next_action_from_handoff` returns the apology. So the guard runs on every candidate and is structurally unable to reject one — worse than the absent guard the entry describes, because a test written against the normalised string is green for ever. **Delegated**: the module is estate-manager's, filed to their inbox rather than fixed here, and this entry stays open until they close it
  - **Check (`handoff_apology_published`, removed 2026-08-26 with the entry)**: `sysadmin-check-snags` — refuted when `next_action_from_handoff` stops returning the hook's line. **The first check whose instrument is another repository's code path rather than its document**, and the question that forced — is a check that cannot run a check at all — is settled by rule 5 rather than by a new verdict: no checkout, no venv, a renamed symbol or a tree caught mid-edit are all `unknown` with the reason named. A test may `skip`, because a test asserting two readers agree has nothing to assert when one is absent; a check reports on a claim, and *nobody managed to test it* is a verdict this module already imports from `schema_guard`. It measures the **producer**, never the publication — `looks_like_no_action` exists in that module today, matches this exact wording and is wired into `/next`, so a check asking *does any guard reject this line* would report the entry refuted while `next_action_from_handoff` went on returning it, which is rule 1's trap in a new dress
  - **Re-measured 2026-08-25 (Session 86) by driving the producer three times, and the entry is refuted against estate-manager's working tree while holding against their last commit.** Their fix was **in flight during this sitting** — `roadmap.py` modified at 22:34, uncommitted, under their own `SNAG-ESTATE-056` — and it is precisely this entry's proposed remedy: `_first_meaningful` becomes `_meaningful_lines` yielding `(marked, cleaned)`, and `next_action_from_handoff` tests `is_placeholder(marked)`. Driven at their committed `roadmap.py` the check reports **match** and reproduces Session 82's reading exactly (`is_placeholder(raw)` True, producer returns the apology); driven at their working tree it reports **mismatch**, `next_action_from_handoff` returning `None`. **Left open deliberately**: rule 2 makes a refuted claim a candidate for closure and never a closure, the fix is not released, and the daemon on 8400 serves start-time code. `estate_module_state()` carries which of the two was measured, because a `mismatch` off a released fix and one off an edit in flight have opposite remedies — `ports_checked`'s rule, applied to somebody else's repository
  - **What the check caught in itself is the part worth carrying.** Its first draft called `roadmap._first_meaningful` to evidence the strip — a **private helper whose name is the thing their fix renames** — so driven at the real fix it reported `unknown` and would have gone on reporting it for ever, structurally unable to witness the closure it exists to notice. A check coupled to the implementation it measures is the shape of the bug it measures. The repair states the mechanism more sharply than this entry ever did: `is_placeholder` says placeholder and the producer publishes it anyway, which uses no private symbol and survives any rename
  - **Closed 2026-08-26 by Session 87, on the mechanical trigger the previous sitting left and on nothing else.** estate-manager committed the fix at **22:42:29** on 2026-08-25 as `a9828dc` — *"the placeholder guard is given a line it can judge, and the branch that had no guard at all gets one"* — closing their `SNAG-ESTATE-056` and naming this repository's cross-repo message `e0461fe9` in the commit body. Driven at their committed tree the check reports **refuted**: `is_placeholder` still says placeholder and `next_action_from_handoff` now returns `None`, so the row is omitted, which is the outcome the "Impact" bullet asks for by name. The entry has nothing left to ask for
  - **Committed is not deployed, and this is closed anyway — which is the judgement rather than an oversight.** `estate-manager-api.service` last entered active at **11:35:25** on 2026-08-25, **eleven hours before** the fix was committed, so the daemon on 8400 is still serving the defective `_first_meaningful` as this is written. Session 86 gave three reasons to hold the entry open and the daemon was the third; it is **discharged rather than met**. This repository does not own their deploy, and holding a *delegated* entry open on the owner's restart state is the second owner the estate rules exist to prevent — the refusal `check_estate_port_8500` and the removed check both make in writing, and the same one that kept this check measuring the producer rather than the publication. `estate_module_state()` now says `committed` where it said `released`, because the first wording read as a claim about what is running over there and it never was one
  - **The live board is clean and that is not why this closed.** No repository on the estate carries the apology line today — their own commit measured 26 projects, 11 handoffs, 9 with a "next" section and **0 emphasised**, and the same count holds here — so `Pick This Up` publishes nothing wrong at this moment. An empty population is not a closure, which is this document's rule twice over (`SNAG-LOG-013` today, `SNAG-LOG-010` before it). Recorded so a later reader does not mistake the coincidence for the evidence
  - **Their fix was two claims wider than this entry, and half its remedy was declined.** The report reproduced exactly and was still short: the "next" heading branch had **no guard at all** rather than a defeated one, so `_Task 1_`, `_TBD._` and `_Fill in the first task_` published as real next actions too — every one of which `is_placeholder` would have rejected even naked. And the belt-and-braces limb was refused with a reason: the hook's wording is not added to `_PLACEHOLDER_RES`, because their module separates an unfilled template from a correct handoff whose content is "no next action", and the wording already sits in `_NO_ACTION_PHRASES`. An entry fixed by neither limb of its own proposed remedy is `SNAG-ROADMAP-002`'s shape, one entry over and nine sittings earlier

- [P3] SNAG-SYSD-002: `kind: timer` recorded nothing, because the properties it reads were never requested (2026-08-08, fixed same day)
  - **Symptom**: Every `kind: timer` check returned `ok` with `last_run_recorded: false` and no `last_run`, `next_run` or `last_result`, for timers that had demonstrably fired — `alfred-evaluate.timer` last triggered 08:00 that morning. The check was not failing; it was recording nothing, and reporting success while doing so
  - **Cause**: [`_timer_facts`](../../sysadmin/monitor/agent.py) reads `LastTriggerUSec`, `NextElapseUSecRealtime` and `Result` out of whatever [`get_unit_status`](../../sysadmin/monitor/systemd.py) returns, and `get_unit_status` asks `systemctl show` for a **fixed property list** that contained none of them. The two modules agreed only by accident and had never agreed at all — the feature shipped inert in Phase 3
  - **Impact**: Narrow in effect, wide in lesson. Six timers were affected and none reported wrongly, they simply reported less than intended, so nothing looked broken. **The failure mode is the point**: a check that records nothing looks identical to a check that records nothing worth mentioning, which is the same shape as the dead `projects.yaml` paths this session exists to eliminate. It survived unit tests because those mock `get_unit_status` and supply the properties the assertions expect — the mock was the specification, and the real function had never been asked
  - **Fix**: Requested the three properties for every unit (systemctl omits inapplicable ones, so it costs nothing and avoids a second subprocess for the estate's six timers; `Result` is meaningful for services too, being how a oneshot reports its last outcome). Added a test asserting every property `_timer_facts` reads appears in `get_unit_status`'s request list — the coupling itself is now checked, rather than only the behaviour on a mock that satisfies it
  - **Found**: while installing `sysadmin-organiser.timer` and verifying it end to end rather than assuming, which is the step [monitorable-project.md](../guides/monitorable-project.md) requires and the only reason this surfaced

- [P2] SNAG-ROADMAP-002: `count_open_snags` miscounts a document that groups or cross-references its snags (2026-08-07, **FIXED by estate-manager 2026-08-25**, verified here the same day)
  - **Symptom**: Two independent miscounts, both found while filing the twelve entries below. (1) A `###` sub-heading *inside* `## Open Issues` hid every snag under it — the count read 4 when 16 were open. (2) Once that was fixed the count read **21 for 16 snags**, because five nested `- **Cause**:` bullets happened to mention another snag's id
  - **Cause**: [`_sections`](../../sysadmin/services/roadmap.py#L79) splits on **any** heading level and returns a flat list, so a `###` under a `##` ends the parent section rather than nesting inside it; and `_SNAG_LINE_RE` is `^\s*[-*]\s+.*\bSNAG-[A-Z]+-\d+`, whose leading `\s*` makes an indented detail bullet indistinguishable from a top-level entry
  - **Impact**: `open_snags` reaches `GET /api/projects/board`, the briefing and `claude-preflight.sh`. Both directions are wrong in the dangerous way — grouping *hides* open bugs, cross-referencing *inflates* them — and neither is visible to the author, who sees a correct-looking document. This file is currently written around the parser rather than the parser matching the format
  - **Fix**: Nest sections by heading depth (a `###` under an "open" `##` inherits it), and require the id on an unindented bullet — or count entries by the `[P0]`/`[P1]`/`[P2]` marker, which only ever appears on a real entry line. Add a fixture covering both shapes: a grouped open section, and an entry whose sub-bullets name other snags
  - **Note**: the same `_sections` flattening governs `next_action_from_handoff` and `first_unchecked_task`, so a handoff using sub-headings under `## Next` is exposed to the first half of this
  - **Fixed by the owner on 2026-08-25 at 09:12:22**, an hour into Session 78, by estate-manager's `SNAG-ESTATE-048` — *"an entry records its own closure, and both of the remedy's limbs were wrong"*. The fix is not either limb this entry proposed: rather than counting `[P…]` markers or nesting by heading depth alone, `read_snags` now reads an entry's **own closure marker**, so a fixed entry left in place under "Open" is finally read as closed — which is the convention this document has followed for eleven sittings and the parser could not see
  - **Verified here rather than taken on trust**, by driving `read_snags` over this file: every known-fixed entry now reports `is_open=False` (`SNAG-DB-001`, `SNAG-DB-005`, `SNAG-DOCS-002`, `SNAG-LOG-009`) and every known-open one reports `True` (`SNAG-TRAY-008`, `SNAG-UNITS-003`, `SNAG-ESTATE-012`). The open count for this file drops **59 → 27** on an unchanged document
  - **So the nine-sitting report that was owed to estate-manager is moot**, and what replaced it is smaller: the owner fixed it before it was reported. The remaining cross-repo ask is the Session 33 question alone
  - **What this entry got wrong, recorded because it is the pattern**: it proposed two fixes and both were *parser-shape* fixes — count the marker, nest the headings. Neither would have helped, because the miscount was never really about shape: it was that **a closure this document expresses in prose had no machine-readable form at all**, and the parser was being asked to infer one. The owner's fix added the form. An entry that names its remedy in the first sitting and is fixed nine sittings later by a third option is `SNAG-DB-005`'s shape, one document over

- [P2] SNAG-CFG-001: `notifications.desktop.min_severity` was read by nothing, and looked exactly like the knob that decides whether an alert speaks (2026-08-11, **fixed same day**)
  - **Symptom**: `config.yaml` carries `notifications.desktop: {enabled: true, min_severity: warning}` and `sysadmin/core/config.py` validates it through `DesktopNotificationsConfig`. **No module reads `config.notifications.desktop`** — verified by grep across `sysadmin/`, which finds the section's siblings (`.dnd` in `monitor/dnd.py`, `.pa_notify` and `.tray.mute_services` in `monitor/notifier.py` and `monitor/reliability_history.py`) and never `.desktop`. The setting that actually gates a desktop toast is **`tray.notify_min_severity`** in the separate top-level `tray:` section, parsed by `sysadmin_tray/config.py`, which the tray reads out of the same file
  - **Cause**: The backend used to own desktop notification and handed the job to the tray in Phase 3 (popup retired 2026-07-24). The producer moved; its configuration did not, and the tray brought its own key rather than adopting the existing one. Both currently read `warning`, so the two have never disagreed and nothing has ever surfaced the redundancy
  - **Impact**: Latent, and of the shape this repository keeps finding — a control that is *requested* but not *enforced*. Lowering `notifications.desktop.min_severity` to `info` changes nothing, and raising it to `critical` silences nothing; a reader tuning notification loudness edits the plausible knob and concludes the setting does not work. Found on 2026-08-11 while writing Session 31's idle nudges, whose whole ladder is designed around *which* severity is audible — three comments were written naming the wrong knob before the grep was run
  - **The defect was larger than the key.** `Notifier.send_notification` — the only method that had ever consulted this section — has **no production caller**: grep finds it in `tests/test_notifier.py` and nowhere else. `Notifier` is instantiated in `main.py`, started in the lifespan and attached to `app.state`, and nothing ever asks it to send an alert. `BaseAgent.raise_alert`'s own docstring says "the notifier service should be called separately" and `monitor/agent.py` says criticals are "to be picked up by notifier"; neither ever happened. The dead key was one symptom of a notification path that had been dead since it was written
  - **Fixed 2026-08-11** by giving the daemon a real notifier rather than deleting the config: `sysadmin/monitor/desktop.py` subscribes to `alert.raised` on the existing event bus and sends via `notify-send`.
